#3167. Breakfast News: What’s Happening with Bitcoin?

Down 50% from its peak, and Wall Street is still leaning in

By TMF Breakfast News – Nov 20, 2026 at 7:30AM EST

Should Bitcoin (BTC -3.67%), recommended by Team Hidden Gems, come up between the turkey and the pie next week, expect at least one relative to mention the recent slide—probably with some satisfaction. The price fell as low as $62,000 late Saturday, capping a sixth straight week of declines, and dipped below that level Thursday night.

Step back a little, and the picture gets harder to look at. Bitcoin hit a record of about $126,000 on Oct. 6, 2025, and it’s now worth less than half that, down more than a quarter for the year. Since 2016, only November 2022, the month the FTX exchange collapsed, got off to a worse start.What knocked Bitcoin down?

There wasn't a single shock behind this so much as a pile-up, and most of the pressure started outside the crypto world entirely.

The first domino was tech, with the decline beginning in late October, when worries about AI valuations and spending pulled tech stocks lower, and leadership turmoil at Nvidia (NVDA -3.12%) only made investors more nervous about risk. On top of that, hotter-than-expected inflation pushed the dollar index to its highest level since April 2025. St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem also said several rate hikes may be needed before next spring.

Crypto had a scare of its own when the $6.3 billion Binance hack in late October—the largest crypto heist on record—led investors to convert stablecoins back into traditional currency, according to Grayscale. Spot Bitcoin ETFs saw about $1.91 billion in net outflows over seven straight trading days through Nov. 13.

What turned a slide into a rout, though, was borrowed money. Once Bitcoin broke below its 200-day moving average near $73,000, forced selling of leveraged positions took over.Wall Street hasn’t walked away

In October, J.P. Morgan Asset Management, part of JPMorgan Chase (JPM -0.03%) and hardly a crypto fund, issued its first-ever 10-year return forecast for Bitcoin, calling for 10% a year, compounded, compared with 5.5% for gold. Strategists Sheena Shah and Dennis Cristallo wrote that "bitcoin is the anchor of the crypto ecosystem."

It isn't a moonshot call, either, and JPMorgan was upfront about the caveats, pegging Bitcoin's volatility at 55% and noting that its unstable correlations still keep it out of most portfolios. Still, the asset-management arm of the largest U.S. bank now considers Bitcoin worth modeling a full decade out.

Consider what a few big financial firms actually did over the past several months.

Charles Schwab (SCHW +0.53%) finished rolling out spot Bitcoin and Ethereum(ETH -5.50%) trading to its retail clients in October. "All clients can now access Schwab Crypto," CEO Rick Wurster said on Oct. 15, and the company is adding crypto transfers and three more coins by year-end. Its executives estimate that Schwab clients already hold about 20% of all U.S. spot crypto ETP assets, which gives a sense of how much demand was there to begin with.

Deutsche Bank (DB -0.67%) switched on Bitcoin and Ethereum custody for paying institutional clients on Nov. 11, right in the middle of the selloff. Paul Maley, its global head of trust and securities services, said the bank aims to build "a fully integrated, regulated ecosystem" for digital assets, and it plans to bring the service to Asia. Over at Morgan Stanley(MS -1.38%), the E*Trade platform launched crypto trading in July through crypto infrastructure firm Zerohash.What the biggest holder is doing

Strategy(MSTR -1.64%), the largest corporate owner of Bitcoin, is still buying, though at a much slower clip than it used to. It bought 485 BTC for $33.4 million between Nov. 9 and Nov. 15, at an average of $68,887 per coin, according to a regulatory filing on Monday, after picking up 1,036 BTC the week before. The treasury company now holds 850,849 BTC, about 4% of the 21 million that will ever exist.

So Strategy hasn’t capitulated, but it isn’t a backstop anyone should count on. Its average cost is $75,452 per coin, leaving its position roughly 15% underwater at about $64,000. Its buying also depends on raising money from investors, which gets harder when its own stock is down.

If you own some Bitcoin, your time horizon should be among your biggest considerations. Drops of 50% or more have been part of owning Bitcoin since its earliest days, so if you bought it to hold for five or 10 years, this drawdown is part of the deal you signed up for. Position size matters just as much, because the right amount is one you could hold through another leg down without needing to sell.

Finally, Fools know to steer clear of leverage with any speculative instrument. This month's worst damage landed on traders using borrowed money, while investors who owned their coins outright got a scary-looking statement rather than a margin call.

Motley Fool co-founder and CEO Tom Gardner made a similar point during his Quarterly Call last December, when this drawdown was still in its early weeks. He traced Bitcoin’s wild swings to the same kind of borrowed money that did the damage this month: when the price runs, “people start to lever up and think, this is the time when I’ll triple my money in four months. That margin gets exposed, and the asset is very volatile.” He drew a distinction worth holding onto: “Bitcoin is not a stable store of value. But I do believe it is a long-term store of value.”

Do you own any Bitcoin—and would you add more at these prices?

Discuss with friends and family, or become a member to hear what your fellow Fools are saying!

#3166. Your Best Suppliers Are Losing Bookings They Deserve To Win

Abhinav Sinha is Co-founder and CEO of ZentrumHub, a unified hotel API platform serving 90+ OTAs globally.

Earlier this year, my team audited the supplier routing of a midsize online travel agency. One wholesaler was confirming nearly every booking it received for hotels in Japan. It had deep direct contracts in the market, clean rates and almost no relocations. It sat fourth in the call sequence. Two of the suppliers ahead of it were failing roughly one booking in 10 on the same properties.

When we asked why the order looked the way it did, we got the most honest answer in hotel distribution: That’s the order the contracts were signed in.

For a decade, our industry has repeated the same line: Supplier loyalty is dead, wholesale inventory is a commodity and the only lever left is margin. I think the diagnosis is wrong. Loyalty isn’t dead. It’s invisible. Most distribution systems were never built to see which supplier is actually performing, so they have no way to reward it.

The commodity story collapses the moment you look at supplier behavior property by property. Wholesalers and aggregators invest in very specific strengths—direct contracts in a handful of markets, rate accuracy on particular chains, confirmation reliability, clean cancellation handling, content depth. These investments are real and expensive.

The channel they flow through isn’t a backwater, either. Expedia Group reported B2B gross bookings up 21% year over year in the second quarter of 2026, well ahead of its consumer business, marking its 20th consecutive quarter of double-digit B2B growth. The money moving through B2B hotel distribution keeps compounding. The sophistication with which buyers choose among suppliers for any single booking has barely moved.

At most booking platforms I’ve seen, supplier selection is static. A preference order is written when contracts are signed. It reflects commercial politics, integration chronology and whoever had the best rates during the pilot, and it’s rarely revisited.

From then on, every search walks the same waterfall. The supplier that’s genuinely best for this hotel, on these dates, for this traveler, often isn’t asked at all or is asked last after caches and timeouts have already shaped the answer.

Static routing also protects the wrong suppliers. When buyers can’t distinguish disciplined suppliers from leaky ones, the whole category gets judged together. The mid-year World Parity Monitor found at least one online travel agency undercutting the hotel’s own website in 75% of searches, and a recurring driver it identifies is wholesale rates resold with unauthorized discounts.

A supplier that keeps its rates clean ends up subsidizing the reputational damage caused by one that doesn’t because the routing layer can’t tell them apart. With performance invisible, the only lever anyone can pull is margin. The race to the bottom is a routing artifact.

The alternative is to route every request on observed performance for that property and market—availability accuracy, booking confirmation rate, cancellation and relocation rates, content quality and net price after failure costs rather than headline price. Each of these is measurable per supplier, per property, from data that platforms already generate.

When selection runs on that data, the supplier who invested in Japan wins Japan. The incentives flip. Investment becomes visible, visibility gets rewarded and suppliers start competing on reliability as well as price. In the migrations I’ve been close to, the first effect is usually not cheaper rooms but fewer failed bookings, which turns out to be worth more.

If you run distribution or supply at a travel platform, four moves matter most in my experience:

1. Measure per property, not per contract. Portfolio averages hide everything important.

2. Price in failure since a rate that’s 2% cheaper but fails far more often isn’t cheaper once you count rebooking costs and customer damage.

3. Treat routing as a living system, reviewed continuously rather than at contract renewal.

4. Share scorecards with your suppliers. The ones worth keeping will improve whatever they can finally see.

If you’re a supplier, ask every buyer one question: How do you decide which of us gets the booking? If the answer is contract order, then a configuration file is neutralizing the market strengths you spent years building.

I argued in July that this industry still prices shopping as if it were scarce. Allocation is the same story one layer down. My bet is that within two years, “how does your routing decide?” becomes a standard question in supplier negotiations ahead of another round of margin shaving. The suppliers that ask it, and the platforms that can answer it with data, will take share from everyone still routing by paperwork.

Loyalty was never dead. We just never gave it a scoreboard.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3165. Here's How Much $10,000 Invested in Altria Stock Could Pay in Dividends Over the Next Decade

It remains one of the S&P 500's premier dividend stocks.

By Stefon Walters – Nov 20, 2026 at 7:40AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Altria Group ›

Altria (MO +0.95%) is one of the market's marquee dividend stocks. In fact, many investors would tell you that Altria's dividend is the only reason to invest, even though the stock price surged more than 33% this year (as of Nov. 18). If Altria continues its impressive run, it'll be its best-performing year since its 2008 Philip Morris International (PM +0.79%) spinoff.

Altria's stock price run won't be the norm, but its dividend is as consistent as it gets, with the company being a Dividend King (a company with at least 50 consecutive years of dividend increases). It routinely offers one of the higher yields among S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.00%) companies and could be a good income play for patient investors. Here's how much it could pay out in dividends over the next decade with a $10,000 investment.

At Altria's price at the time of writing, $10,000 would buy about 130 shares, so we'll use that for our example. At Altria's annualized payout of $4.44 ($1.11 quarterly), those shares would pay $577.20. If we assume Altria increases its annual dividend by 5% each year (it has averaged more than 6% annually over the past decade), here's how much you could expect in annual cash payouts:

With a single $10,000 investment, you would've received close to $7,260 in cash payouts over a decade. The real magic, though, happens when you reinvest your dividends back into the stock, giving them a chance to earn dividends on them. In this scenario, had you reinvested dividends from year 1, your final investment value would easily be thousands more than $17,260 (original $10,000 investment plus $7,260 in cash payouts).

Altria is uniquely positioned as the country's largest tobacco producer, leading an industry at a crossroads. On one end, you have declining smoking rates in U.S. adults. According to the CDC, 42.4% of U.S. adults smoked in 1965. Now, that has dropped to 9.9%.

When you're the country's largest cigarette producer, that decline has a noticeable impact on business.

On the other end, tobacco users are very loyal and like what they like. Someone who smokes Marlboro Red or Black & Mild cigars (two of Altria's most popular brands) will typically buy them, even if prices go up. Of course, consumers have financial limits, but for the most part, this dynamic has given Altria the pricing power to offset some of its volume decline.

Altria hasn't done a great job establishing a smoke-free alternative to compete with popular brands like Philip Morris International's Zyn, but it isn't time to ring the alarm yet. Altria has enough time and more than enough resources to make something happen.

If you're looking for a good income investment, Altria is a buy. It knows its appeal is its dividend, so it's as shareholder-friendly as they come. I don't expect that to change in the foreseeable future.

#3164. Top Wall Street analysts recommend these 3 dividend stocks for higher returns

Dividend-paying stocks can enhance portfolio returns by providing a steady income stream to investors and the potential for capital appreciation. Several investors seek exposure to dividend stocks in a volatile backdrop to ensure stable income.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, backed by strong financials and cash flows to support consistent dividend payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Energy Transfer (ET) is a diversified midstream energy company with about 140,000 miles of pipelines and associated energy infrastructure in 44 states. The company made a quarterly cash distribution of 34 cents per common unit for the second quarter of 2026. At an annualized payment of $1.36 per common unit, ET offers a yield of 6.3%.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Jeremy Tonet reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer stock and slightly raised his price target to $25 from $24. The analyst noted that the company delivered a strong Q2 beat across all key metrics.

Tonet highlighted that Energy Transfer raised its 2026 adjusted EBITDA guidance to $18.8 billion-$19.1 billion from $18.2 billion-$18.6 billion, reflecting robust base results and cost optimization. The 5-star analyst added that the company "tightened" its capex to $5.6 billion–$5.9 billion and expects capex above $5 billion annually through 2029, given solid opportunities.

About the Hugh Brinson pipeline, Tonet noted Phase 1 (1.5 bcfd) reaching full capacity by September 1 and Phase II capacity (0.7 bcfd) going online by Q1 2027. The analyst also noted completion of a 14-mile Hugh Brinson lateral in Abilene, Texas, and final stages of talks for an additional 250 mmcfd of Oklahoma power plant demand.

"All in, we see ET well positioned to continue capitalizing on organic growth opportunities across the value chain," said Tonet.

Tonet ranks No. 922 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 9%. See Energy Transfer Financials on TipRanks.

This week's second dividend pick is independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR). The company declared a base dividend of $0.16 per share for the third quarter, payable on September 30, 2026. At an annualized dividend of $0.64 per share, PR offers a yield of about 2.7%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Permian Resources stock and increased his price target to $27 from $22, citing an improved outlook on oil production growth and operational efficiencies.

The 5-star analyst noted that while PR stock has outperformed its peers so far in 2026, he sees further upside. Mehta is confident about the company's operational improvements and ability to deliver incremental free cash flow per share through the strategic ground game program. He added that the program has completed about $1.05 billion in bolt-on deals this year (as of August 5).

"We see the continued success of PR's ground game as constructive toward future inventory replenishment and increasing working interest over time," said Mehta.

The analyst also emphasized Permian Resources' solid track record of generating growth in free cash flow (FCF) per share. Mehta expects the company's free cash flow per share to grow at a 20% CAGR (compound annual growth rate) from 2025–2028.

Mehta ranks No. 401 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.8%. See Permian Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Sempra Energy (SRE), a utility holding company. Earlier this month, the company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.6575 per share, payable on October 15. At an annualized dividend of $2.63 per share, SRE stock offers a yield of about 3.1%.

Recently, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith upgraded SRE stock to Buy from Hold but slightly lowered the price target to $97 from $101. The 5-star analyst noted that Sempra stock is trading at a price/earnings multiple that is 14% below its electric peers due to concerns about Texas transmission capital expenditure and the failure of California legislation.

Smith noted that the derating of weaker California peers PG&E (PCG) and Edison International (EIX) is weighing on the sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation but is becoming less relevant given their materially higher fire risk compared to Sempra Energy.

Furthermore, Smith expects Sempra Energy's Texas capex plan to stay in place despite the opposition to transmission and data centers, though delays to 765-kV projects are likely. While a full recovery for SRE stock will need a clearer outcome from the Texas legislative session, Smith believes that the stock may already be an early opportunity for patient investors.

"We are early on the upgrade but see limited downside at current levels as the market is pricing in little transmission upside," said Smith.

Smith ranks No. 945 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.2%. See Sempra ETF Exposure on TipRanks.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates rose enough today that you might be feeling some sticker shock.

If you're here looking for lower mortgage interest rates, today is definitely not your day.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.38% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 25 basis points higher than yesterday and 12 basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Keep in mind that mortgage rates are always on the move, and that if you're tracking rates day-to-day, you're going to see a lot of volatility. Zooming out and looking at the bigger picture — like a graph showing at least a month's worth of rate data — can help you see the overall trend.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

On Friday, the Bureau of Labor Statistics released the Producer Price Index for October, which tracks the change in prices that U.S. producers are able to get for their products. This is one way to measure inflation on the market end, rather than the consumer end.

The results were mixed. While prices didn’t rise quite as high as economists anticipated month-over-month (0.3% vs. 0.4%), year-over-year price growth did reach 6.5% — the largest increase in four years. This points to even higher costs for consumers coming in the near future.

Investors are convinced that the Federal Reserve will raise overnight borrowing rates again in December to pull inflation down. And higher overnight borrowing rates — called the federal funds rate, which is what banks pay to fund products like mortgages — usually lead to higher mortgage rates.

» MORE: How the Federal Reserve affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.88% or higher — but there aren’t many people for whom that’s true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3162. AI For Math Proofs: Organized Monkeys Or Superhuman Genius?

Ambuj Kumar, Simbian.ai co-founder and CEO, pioneered confidential computing, and builds AI agents to counter AI threats.

On October 6, 2026, OpenAI posted over 700 AI-written math manuscripts. About 300 come with proofs in Lean, a language a computer can check line by line. One claims a zero-free region for the Riemann zeta function, where the real part exceeds 7/8, a partial result related to the long-standing Riemann hypothesis. Months earlier, an unreleased Claude model raised the proven share of zeta zeros on the critical line from 41.6% to 67.2%, with a Lean proof.

This doesn’t mean that mathematicians are celebrating. In September, Terence Tao and 24 other Fields Medalists signed a declaration saying that “the goals of the AI companies and the goals of the mathematical community are severely misaligned.” In a lecture at Caltech on October 9, Tao went further: “Further blind optimization of problem-solving alone is now actively harmful to the long-term health of mathematics.”

So, are these machines geniuses? To judge that, start with how an LLM learns math.​

​Many labs train reasoning models using reinforcement learning, rewarding models when they produce correct answers. The approach works particularly well when those answers can be objectively verified. Whether an essay is good or a security alert is real is a judgment call, and a noisy reward teaches a noisy model.

Math offers a particularly useful advantage: When a proof is formalized in Lean, its checker can determine whether the argument follows from the stated assumptions, without requiring a human to review every step. ​It should therefore be no surprise that models trained this way can now produce solutions to problems that have stumped generations of mathematicians.​

However, it also creates a problem. When proofs are checked automatically, the model only has to be right once, and you can keep it trying for as long as you can pay for those GPUs. Give enough monkeys enough typewriters, and one will eventually type Hamlet. In practice it never happens, because probability is so low we would have to wait for trillions of years.

LLMs are stochastic systems that can generate mathematical arguments and Lean proof attempts. Many attempts will fail, but a model that can produce sufficiently promising candidates can be paired with an automated checker to identify formally valid results.​ How many attempts would it take to solve a famous problem? What if a lab can spend tens of millions of dollars on compute, while successful announcements strengthen perceptions of its AI capabilities and potentially improve its competitive position? Those economics justify a very large search.

Only the successful results receive individual attention. The thousand failed tries, the tokens burned and the size of the model running the search largely stay out of view.​​

​None of this is an accusation. Math has long been the clearest test of intelligence, and labs should test their models on it.

The trouble is what we use math to measure. We admire mathematicians like Terence Tao for their ability to develop insights and solve problems that might take others months or years to understand. When Ramanujan filled notebooks with identities that took others decades to prove, part of the wonder was how little he seemed to need.​

The labs only disclose some of the cost, but not enough. Anthropic says its zeta result took 31 million output tokens, after a first session tried 650 ideas without success. OpenAI says its model averaged three hours of ChatGPT Pro thinking per result. Neither lab has said how big its model is. OpenAI has not published its prompts or per-result compute, although an advisory group of mathematicians at the Institute for Advanced Study asked for both on September 29.​

A mathematician who proves a theorem after a month of drafts has run a 20-watt brain for that month. A hypothetical 10-trillion-parameter model that needs a hundred attempts could consume vastly more energy, depending on the hardware and compute involved. From outside, the two announcements look the same, and nothing tells us whether we are looking at a mind like Ramanujan’s or a well-organized room of monkeys.​

​I propose that every AI-generated proof ship with a disclosure card, like a security researcher publishes an advisory for every vulnerability they disclose.​

The security world has developed a useful model for this. CVE provides standardized identifiers for publicly disclosed vulnerabilities, while CVSS offers a common framework for assessing their severity. Coordinated disclosure processes also establish expectations for documenting findings, notifying affected parties and appropriately attributing researchers.​

A similar discipline can be followed here. arXiv could add a metadata field for AI-assisted proofs, journals like the Annals of Mathematics could require a card at submission and the Lean mathlib community could require disclosure in every pull request that credits an AI contributor.

Defining what counts as an attempt will be hard, though, and labs will not shy away from calling model size and per-result compute trade secrets. Also, the lab that discloses first will look worse than the one that stays silent, which is why the first standard has to come from a venue that can refuse to publish without the card.

Once cost sits next to every result, a small model’s first-try proof outranks a giant model’s hundredth try, and labs have a reason to build small models that find short proofs mathematicians can read and build on. Until a result comes with its card, and until these models work alongside mathematicians, I believe we should hold back the respect we reserve for people who solve problems the traditional way.​​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3161. This Is the Simplest ETF Portfolio for Investors Who Want Growth and Income

Investors can combine ETFs that cater to both objectives.

By Neil Patel – Nov 20, 2026 at 7:45AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Vanguard Morningstar Growth ETF ›

Investing in the stock market is interesting because not all participants share the same objectives. Some are fully focused on achieving the highest returns, which will push them to take on more risk. There are other investors, however, who want to own assets that generate steady income. This will lead them to discover stable dividend-paying stocks.

Maybe you sit right in the middle of the spectrum: Your goal is to have growth and income exposure. But you don't want to spend time picking individual stocks. Exchange-traded funds (ETFs) might be the answer. Here's what I believe is the simplest ETF portfolio to build for growth and income.

To find a solid growth ETF, you don't have to look very far. The Vanguard Morningstar Growth ETF (VUG -0.70%) is a good choice. It charges an expense ratio of 0.03%. I suggest using half of your available cash to buy shares in this investment vehicle.

This ETF tracks the performance of the Morningstar (MORN +2.04%) U.S. Large Cap Growth Index. It contains 135 different stocks in businesses that have increased revenue and profits at above-average rates in the past.

The performance is notable: In the last decade, the Vanguard Morningstar Growth ETF has generated a total return of 399% (as of Nov. 16). The S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.00%) had a total return of 308% by comparison. No one knows if the superior performance will continue, but you ignore historical trends at your own peril.

The technology sector represents 69% of this Vanguard ETF, with consumer discretionary (13% weighting) a distant second. This shouldn't exactly come as a surprise. Many of the top growth stocks operate in tech-related industries.

The risk is that markets are changing rapidly, challenging established competitive advantages. On the other hand, the advantage is that there is significant potential in the long run.

By owning this ETF, investors will instantly build exposure to the artificial intelligence (AI) revolution. Apple (AAPL +0.14%), Nvidia (NVDA -3.12%), and Microsoft (MSFT +0.84%), which combine for 37% of the Vanguard Morningstar Growth ETF, are the three largest positions. While the degrees vary, they are all positioned to benefit from the secular AI trend, an area of the market that is hard to avoid if you're craving growth.

For the income part of the equation, consider the Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF (SCHD +1.32%)for the other half of your cash position. This ETF, which carries an expense ratio of 0.06%, is an attractive option for those seeking income.

The Schwab ETF follows the Dow Jones U.S. Dividend 100 Index. Companies are screened based on different fundamental factors, a sign of their financial strength that can be valuable in times of economic stress. And they're required to have paid dividends in 10 straight years.

Over the last decade, the ETF's total return came in at 257%. While you give up some opportunity on the capital appreciation front, you capture a healthy 2.95% trailing-12-month dividend yield. That's almost three times more income than the S&P 500 index will get you. The ETF's payout has also doubled in the last decade.

If you want to explore options outside the public equity market that are more stable, consider a fixed-income ETF. The iShares Trust-iShares 0-3 Month Treasury Bond ETF (SGOV +0.03%), charging an expense ratio of 0.09%, is compelling. It owns short-term Treasury bills. The trailing-one-year dividend yield of 3.63% is much higher than what you'll receive from the Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF. However, there is no growth potential here.

Whether you choose one or both of the Schwab and iShares ETFs for income, pairing your choice with the Vanguard Morningstar Growth ETF creates a well-rounded portfolio. Two objectives will be satisfied simultaneously.

#3160. Nvidia and Micron Can't Make AI Chips Without This Growth Stock. Here's Why It Could Soar.

This company is well positioned to capitalize on the growing demand for AI chips across both logic and memory manufacturers.

By Adam Levy – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:00AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Applied Materials ›

Nvidia (NVDA -3.12%) and Micron Technology (MU -5.35%) both make chips that are essential to the continued AI build-out, but their chips couldn't be more different. Nvidia makes advanced logic chips and graphics processing units (GPUs) used for training and inference of large language models. Micron makes memory chips, and demand for its high-bandwidth chips has soared, as the ability to quickly access large volumes of data for training and inference has become a bottleneck in the continued development of AI.

While Micron prints its chips itself, Nvidia contracts chip manufacturing to Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing. However, there's one company neither could produce its chips without: Applied Materials (AMAT -5.05%). The company supplies manufacturers such as Micron and Nvidia's partner, TSMC, with a wide range of wafer fabrication equipment.

The company is growing quickly, but the market may still underappreciate the opportunity ahead for the business, and the stock could soar from here.

Applied Materials holds a broad portfolio of wafer fabrication equipment that's used by both logic chipmakers like Nvidia and memory chipmakers like Micron. As Micron and other memory chipmakers respond to the spike in demand for their chips, Applied's share of semiconductor system sales for DRAM and flash memory chipmaking equipment climbed to 42% of sales in the company's fiscal fourth quarter. That's despite heavy spending from advanced logic chipmakers like TSMC as well.

Overall, Applied Materials' revenue climbed 21% in 2026, but that growth has accelerated sharply every quarter. Revenue was up 53% year over year in Q4. What's more, management expects 2027 to come in strong as well. Its first-quarter outlook calls for $11.1 billion in revenue at the midpoint, up 22% from last year. But it expects the market for wafer fabrication equipment to grow 30% overall for the full year and to gain market share, implying revenue acceleration throughout the year.

Applied's broad portfolio of equipment positions it well to gain market share in the fast-growing industry. It's the only equipment maker that competes across all three market segments: etch, deposition, and process control. Its size gives it the advantage of investing more in research and development than its competitors, while working closely with manufacturers to build integrated solutions that save floor space. Those solutions increase switching costs for manufacturers, locking them into Applied's products and servicing.

In the longer run, management expects its gross margin to expand by five percentage points from 2025 to 2030, while operating margin expands by 10 percentage points during the same period. Combined with the robust outlook for revenue growth driven by expected spending on artificial intelligence, Applied should deliver very strong net income growth and free cash flow. Management is committed to returning excess free cash flow to shareholders over time, reducing its share count, and further boosting earnings per share.

Applied Materials stock currently trades for less than 20 times forward earnings estimates. That's despite expectations that earnings per share will grow more than 46% over the next year and another 28% the following year.

That low multiple is due to the cyclical nature of the business, and there's a significant risk that its equipment sales growth will taper as AI spending stabilizes and capacity constraints ease. That said, the company's services revenue acts as a ballast, providing a consistent stream of revenue, as its equipment must be serviced regularly to maintain precision.

That said, an earnings multiple of 20 is closer to its mid-cycle multiple, and the outlook for wafer fabrication equipment spending (as well as analysts' outlooks) indicates we're closer to the start of an up cycle in earnings. Importantly, the stock price could fall to about 12 times trailing earnings by the end of fiscal 2029 and still trade at roughly the same price as today. If it trades closer to the mid-cycle multiple of 20, that's upside of 63% from here. In my opinion, that's somewhat conservative considering the outlook for equipment demand.

#3159. Asked on Reddit: I Lost My Job; What Should I Do First?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Income loss is stressful; the first steps involve cutting costs and focusing on essentials.

A Redditor recently shared that they were struggling to decide which financial steps to take first after a job loss.

Should they withdraw money from retirement savings to have more cash on hand — but pay an early withdrawal penalty? Or is it better to rely on the severance payout to handle expenses?

Many users responded with good ideas: File for unemployment, try to avoid tapping into retirement savings, and cut expenses as much as possible.

For more ideas, I asked three financial experts how to manage money in the immediate aftermath of job loss.

While it’s tempting to make quick decisions about tapping into a retirement fund or selling a house, it’s better to wait on any big moves, says Parker Wine, retirement planning advisor at the financial firm Daniel A. White & Associates in Lewes, Delaware.

That’s because you need time to digest the situation and weigh the pros and cons of different options, he says. “People can get impulsive and panic, and it’s usually a bad thing to make decisions when you’re panicking,” he says.

Take your time, get second opinions and think through all of your options first, Wine suggests.

If you’re fortunate enough to receive a severance package, wait to sign it until you’ve reviewed it carefully and understand all of the details, says John Rafferty, partner and investment advisor representative at Solomon Financial in Carmel, Indiana.

For example, make sure you know about your health insurance options, such as whether you’ll receive insurance through COBRA or need to take out a new policy in the health insurance marketplace.

Filing for unemployment and checking into any other available workplace benefits — such as help with your new job search — is also an important part of understanding what support to expect in the weeks and months ahead, Rafferty adds.

Creating a bare-bones budget stripped of “extras” like restaurant meals or travel can help the severance payment last as long as possible, Rafferty says.

“Now that you don’t have income coming in, ask yourself, ‘How much can I spend and where?’”

You might be able to cut back on entertainment costs, coffee runs and clothing purchases, for example.

At the same time, it’s important to prioritize the essentials like mortgage payments, rent, utilities and food. Rafferty recommends calling your mortgage company, utilities provider and any other lenders to ask if they have a hardship program for those experiencing financial challenges.

Opting in could give you more time to pay your bills or temporarily lower payments.

It’s hard to stick to a budget that eliminates all entertainment, so Rafferty suggests finding enjoyable activities that come with a low price tag. Hiking, discovering a new park and participating in free community events are all ways to enjoy downtime on a budget.

“Having that release is mentally a big thing so you can stay prepared for the job search,” he says. “Otherwise, it’s easy to get down and feel negative, and we need things to help us feel positive.”

Similarly, connecting with friends and family during this time is crucial, says Laura Adams, accredited financial counselor and financial coach in Wilmington, North Carolina.

People often feel a sense of shame or embarrassment after job loss, and Adams encourages people to talk about their feelings and ask for help if needed. “Financial shame can cause us to make emotional rather than confident financial decisions,” she says.

Starting some kind of side hustle to bring in extra cash can also help bridge the gap between the job loss and your next job, Wine says. Substitute teaching, working at a local public golf course and driving for a rideshare service are all options he’s seen people try.

Doing something new also helps stave off boredom while unemployed, but he cautions that you’ll still owe taxes on any side income you bring in, so plan accordingly.

All three financial experts also emphasized that a job loss is easier to handle with an emergency savings fund you can turn to, and suggested building one up — ideally three to six months’ worth of expenses. But starting small helps, too — while employed, if possible.

The Redditor who posted the question mentioned that, like many Americans, they lacked emergency savings. This added urgency to the question of what to prioritize.

Reddit is an online forum where users share their thoughts in “threads” on various topics. The popular site includes plenty of discussion on financial subjects like how to deal with job loss, so we sifted through Reddit forums to get a pulse check. People post anonymously, so we cannot confirm their individual experiences or circumstances.

#3158. Moving Toward Autonomous Planning Without Losing Sight Of ROI

Alex Koshulko, Ph.D, is a supply chain planning expert with 10+ years of experience. CEO and co-founder of Streamline.

What if your AI agents hit their KPIs and still cost your company $330,000?

In a simulation published by Supply Chain Management Review, five AI agents represented different supply chain functions. Four improved their performance metrics, yet the simulated company’s EBITDA fell by approximately $330,000 compared with a scenario where their objectives were aligned. Each function pursued its own targets at the expense of the overall business.

For me, this exposes one of the challenges in autonomous planning. An agent can perform its assigned task successfully while making decisions that hurt the company’s financial performance.

As businesses give AI more authority, they need to understand what those decisions will cost, what they will improve and how to measure the results.

Ask a planner why they cut last week’s purchase order by 20%, and you may hear reasons that the planning system doesn’t fully capture. An AI agent reviewing the same order needs the numbers and the context behind them.

Companies preparing for autonomous planning have good reason to start with data management. McKinsey found that approximately 65% of advanced planning system programs failed to achieve their expected ROI, with poor data management among the five leading causes.

Fixing the data addresses part of the challenge. But how do you give an agent the knowledge planners use to interpret it and assess the consequences of their decisions?

The solution might be a contextual layer that brings together ERP data, input from planners and simulation results that show how different planning choices could affect operations and finances. Building it requires mature processes, but the work can improve them.

The goal is to give agents enough context to evaluate the financial consequences of their actions before execution, with people responsible for setting objectives and constraints. But even then, forecasts and simulations cannot guarantee the outcome. Business results must determine when agents receive greater authority.

A planner may spend time exporting reports, reconciling figures and obtaining approvals because the systems involved cannot share information directly. An AI agent with access to the relevant data and business context can handle parts of that work without following the same sequence of steps.

Before delegating a workflow, ask: if an agent had direct access to the right information, would we design the process the same way?

Gartner predicts that cross-functional multiagent systems will execute 35% of enterprise workflows with minimal human approvals by 2030, up from 3% in 2025. As companies delegate more work to agents, they need to distinguish manual steps imposed by system limitations from controls that protect business outcomes.

Data transfers and duplicate checks may no longer require human effort, but approvals tied to financial exposure, customer commitments or difficult-to-reverse decisions serve a purpose. These distinctions should shape the workflow before an agent takes over its execution.

I suggest beginning with a narrow pilot where the consequences of a wrong decision are manageable and can be detected quickly. Replenishing a stable product is a more practical starting point than allocating scarce inventory among strategic customers during a disruption.

The pilot should show whether the business outcome improved against a baseline. Before expanding its authority, the company should test how the agent responds when demand, supply conditions or business constraints change.

The level of autonomy needs to reflect the risks of each decision. A company might allow an agent to handle replenishment within defined limits, require planner approval for higher-value orders and keep strategic allocation decisions under human control.

Gartner’s forecast illustrates how limited autonomous decision-making is expected to remain. While 83% of surveyed organizations had already invested at least $3 million in planning automation, including AI, Gartner expects only 5% of organizations implementing supply chain planning automation to allow systems to make at least 10% of planning decisions autonomously by 2030.

When financial performance improves after an AI rollout, how much of that improvement can we credit to the agent? Inventory may have fallen because demand weakened or supplier terms changed. Both factors need to be accounted for when calculating the agent’s contribution.

It’s also worth looking at the impact on working capital and operating costs. Lower inventory can free up cash, but if it leads to emergency shipments or missed orders, those consequences belong in the assessment, too. So do the costs of deploying and operating AI.

A purchasing decision may take weeks to reveal whether it protected service levels or created excess stock. During that time, an agent could make dozens of similar decisions before the business sees the consequences. Evaluate ROI across the relevant purchasing and replenishment cycles, allowing enough time to measure the financial outcomes of those decisions, including the cost of correcting errors.

Gartner predicts that by 2029, 60% of supply chain planner roles will shift from operational execution to strategic oversight as autonomous planning systems take over routine decisions.​ As a result, I expect planners to spend more time setting the boundaries for autonomous execution. They will define spending limits, decide when human intervention is necessary and review results across planning cycles.​

A planner who once approved individual purchase orders may soon oversee hundreds of decisions made by AI agents. In our practice, for example, a North American CPG manufacturer increased the number of forecast edits by more than 100 times through the use of AI agents. This illustrates how agents can expand the scale of planning activity, but it also makes oversight more important. Planners will need to recognize when established rules require adjustment, whether because supplier conditions have shifted, customer priorities have changed or financial results no longer justify the current approach.​

AI agents will not eliminate the need for human judgment in supply chain planning. While they have tremendous potential in the supply chain, ​their value will depend on how effectively organizations combine autonomous execution with human oversight.​​​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3156. Anthropic Went Public at $111.50 a Share. Here's What a $1,000 Stake Is Worth After Day 1.

It will take more than a day for retail investors to make the kind of money they are likely hoping for from Anthropic.

By Jack Delaney – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:13AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

On Thursday, Nov. 19, Anthropic (ANT -6.21%) opened to the public at $111.50 per share. The stock price touched $115.10 at one point during the day, then fell below $100, closing at $97.17.

While major news outlets report Anthropic shares climbed 17% on the day, for any retail investor who bought $1,000 worth of stock, there's a good chance they've already lost money. This is why that disconnect exists, and what to consider after Anthropic's first day as a publicly traded company.

The returns for retail investors may differ from those reported by major news outlets due to the gap between the initial public offering (IPO) price and the price at which Anthropic shares opened to the public.

The IPO pricing was set at $83, a level typically reserved for high-net-worth individuals and large institutions. For that group, with Anthropic closing at $97.17 per share, their return was 17%.

Let's say a retail investor bought $1,000 worth of stock, and their entire order was filled at the opening price of $111.50. That would give them approximately nine shares. At a closing price of $97.17, that $1,000 investment would have declined to around $870.

Fortunately, for investors who plan to hold for the long haul, day one won't make or break an investment.

Meta Platforms (META -1.76%) is one of the best examples of having a rough IPO and a tough few months of trading before ultimately rewarding long-term investors.

When it went public as Facebook in May 2012, it was chaotic. On the very first day of trading, a glitch delayed the public offering by 30 minutes, and some traders didn't receive confirmation that their trades had gone through. The stock price finished flat on the day, and things only got worse from there. According to research from The Motley Fool, out of the 10 largest IPOs by capital raised to date, Meta has had the worst three-month post-IPO performance, with shares down 47%.

That said, the good news for anyone who still had conviction in the company was that they were handsomely rewarded. Five years after its public debut, the stock price was up 289%. Then, by May 2026, about 14 years after the IPO, the stock price had climbed 1,572%.

What that tells investors about Anthropic is that it's not the first day of trading, or even the first few months, that will determine whether the company was a good investment. It will take years to find out, making it the best fit for an investor who can handle price swings and has a patient approach.

#3155. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.

Anmuth ranks No. 832 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of about 9.40%. See Meta Platforms Financials on TipRanks.

#3151. Coming Out of Retirement in 2027? 3 Things to Brace Yourself For.

It could have more complicated effects on your budget than you imagined.

By Kailey Hagen, CFP® – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:15AM EST

Coming out of retirement might not have been part of the plan for you, but it’s one of your best options if you find yourself draining your savings faster than you anticipated. A steady paycheck can slow the drain on your retirement accounts, and some people find they enjoy the sense of purpose and opportunity to socialize that a job can bring.

But there can be a few drawbacks to coming out of retirement that are worth being aware of before you rejoin the workforce. The following three things are unlikely to be deal-breakers, but they may force you to rethink your budget going forward.

If you've been out of the workforce for years or even decades, finding new employment could take some time. This is especially true if you work in an industry where things change rapidly. You may have to live off your savings for longer than you planned while trying to find new employment.

You can increase your odds of success by staying up to date with the latest industry trends and, if possible, pursuing advanced certifications. Or, if you don't need a ton of extra income, look outside your industry for new opportunities you may not have considered before. Part-time or remote work could give you some flexibility, so you still have time for hobbies.

Working seniors on Social Security who earn more than a certain amount from their jobs are at risk of losing some of their benefits to the earnings test. This withholds $1 from your checks for every $2 you earn over $25,560 in 2027 if you're under your full retirement age (FRA) all year. This is 67 if you were born in 1960 or later. Those who will reach their FRA next year only lose $1 for every $3 they earn over $67,920, and only if they earn that much before their birth month. If you're at or over your FRA for all of 2027, you won't have to worry about the earnings test at all.

Losses from the earnings test could shrink or even temporarily eliminate your Social Security checks, forcing you to rely more heavily on personal savings and income from your job. You’ll get a one-time benefit boost when you reach your FRA to make up for what you lost to the earnings test in years past, but that won't help you much in the short term. Make sure you have a plan for how you'll cover what your benefits won't.

If you're on Social Security, your job could force you to owe Social Security benefit taxes, even if you've never faced them before. Or you could pay more in benefit taxes than you're used to. That's because the government looks at your provisional income -- adjusted gross income (AGI), plus nontaxable interest and half of your annual Social Security benefit -- when deciding whether your benefits are taxable.

Income from a job could increase your AGI, and that could make up to 85% of your benefits taxable at your ordinary income tax rate. This could add hundreds or thousands of dollars to your tax bill. But that doesn't mean you'll have an outstanding balance when you file your return. If you normally get refunds, this might be enough to offset the additional taxes.

None of these things are reasons to avoid seeking out another job if you really need the income. The money you earn will likely be enough to offset these extra costs. But make sure you're budgeting accordingly, so you don't run into any surprises later.

#3150. Your AI Isn’t Misbehaving; It’s Obeying

Global Field CISO at Mimecast. Creator of Risk Reframing. 20+ years across intelligence agencies, cybersecurity, and executive leadership.

​Imagine an employee creates an AI agent to accelerate customer renewals. It reads account histories, reviews support cases, drafts recommendations and updates the CRM. It performs consistently, uses only approved tools and has valid credentials.

Then it exposes one customer’s information while working on another. The security team investigates. There is no compromised identity, no anomalous login, no unauthorized connection.

In August, NIST published its “Human-Centered Cybersecurity Guidelines and Resources Concept Paper,” which argued that decades of security programs have treated people as vulnerabilities to be trained and tested, and the breaches keep coming anyway. Awareness training sets “unrealistic expectations that employees will always make the ‘right’ decisions,” while the real root causes (unusable processes, disruptive controls, a culture nobody absorbed) go unaddressed.

It’s a paper about end users, but read next to AI agents; it describes the same failure one layer removed. The reflex to scrutinize the person closest to the failure—the employee who clicked, the agent that acted—lets the design decision upstream of them off the hook. For agentic AI, that decision is provisioning: the first decision, made before any systems act, when a person defines what an agent is for, what it can touch and where its authority ends. Call the practice of getting it right provisioning assurance.

Most security programs observe activity: what an identity accessed, whether behavior violated policy. AI agents complicate that because they don’t just exercise access; they interpret objectives and act on someone’s behalf, executing the judgment they were given more faithfully than any human would.

Before an agent acts, a person has already decided its purpose, permissions and operating conditions (explicitly, or casually in a prompt or an approval screen). The agent inherits them either way. Provisioning is not only technical setup; it’s the moment human judgment becomes machine authority. Selecting “allow” takes a second. The authority it creates operates repeatedly, at scale.

In September, I argued every AI incident runs on two timelines: the one we investigate and the one that set it in motion. Here’s the version of that split that matters for provisioning: There’s the timeline we know how to read (alerts, logs, containment) and an earlier one, often six months back or longer, when someone decided what the agent was for, what access it would hold and what failure was tolerable. Monitoring tells us what happened after that authority was granted. It doesn’t tell us whether granting it was sound.

An agent doesn’t need to misbehave to cause harm. One instructed to speed up support-case resolution may prioritize speed exactly as directed while overlooking contractual restrictions or a customer’s downstream needs. It may even look successful by the metric it was given.

The renewals agents show where the failure lives: in the original judgment—an objective too narrow, boundaries incomplete, trade-offs never made explicit, no one accountable for the outcome. None of that produces an anomaly. The agent scores clean because it’s faithfully executing a defective charter, and most AI governance programs are built to catch an agent drifting from its instructions, not one whose instructions are the source of the risk.

Seeing that judgment means comparing three versions of an agent’s authority: declared authority (what the provisioner says it does), inferred authority (what its permissions and connections actually make possible) and observed authority (what it does in practice). An agent is described as a feedback summarizer (declared). It can read and modify CRM records and message a collaboration platform (inferred). It starts exporting customer records to an outside environment (observed).

That distinction clarifies who owns the fix. Behavioral drift (an agent departing its baseline or instructions) deserves monitoring and containment. Judgment drift (an employee changes roles, a project ends, an approval step gets removed but the agent’s authority stays put) calls for renewed authorization, not containment. Provisioning failure—original purpose or boundaries were inadequate—requires revisiting the human decision itself. Treat all three as agent-behavior problems, and you fix the symptom, not the judgment.

Every material expansion of authority (a new connector, read access becoming write access, an approval checkpoint removed, ownership transferred) is a new provisioning event, whether or not the organization recognizes it. This matters most when people move: An agent built for a legitimate purpose can keep running on valid credentials long after its creator changes roles or leaves, its authority sitting there un-renewed and unclaimed. The question isn’t just “Is the agent still running?” It’s “Who is renewing the judgment that allows it to run?” Renewal isn’t remediation: It’s what you do because time passed.

Inventories are the right start: You can’t govern what you can’t see. Provisioning assurance is the next step: preserving the decision behind the agent, not just the agent itself. For every agent with meaningful authority, leaders should be able to answer four questions. Who owns its authority and consequences? What outcome was it authorized to pursue? What’s explicitly outside its scope? And what event triggers reauthorization or retirement? Most of this can be inferred from identity and configuration. The human’s job is to confirm what technology can’t: purpose, boundaries, accountability.

That’s provisioning assurance: evaluating whether machine authority rests on identifiable ownership, an adequate purpose and a defined moment for renewal. The goal isn’t to score the employee. It’s to assess the quality of the decision.

NIST is right that stronger cybersecurity starts with people. An agent’s behavior tells us what happened. Its provisioning tells us what human judgment became capable of doing. Assurance tells us whether that judgment was sound. The next generation of AI governance can’t begin with the agent’s first action—by then, the most consequential decision has already been made. Organizations don’t just inherit technology. They inherit authored judgment. Increasingly, that judgment can act.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3149. With Mortgage Rates Rising, Should You Hibernate Until Spring?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Higher borrowing costs aren't the whole story. Here's what to consider before putting your house hunt on hold.

Mortgage rates are back up this week, wiping out modest relief from a monthlong drop that began in mid-October.

The average rate on a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage shot up 29 basis points to 7.28% APR in the week ending Nov. 19, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.) We calculate our weekly average using daily APRs recorded over the past five business days.

A mortgage is a big ol’ loan, so when rates go up, those fractions of a percentage point add up fast. Say you’re borrowing $400,000 on a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage. Compared with last week, your monthly principal and interest payment would be about $78 higher today.

One bad week doesn't make a trend, but a jump like this can make putting your house hunt on hold until spring feel like a safer bet. So … is it?

Before deciding whether to buy a house now or wait, it helps to know what's pushing mortgage rates higher.

Right now, mortgage rates are still feeling the aftershocks from two major inflation reports last week. First, the Consumer Price Index (CPI) showed consumer prices rose more than expected in October. Then, the Producer Price Index (PPI) showed wholesale inflation climbed 6.5% over the past year — its biggest annual increase in nearly four years.

While CPI shows how prices have changed, PPI can offer clues about what's coming next. Faced with higher costs, businesses may raise their own prices to compensate, keeping inflation elevated. Federal Reserve policymakers aren't expecting much relief anytime soon, either. According to meeting minutes released Nov. 18, they expect inflation to remain elevated above their 2% target for a while.

To cool rising prices, the Fed can raise the federal funds rate, which is the benchmark interest rate banks charge each other for overnight loans. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but lenders anticipate its decisions, so mortgage rates often move before the Fed acts. A rate hike is widely expected at the Fed's Dec. 8-9 meeting.

Even after a Fed rate hike, there’s no telling how quickly inflation will cool — or whether mortgage rates will follow. (Inflation is just one of many forces that affect mortgage rates, after all.) So if you do wait until spring to buy a house, don't do it just because you're betting on a cheaper mortgage.

If you're not in a rush to move, winter can be a good time to regroup. Fewer homes typically go up for sale from November through February, so waiting until spring could give you more options.

But new data from the National Association of Realtors suggests this year's slowdown goes beyond the usual seasonal lull. High mortgage rates and home prices are making it tough for many buyers to make the numbers work. For some buyers, waiting isn't a strategy — it's the only option.

Pending home sales, a measure of homes under contract, fell 16.7% in October compared with a year earlier, NAR reports. All four U.S. regions saw declines. At this rate, NAR chief economist Lawrence Yun warned that annual home sales could fall to their lowest level since the mid-1990s.

“Either sizable housing demand is building in the background, or the dream of homeownership is increasingly out of reach for many Americans,” Yun said in a news release.

Whether you're waiting by choice or necessity, you can use the time to strengthen your finances. Paying down debt, strengthening your credit score or saving for a bigger down payment could help you qualify for a better mortgage deal.

Every little bit helps when today’s mortgage rates are this high.

Mortgage preapprovals typically expire after 60 to 90 days. If you're already preapproved, why not keep an eye on listings this winter? (Hey, I’m an optimist. All it takes is one house.)

Think like a seller for a second. If you're listing your house in the dead of winter, you're probably not doing it just for fun. You might have a deadline, and you’re probably more willing to negotiate. In the winter, buyers have less competition — and more room to negotiate a lower price, closing cost credits or repairs. Those savings on the sticker price might help offset today's higher borrowing costs.

Spring typically brings more homes for sale, but it also brings more buyers competing for them. And if mortgage rates end up dropping this spring, you won't be the only one coming out of hibernation.

#3148. Forget the Headlines: Novo Nordisk's (NVO) 4.6% Dividend Yield Is the Real Story for Patient Investors

The dividend is hefty -- and it's been growing briskly, too.

By Selena Maranjian – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:29AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Novo Nordisk ›

There are exciting headlines galore regarding Danish healthcare company Novo Nordisk (NVO +0.17%):

See? It's enough to make one itch to become a shareholder. The stock is well worth considering for your portfolio. But look beyond the headlines, and you'll see another compelling reason to invest in Novo Nordisk: its dividend. The payout recently yielded a fat 4.6% (and when you add in the effect of share repurchases, the total shareholder yield comes in at about 5.5%). For comparison, the S&P 500 index of America's 500 largest companies recently yielded a mere 1%.

It's a growing dividend, too. Unlike American dividend-paying companies, which generally pay dividends quarterly, Novo Nordisk pays twice a year, offering an "annual" dividend and an "interim" one. Together, they totaled $1.85 per share in 2026, and that's up from $0.81 in 2022 and $0.62 in 2019.

Better still, the company's payout ratio -- the percentage of earnings being paid out in dividends -- was recently just 45%. That number suggests that Novo Nordisk shouldn't have any trouble paying future dividends, and that there's plenty of room for further growth.

So -- should you invest in Novo Nordisk? Certainly consider it, especially if you're seeking both income and growth. The shares seem reasonably or appealingly valued at recent levels, with a recent price-to-sales ratio of 3.5, well below the five-year average of 8.1, and a recent forward-looking price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 12.3, not far from the five-year average of 11.9.

Beyond the stock's valuation, Novo Nordisk's business is attractive. It has long been a force in treating diabetes, but now it's also operating in the weight-loss drug realm, with success. Perhaps most promising is its oral GLP-1 drug, which is preferable to a regular injection for many -- and which will soon be available in 20-plus markets.

The company does face competition, and it has had some setbacks in clinical trials, but its pipeline remains promising.

#3144. Where Will VOO Be in 30 Years? Here's What History Says.

It's impressive what the long-term power of compounding can produce.

By David Dierking – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:30AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Vanguard S&P 500 ETF ›

The Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -0.03%) has been one of the simplest and cheapest ways for investors to build long-term wealth. By investing in the S&P 500, you're investing in practically the entire U.S. economic growth story.

Historically, the S&P 500 has delivered an average annual return of around 10%, assuming that dividends are reinvested. That doesn't guarantee similar results in the future, but it does give us a useful benchmark for estimating where the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF could be in 30 years.

Let's assume you invest $10,000 in the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF today, reinvest all the dividends you get along the way, and leave it untouched for 30 years.

If it can match its historical 10% annualized return, that initial investment would grow to approximately $174,500, more than 17 times your starting amount without contributing another dollar over the subsequent three decades.

Even at a more conservative 8% annual return assumption, that same investment would grow to roughly $100,600. It's a testament to the power of long-term compounding growth.

Of course, the market won't deliver a consistent 10% return every year. There will be recessions, bear markets, and significant drawdowns along the way that'll test your discipline. But if you're able to ride these periods out and even keep contributing to your investments over time, the rewards will very likely be substantial.
