#3141. This Oceanfront Hilton Resort in Florida Is Great for Groups

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

The recently renovated Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort offers great views, a well-equipped fitness center and delicious food.

Hilton is making a big play in South Florida with its Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort. The massive 1,000-room property — which underwent an $80 million renovation before opening under the Signia Hilton brand in May 2026 — sits on the beach in Hollywood, Florida, just outside of Fort Lauderdale.

The resort caters primarily to business and group travelers (it’s attached to a massive events space), but it’s also a strong option for vacationers looking for some R&R. The main downside: The location isn’t great if your trip primarily revolves around off-property excursions, dining and nightlife.

Room rates typically run $300 to $600 a night, depending on the season, but you can also use Hilton Honors benefits to make your stay more affordable. There’s also a resort fee of $59 per night — but Hilton waives that when you book using Hilton points.

Hilton covered the cost of my stay for two nights, but the opinions in this review are my own. Here’s what guests can expect at this oceanfront resort.

Since the Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort is part of Hilton Honors, there are several ways to book a stay there.

If you’re using Hilton points, expect to pay 70,000 to 80,000 points a night when standard rooms are available. Hilton elite status — a benefit you can earn by holding any Hilton Honors credit card — gets you the fifth-night-free perk, where you stay for at least five nights but pay for four when booking with points.

For example, if you were to stay five nights at 80,000 points a night, you’d pay 320,000 points total, bringing down your average nightly rate to 64,000 points.

You can also book using Hilton’s generous free night certificates, which can be used at almost any property in the portfolio. If you hold the Hilton Honors American Express Aspire Card, you automatically get a certificate each year. It’s also possible to earn certificates through reaching spending thresholds on the card and on the Hilton Honors American Express Surpass® Card. Terms apply.

Make sure to check the cash rate against the points rate to make sure you’re getting good value from your redemption. NerdWallet values Hilton points at 0.4 cent apiece.

For cash bookings of two nights or more, you might consider using The Hotel Collection via American Express’ travel portal for eligible cardholders. Eligible bookings come with benefits like a $100 property credit and other amenities subject to availability, including a room upgrade, early check-in and late checkout.

Keep in mind that third-party bookings typically don’t earn Hilton points or count toward elite status qualification.

It’s hard to miss the Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort, with its signature H-shaped facade and sleek white-and-glass exterior.

Stepping into the hotel, I was immediately struck by the lush greenery filling the entrance and indoor courtyard. The grand lobby has soaring glass ceilings, giving the space a greenhouse feel, although the strong air conditioning keeps you cool.

Despite its massive size, it doesn’t have a sprawling layout, which I appreciated. Everything is close together, and you can walk from the check-in counter to any of the indoor restaurants in under a minute.

I stayed in an oceanfront suite with a balcony, which was spacious and had a fun beachy vibe. To get to the rooms, you’ll need to use either the north or south tower elevator banks.

In the living room, there was a large sitting area featuring a TV, a couch, an accent chair and a four-person high-top table.

Although I didn’t spend too much time on the balcony — the October balminess was a bit too much for me — I enjoyed stepping out each morning to admire the view.

The bedroom had a king bed with extremely comfortable bedding and an oversized chair that was a great spot to relax and read a book by the window.

The bathroom was spacious, featuring a shower and a separate tub (something every room has).

Even the standard room I toured felt roomy. I liked that it shared similar elements with the suite, including the oversized lounge chair.

This is what you’d normally get when booking a room at the cheapest points price.

There are several dining venues on the property, ranging from a grab-and-go cafe to a high-end steakhouse. It is a luxury resort, so expect the prices to reflect that.

For breakfast, you can head to Palmea Kitchen, which is also open for lunch. I had the breakfast buffet, which offered a nice spread of classics, from hot food items and pastries to cold cuts and smoked salmon.

If you don’t have time for a sit-down breakfast, you can head next door to The Press, which offers made-to-order and grab-and-go food options throughout the day. You can also get smoothies, juices, snacks, pastries and other sweet treats.

The Canteen is another grab-and-go option that also sells snacks, drinks, souvenirs and sundries, similar to a convenience store.

For lunch and dinner, try Solara. It doesn’t have a traditional restaurant feel, as it’s mostly an indoor-outdoor bar space. One side connects to the lobby, while the other side faces the Solara adults-only pool.

It serves a lunch menu, as well as a substantial bar food menu that’s available late into the night.

You can also eat at Playa Beach Bar & Grill, a beachside restaurant serving Latin-inspired cuisine. I enjoyed spending a lazy lunch there and watching the waves crash onto the shore.

For a fancy sit-down dinner, go to Diplomat Prime, the property’s steakhouse. We had a group dinner at the restaurant, and the meats were, of course, the highlight. But don’t sleep on the seafood, which was also delicious.

One of the best parts of the Dip, as it’s affectionately known, is its location right on the beach. Guests can access the stretch of beach directly adjacent to the property. But it’s not a private beach, so you might encounter crowds.

I didn’t spend any time on the beach itself — it was extremely windy while I was there — but the hotel’s resort fee includes amenities like a beach lounge (umbrella not included), a daily one-hour kayak or paddleboard rental, and beachfront yoga classes.

If you don’t want to get sand everywhere, you can head to one of the three pool options: the adults-only Solara pool, the family-friendly lagoon pool and the Verandah Social Club for those 21 and older (requires paid access).

The family-friendly lagoon spans the length of the property and runs beneath the Solara pool. It also has its own hot tub.

I spent an afternoon in a cabana at the Verandah Social Club, which has some smaller plunge pools and a hot tub.

I appreciated the attentive service from the staff there, who constantly checked on whether guests needed anything.

If you’re looking for other ways to stay active, head to the two-story fitness center, featuring a variety of equipment like free weights, exercise bikes, a rowing machine and treadmills.

Next door, you’ll find the spa and wellness center. I had a relaxing massage that incorporated hot stones and aromatherapy.

The locker rooms come with a sauna and steam room, although I wished there were more amenities like a hot tub and cold plunge.

One of the resort fee inclusions is complimentary high-intensity interval training workout classes at the gym across the street. I signed up for an early morning class. The instructor was friendly, and it was a great way to start my day.

Other amenities include a complimentary one-hour e-bike rental each day, as well as a kids’ club with various activities (but drop-off fees apply).

I had a fantastic stay at this resort, but I’d recommend it for a specific traveler.

If you’re planning to stay on-property your entire trip, it’s a great option. That’s probably why it’s such a popular convention hotel, since everything you need is easily accessible.

I loved the hotel’s design, including the jungle-like atmosphere in the lobby, and I found the dining options to be tasty, albeit pricey.

But if your trip revolves around visiting Miami attractions, you’ll likely want to stay closer to the action.

#3138. ​Speed And Steering: Make Room For Both In One-On-Ones You Run

Aditi Khullar, VP at Two Sigma, senior engineer bridging research to production; experience leading AI/ML teams.

When my dad taught me to drive in an empty parking lot, he gave me only two pieces of advice: Watch your speed and look where you want to go.

The same holds true in engineering management, except the lot is never empty and everyone else is driving, too. Here, the questions you ask are your biggest tool. They set the team’s speed and the direction of its mission, and they come in two kinds. Momentum questions (how and when) control the speed, moving the work along a path already chosen. Direction questions (what and why) do the steering; they set priorities and, when needed, force a pivot.

For years, I asked both kinds in the same one-on-one, and I always gravitated toward the momentum questions. My own sense of urgency, and my team’s drive to get things done, kept pulling our conversations toward one more blocker to clear. The direction questions, which could slow things down, kept slipping to next week. So, I split the hour in two: the first half for momentum, the second for direction.

Andy Grove made the case for a full hour decades ago, because the harder topics rarely surface in the first twenty minutes. I recommend you protect a full hour with each report, every week, and give each kind of question its own protected time.

The first part of a one-on-one is about the work in front of your team. Follow their lead and keep it moving: what is blocking you, what do you need from me, how will you test it, when will it land. These questions clear the road and, asked consistently, teach people to ask them of themselves.

They help you as much as it helps them. As Alison Wood Brooks and Leslie John found, questions like these surface risks while they are still easy to fix and give you an honest picture of where the work stands, so you can escalate early and report the status with confidence.

Speed only helps if you are heading the right way, which is where direction questions come in.

This is where you steer, and where you get to know the person you are steering with. As you grow more senior, you earn more license to nudge, direct and, when it matters, drive.

One engineer wanted to grow into a new role, so I chose projects to build those skills. With another, I was honest about how their pace was being perceived and what it could mean for their trajectory. These conversations build trust, because people see you have their interests at heart. The more often you have them, the fewer hard ones you need, and the easier those land.

Skip the direction questions, and what drives a person never gets discussed, and their growth is left to whichever project lands on their desk. Protect this time, and you can match people to work that stretches them, so they grow and the project gets their best.

It is worth noting, however, that some things don’t fit either half. A strained relationship with a teammate, or a hard time outside of work, can matter more than any deadline. When that happens, let the hour make room for it.

The split matters more than the schedule, and the two parts do not have to run on the same rhythm.

During a delivery push, momentum may need a weekly beat while direction moves to every other week, and skip-levels and quarterly reviews can carry the bigger direction questions. What matters is that direction has a protected place somewhere, even when it is not every week.

The balance also shifts with seniority, or what Andy Grove called task-relevant maturity. With junior engineers, the discussion often gravitates to momentum questions, because asking how they will test a change, week after week, is often how they learn to ask it themselves. Senior engineers already ask those questions, so their hour moves toward direction, and too many momentum questions aimed at them could be perceived as micromanagement. Google’s Project Oxygen found the same, with engineers disliking technical micromanagement but welcoming close attention to their careers.

Engineers shift from momentum toward direction as they grow. Teams don’t. They need direction questions at every stage, and only the question changes. A new team needs, “What are we building, and why?” A mature team needs, “Is this still the right thing?” to catch the drift of running on autopilot.

Whatever the mix, the direction questions are where you learn both where the team is heading and where each person fits in it. So, before your next one-on-one, don’t ask what you need to cover. Ask whether you are there to increase the speed or to change the direction, and give each its own portion of the hour.​

The views expressed herein are the author’s own and do not represent the opinions, positions or strategies of the author’s employer or any of its affiliates, employees or officers. This article is for informational purposes only and should not be construed as professional, strategic or investment advice. References to specific companies, products or technologies do not imply endorsement or any commercial relationship.

#3137. Prediction: Nvidia Will Hit a New All-Time High in 2027 (Hint: Watch This Metric)

Nvidia has lost nearly 37% of its market value in recent weeks, but an epic recovery could be in store next year.

By Adam Spatacco – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:30AM EST

It's been a turbulent November for Nvidia (NVDA -2.66%) as the company deals with the unexpected resignation of founder and longtime CEO Jensen Huang. Uncertainty surrounding the company's leadership transition, investigations involving ties to China, and questions about its artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure partnerships are weighing heavily on investor sentiment.

Nevertheless, Nvidia's latest financial results suggest the company's business is expanding at a record pace. During its fiscal third quarter (ended Oct. 25), Nvidia generated $115.8 billion in revenue, representing 103% year-over-year growth. Meanwhile, adjusted earnings per share (EPS) climbed 111% to $2.68. Despite this impressive performance, Nvidia stock is trading roughly 37% below its all-time high.

With that said, one financial metric could ultimately drive the stock to new highs: earnings per share (EPS). Nvidia has two opportunities to expand EPS over the coming year, combining organic profit growth with an enormous share repurchase program.

Nvidia's first opportunity to increase EPS comes directly from its underlying business. The company is a key beneficiary of AI-driven demand from hyperscalers expanding their data center build-outs. Nvidia's data center division generated $107.6 billion in revenue during the third quarter, increasing 110% year over year.

What's particularly impressive is Nvidia's ability to convert its top-line growth into substantial profits. During the third quarter, the company reported a gross margin of 74.8%. This reflects the pricing power associated with Nvidia's GPUs, networking products, and CUDA software ecosystem. These economics allow Nvidia to generate enormous profits alongside an accelerating revenue base.

Although management expects gross margin to moderate to between 71% and 72% in the fourth quarter, it anticipates a recovery to between 72% and 73% in fiscal 2028. More importantly, interim CEO Colette Kress raised the company's fiscal 2028 revenue growth outlook to approximately 75% year over year, up from its previous projection of 70%.

Even if gross margin remains below its peak, Nvidia can still produce higher earnings through increased sales volumes and operating leverage. In other words, as revenue grows, certain operating expenses may increase at a slower rate relative to sales. This would allow additional revenue to contribute disproportionately to operating profits. The idea here is that Nvidia doesn't necessarily need to maintain record gross margins to achieve record earnings.

Nvidia's second earnings catalyst is its share repurchase authorization. In September, Nvidia's board of directors approved a $150 billion increase to its existing repurchase program. As of the end of the fiscal third quarter, Nvidia had $227.3 billion remaining under its authorization.

Management affirmed its intention to execute the remaining portion of the repurchase authorization by the end of fiscal 2028. This makes stock buybacks an important, albeit underrated, component of Nvidia's capital allocation strategy. The significance of Nvidia's share repurchases becomes clearer once you understand how EPS is calculated.

Earnings per share equals a company's net income divided by its shares outstanding. When Nvidia repurchases and retires shares, the company effectively reduces the denominator in that equation. This means EPS can increase even if net income remains unchanged.

For example, suppose a company generates $100 billion in annual net income across 25 billion shares. That would translate into EPS of $4.00. If the same company reduced its share count by 5%, while net income remained unchanged, EPS would increase to $4.21 without any additional revenue or profit growth.

Stock prices are influenced by two interconnected variables: earnings per share and the valuation multiple investors are willing to pay for those earnings. A company's share price can rise even when its price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio remains unchanged, provided EPS increases. This unique relationship makes the mechanics around Nvidia's potential earnings expansion particularly interesting.

Nvidia's combination of projected revenue growth, compounding profitability, and aggressive share repurchases creates a compelling outlook around next year's EPS. These catalysts provide the foundation for a bounceback in Nvidia stock even without assuming a dramatic increase in the company's valuation multiple.

I think Nvidia stock could soar next year, driven by accelerating EPS growth rather than speculative enthusiasm. Against this backdrop, investors should monitor Nvidia's net income growth and changes in shares outstanding over the coming quarters. Taken together, these metrics could reveal whether Nvidia's financial performance is strong enough to overcome its recent challenges and propel the stock to new highs.

#3135. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#3134. Cathie Wood's Ark Has Been Selling Palantir Stock. Under $160, I'd Buy It Now.

Shares of the AI software firm have kept sliding since the sale. Is that a reason to steer clear?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:44AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Palantir Technologies ›

Funds run by Cathie Wood's Ark Invest sold 58,339 shares in Palantir Technologies (PLTR -1.33%) on Nov. 12, worth about $10 million at the day's close of $171.58. Ark's flagship fund sold 53,547 of the shares, and its internet-focused fund sold the other 4,792.

The artificial intelligence (AI) software company's stock has kept dropping since. As I write, Palantir trades near $157, about 9% below where Ark sold and about 25% below its Oct. 9 record close of $209.05.

A big growth investor selling into a slide can look like a vote against the stock. But I don't think that particular sale says much. And the fall since then has sent shares well below the buy price of around $172 I set for Palantir earlier this month.

Ark trades its Palantir stake often, and in both directions. It sold around $9.6 million of the stock in three days from Oct. 19 through Oct. 21. Then on Nov. 3, the day after Palantir's third-quarter results, Ark bought 63,638 shares across five funds, worth around $11.2 million at that day's close of $175.40.

So, the Nov. 12 sale mostly undid a purchase Ark made just after earnings. Four days later, though, Ark's robotics-focused fund sold around $16 million of the stock. Counting every November trade through Nov. 16, Ark has sold more Palantir shares than it bought this month.

The stake is still big, too. As of Nov. 18, Ark's flagship fund owned around 2.1 million Palantir shares, worth about $325 million, according to Ark's daily holdings file. The flagship fund's 53,547-share sale on Nov. 12 was under 3% of that.

Showing how consistently the company has topped its own forecasts, Palantir's third-quarter revenue climbed 96% year over year to around $2.31 billion, about 7% above the midpoint of the guidance management gave in August. It had done that twice already this year. First-quarter revenue was around 6% above the guidance midpoint, and second-quarter revenue about 8% above it. And every report brought a higher full-year outlook. Palantir's 2026 revenue guidance began the year at around $7.19 billion. It's now around $8.53 billion, up about 19%, which would mean growth of 91% over 2025.

The firm also raised its forecast for U.S. commercial revenue (sales to American businesses) to at least $3.56 billion, which would be growth of at least 143%.

For the last three months of 2026, Palantir sees revenue of about $2.65 billion, up 88% year over year. That would be down from the third quarter's 96% growth. But if Palantir beats its forecast by a similar margin again, fourth-quarter revenue could come in near $2.8 billion, about twice the $1.41 billion it posted a year before.

Plus, profits are likely to climb faster than sales. Management sees fourth-quarter non-GAAP (adjusted) operating income of about $1.64 billion, over double the $798 million it posted for the same quarter last year.

Palantir earned $1.43 a share in its past four reported quarters. At roughly $157, this puts the stock's price-to-earnings ratio near 110.

That's a high price by nearly any standard. But it's far less extreme than it was a few weeks ago. At its Oct. 9 record close, Palantir's price-to-earnings ratio stood near 179, based on the $1.17 per share Palantir earned over the four quarters through June. Some of that decline in the price-to-earnings ratio came from the earnings side, as the third-quarter report raised the company's trailing earnings per share by about 22%.

Measured against analysts' consensus estimate for Palantir's 2027 earnings per share, the stock's forward price-to-earnings ratio is around 62.

Sure, a stock at about 110 times earnings needs years of fast profit growth to pay off. And the guided slowdown to 88% revenue growth is a reminder that Palantir's growth will eventually cool. But I think a business that's topped its own forecast three straight quarters makes that bet easier to take.

In the end, I don't think Ark's trim tells investors much about where Palantir is going. The company's own numbers say more, and they still point to fast growth in both revenue and profits.

Under $160, I'd consider buying Palantir stock now. But I'd add shares in small batches over the coming months, as a stock this volatile could easily dip further first.

Atmos Rewards offers six communities, each with its own travel perks and opportunities to earn bonus points

Atmos Rewards is the airline loyalty program for Alaska Airlines and Hawaiian Airlines. One of its newer features is Atmos Communities, which offer additional rewards and benefits tailored to different travel interests.

There are six communities, including two reserved for Alaska and Hawaii residents. The other four are open to members regardless of where they live. But don’t get caught up in the community names. The best choice for you may depend more on your travel plans than the groupings suggest.

Here’s how to decide which Atmos Community best fits your travel patterns.

Atmos Communities are like groups within the Atmos Rewards program that offer additional benefits based on different travel interests.

They’re free to join for Atmos Rewards members. Four communities are available to all members, while community Club 49 is reserved for Alaska residents and community Huaka’i by Hawaiian is reserved for Hawaii residents.

You can belong to only one community at a time and switch communities once per calendar year. Consider your upcoming travel plans before choosing.

Joining a community is easy: Log in to your Atmos Rewards account and navigate to “Account overview.” Select “Join a Community,” browse the options and click the “Join” button under the one you choose.

Each Atmos Community has a list of 10 destinations where members can earn bonus points and status points. Although the destinations differ by community, the rewards structure is the same.

Through Dec. 31, 2027, members can earn up to 10,000 bonus points and 10,000 status points by visiting qualifying destinations.

Here’s how many points you’ll earn as you reach each milestone:

Visit one destination, earn 100 status and bonus points.

Visit two destinations, earn 200 status and bonus points.

Visit three destinations, earn 300 status and bonus points.

Visit four destinations, earn 400 status and bonus points.

Visit five destinations, earn 500 status and bonus points.

Visit six destinations, earn 750 status and bonus points.

Visit seven destinations, earn 1,000 status and bonus points.

Visit eight destinations, earn 1,250 status and bonus points.

Visit nine destinations, earn 2,500 status and bonus points.

Visit 10 destinations, earn 3,000 status and bonus points.

The first member of each community to visit all 10 qualifying destinations during the challenge period will also earn 100,000 bonus points.

New lists of qualifying destinations for each community take effect on Jan. 1, 2028.

Here’s a closer look at the six communities, their benefits and the destinations eligible for bonus points.

Get 20% off Atmos Rewards Unlocked experiences.

Don’t be persuaded by the names of the communities. Look a little deeper, because there’s a lot of overlap between destination lists. Compare the options against your upcoming travel plans.

You can change communities once per calendar year, so you aren’t locked in to your initial choice indefinitely. However, switching communities resets your destination-challenge progress, but you’ll retain any points you’ve already earned.

If your home airport (or one you visit frequently) is already on a community’s list, you may have an easier time earning points. Also consider whether a particular community benefit, such as free checked bags or points back on award flights for kids, outweighs the destination challenge.

Finally, pay attention to airport codes. For example, Tokyo’s Narita (NRT) and Haneda (HND) appear on different community lists. New York’s qualifying airport is JFK, while it is DCA in Washington, D.C. If you have a choice between airports, check which one qualifies for your community’s challenge.

#3130. Would Warren Buffett Buy Anthropic? This Is the One Question He'd Have

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:57AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Legendary investor Warren Buffett led Berkshire Hathaway for 60 years, roughly doubling the annual gains of the S&P 500 over that period.

Buffett was also known for his folksy investing advice, and his #1 criterion for investing in a stock was whether it has an economic moat or not, his phrase for a sustainable competitive advantage.

Anthropic (ANT -6.21%) just completed its IPO, raising more money in its initial public offering than any company in history at $81.3 billion.

The AI lab has reported jaw-dropping growth this year, with revenue up 1,200% over its first three quarters. However, Buffett's top concern may be the most important question investors can ask about Anthropic right now.

Anthropic has established itself as the leading AI lab, taking market share from rival OpenAI over the last year, and surpassing it in revenue as it's released a series of frontier models and new Claude products that have decimated competing software stocks.

However, skeptics have argued that the company lacks a sustainable competitive advantage, that token prices are falling as cheaper models take more of its business, and that it's essentially in an arms race with OpenAI, which could make it difficult for either company to remain consistently profitable.

It's clear that the market opportunity in AI is massive, but that alone doesn't establish an economic moat; it just makes the stakes that much higher.

Anthropic believes that its scale and growth have enabled flywheels that constitute sustainable competitive advantages. The first is its "improvement loop," which essentially means that as more people use the product and it gets more feedback, the models improve, which increases both users and use cases. Google Search benefits from a similar flywheel.

The second is its “investment loop”, whereby higher growth leads to more revenue to invest in Claude, which leads to better models and more capabilities. Both loops drive improvements in Claude.

According to this logic, Anthropic believes size and scale are its main competitive advantages. The company also sees Claude, or the frontier intelligence behind its AI chatbot technology, as part of its competitive advantage, and that's been demonstrated by Anthropic's ability to overtake OpenAI despite a later start.

Buffett is a committed value investor and preferred to invest in companies that he knew how to value.

Anthropic may face as much uncertainty as any other company in market history. Some expect its blistering growth to fizzle as companies move away from "tokenmaxxing," or maximizing token consumption, and as cheaper models eat into Anthropic's market share. As token prices come down, some investors are ringing alarm bells about the sustainability of generative AI as a business model, as cheaper models built on distillation could put significant pressure on frontier labs like Anthropic and OpenAI.

Buffett almost certainly would not buy the Anthropic IPO for the same reason that he passed on stocks like Alphabet and Amazon early in their history, though he later regretted those decisions.

Anthropic is full of uncertainty, and there's no easy way to predict the company's cash flow, even a year from now.

However, just because Anthropic isn't right for Buffett doesn't mean it doesn't belong in your portfolio. Anthropic has enormous upside potential. The company has identified a $40 trillion market opportunity in front of it, and it has a technological lead and a disruptive product that could touch everything from chip design to robotics to drug discovery.

Anthropic won't become a Berkshire holding anytime soon, but after a moderate gain on its IPO day, the stock still has a lot of room to run. For growth investors, Anthropic is worth getting some exposure to.

#3125. The 1 Unstoppable ETF I'd Buy Today and Hold for Decades

Returns matter most to investors, and this ETF has a strong record.

By Neil Patel – Nov 20, 2026 at 9:00AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Invesco QQQ Trust ›

Owning exchange-traded funds (ETFs) can be a smart approach to stock market investing. There are an unlimited number of flavors, though, which can be overwhelming. According to ETF Database, the U.S. has more than 3,800 equity-focused ETFs. How can you even begin to narrow down the list?

Investors should consider important variables, such as fees, performance, and exposure. Taking these factors into account, there's an unstoppable ETF that stands out. It's one I'd think about buying today and holding for decades.

In the last decade, the Invesco QQQ Trust (QQQ -0.93%) has rewarded shareholders with a total return of 527% (as of Nov. 16). The S&P 500 index (^GSPC +0.00%), viewed as the preeminent stock market benchmark that represents 80% of the total U.S. market's value, has delivered a total return of 308% over that time. The QQQ wins by a mile in terms of performance.

It owns the 100 largest non-financial stocks trading on the Nasdaq exchange. Technology stocks rule, which helps explain its impressive track record. And concentration is high, with the top 10 businesses making up 46% of the ETF.

The fee is tough to argue with. The Invesco QQQ Trust charges an expense ratio of 0.18%. That's a small price to pay for such stellar gains.

It looks like an obvious investment vehicle to own compared to what professionals offer. The vast majority of actively managed large-cap funds deliver returns that fall short of the S&P 500 index over long time horizons. But they still charge clients high fees. That's a terrible deal.

The growing importance of the technology sector has been the most powerful trend shaping the economy and stock market in recent memory. This has played directly to the benefit of this ETF. But there's absolutely no guarantee that the Invesco QQQ Trust will continue crushing the overall market in the future.

Artificial intelligence (AI) has been a key tailwind. But the technology's financial payout may fail to live up to the hype, which could spur near-term pain as AI stocks face downward pressure.

If you're staring at a time horizon measured in decades, though, I believe it's a mistake not to be bullish on technology companies. Nobody knows what new industries, businesses, products, and services will be created. But it's reasonable to assume that disruption and innovation will continue. Owning the Invesco QQQ Trust ensures you'll capture tomorrow's winners.

Position size should still reflect your investment risk tolerance. For aggressive investors, putting 25% of your portfolio in this ETF, provided you don't have overlapping exposure to the same stocks elsewhere, could be the right move.

#3123. Tokenomics In Commercial Real Estate AI: 4 Questions To Ask Before Starting Each Task

Wesley D. Snow, Co-Founder and President of Ascendix Technologies. 30+ years in software development; a pioneer in AI for Real Estate.

​For many firms, AI cost management begins with an unexpected bill.

For a long time, the cost of each AI task was hard to track, partly because the tools didn’t show it and partly because there is no clear understanding of how many tokens each task will take.

As a solo worker who uses AI for tasks it can facilitate, and not every little thing in my to-dos, I rarely hit the token wall. But as the head of a company full of workers who are curious about exploring AI and implementing it into their workflows, an overcharge is actually something I expect.

AI companies priced their plans to win market share, and many users, including me, built daily habits around those tools. The next invoice is often an unpleasant reality check. Mine was $1,500, and it came from a single automated job. ​

​AI providers bill each request in tokens, and a token is roughly three-quarters of a word. Each request is billed for what AI reads (cheaper input tokens) and, separately, for what it writes (more expensive output tokens). For example, according to Anthropic’s current pricing, Fable 5.1 charges $10 per million input tokens and $50 per million output tokens it writes. So, each written word costs five times as much as a read one.

The choice of model is an even larger factor than the read/write price split. The smallest Claude model, Haiku 5.5, charges $0.10 per million input tokens, 100 times less than Fable. By my rough math, a typical deal summary costs about 15 cents on the top model and less than a penny on the smallest one.

A difference of a few cents matters little for one request and adds up quickly for automated jobs that run many times a day. ​​

Looking up a contact or sorting emails by sender takes a person a few seconds. An AI assistant, given the same task, searches your connected systems and reads through records to find a phone number you could have looked up yourself, and you pay for every record it reads.

Summarizing a 60-page lease or rebuilding a deal history from scattered emails takes a person’s hours, and AI does it in minutes, so the time saved easily covers the token cost.

Because output tokens cost more than input ones, tasks with a lot of reading and a short answer are the cheapest way to use AI.

A lease summary fits that pattern, since the AI reads the full lease and writes a page or less. An interactive dashboard or a deck works the other way, because nearly every token in it is generated output. Generating an email is still relatively cheap, especially when you do not use the most advanced model on such a trivial task.

Visual, dynamic, interactive and self-updating output is the most expensive kind.

A mistake in a rough first draft of an email takes a minute to fix, while a lease abstract with the wrong rent escalation can lead to a billing dispute and a lost client.

AI tools make errors more often than their confident tone suggests. Invented details and outdated information are not as common in AI output as it used to be, but the time spent checking should count toward the task cost.

A question you ask once is billed once, while a job that runs on a schedule is billed on every run for as long as it stays switched on.

My $1,500 bill came from a scheduled job. A while back, I set up a job that reads my inbox every three hours, matches senders to CRM records, and logs the emails. It reads every message, including unqualified cold pitches from data sellers and follow-ups from a webinar I attended once. It ran on the most expensive model because that was the default setting, and once it was running, I stopped checking it. ​

Large companies run into the same problem on a larger scale. Uber reportedly used up its 2026 AI budget in four months and then capped employees at $1,500 per tool per month, roughly the amount my inbox job cost me. ​​

Run a week of your tasks through those four questions and sort them into the ones worth sending to AI and the rest.

Lease summaries, meeting prep from emails and notes scattered across systems, deal histories and first drafts can usually be sent to AI, because each one replaces hours of reading with a short answer that is quick to check.

Contact lookups, inbox sorting and dashboards nobody opens can usually be done without AI, because a person can do them faster or nobody uses the result.

Repeating this as your tasks, the available models and their prices change will lead you to effective AI cost management instead of slowing down AI adoption or stopping its use at all. ​

It also helps to tell the AI that you’re working within a budget and ask it to flag tasks where a cheaper model or a shorter answer would do. In my experience, it suggests useful savings once it knows that cost matters. ​

I still use AI every day, but I now check what a task will cost before I hand it over. The inbox job behind my $1,500 bill has been replaced by an AI email parser built for that task. It operates on a cheaper model and can process every incoming email automatically, but I keep that setting switched off and send it only the emails I want logged in the CRM, so webinar follow-ups and cold pitches never reach it.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased today, but borrowing costs are still expensive for many home buyers.

Mortgage rates dropped today, but anything above 7% still puts pressure on home buyers’ budgets.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.24% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and three basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

A significant drop is eye-catching, but you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Next week, the Nerds are waiting for the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index, due out Wednesday. It's the Federal Reserve's preferred measure of inflation, so investors will be watching closely. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but lenders anticipate its decisions, so mortgage rates often move before the Fed acts. Stubbornly high inflation is a big reason why we’re seeing upward pressure on mortgage rates right now.

If PCE shows inflation comes in hotter than expected, mortgage rates could climb as markets anticipate further Fed rate hikes. (An increase to the federal funds rate is already widely expected at the Fed’s Dec. 8-9 meeting.) A cooler reading could ease some pressure on mortgage rates for now.

I’m not getting my hopes up too high for meaningfully lower inflation, though. Last week's Producer Price Index showed wholesale inflation climbed 6.5% over the past year — its fastest pace in four years. Wholesale inflation measures the price businesses pay for things. When businesses’ costs go up, they could pass those along to consumers in the form of higher prices — keeping the flames of inflation alive.

Whether Wednesday's PCE brings relief or another helping of bad inflation news, we'll know before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.74% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3120. Anthropic Jumped 17% in Its Debut. Here’s Why I’m Waiting to Buy ANT Stock.

Anthropic's hot debut on the market tells me everything I need to know.

By Keithen Drury – Nov 20, 2026 at 9:01AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Anthropic's (ANT -6.21%) IPO was one of the most successful in history, with the stock spiking to $111.50 after the IPO price was set at $83 per share. However, the stock settled down immediately after that and ended the day at $97.17 per share, up 17%. That's a large gain on day one, and investors may be a bit wary about investing in a stock that rose that much in one day.

I'm in the same boat and am staying patient by not buying Anthropic shares yet. Why? I've got two important reasons that investors should keep in mind.

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei. Image source: Getty Images.

First, I don't know enough about Anthropic's business and how its management team does earnings reports. That could shift my sentiment one way or another, and I won't know anything about that for a few quarters. So, it could be six months or more before I'm comfortable with how Anthropic does its earnings releases.

On another earnings note, I want to see how Anthropic's revenue growth is trending. There's no debate about how fast Anthropic is growing, as revenue is increasing at a lightning pace. However, it is slowing down.

During Q3, its revenue totaled $17 billion, up more than 12-fold year over year. In Q2, its revenue totaled $11.6 billion and $4.7 billion in Q1. When you calculate the quarter-over-quarter growth rates, it reveals something interesting. In Q1, Anthropic's quarter-over-quarter growth rate was 132%. In Q2, that figure accelerated to 144%. In Q3, that total fell off a cliff to 47%.

That may seem like a reason to panic, and looking at the actual dollar figures provides more color. From Q1 to Q2, Anthropic added $6.82 billion in revenue. From Q2 to Q3, it added $5.41 billion. So, even from a raw revenue standpoint, Anthropic's Q3 was not nearly as successful as its Q2.

The question is: will that trend continue in Q4, or will there be a great jump in Q4 due to pent-up demand that maybe wasn't met in Q3?

I have no idea how to answer this question, which is why I think that staying patient here makes a lot of sense.

History is also on my side, as I've seen plenty of hot IPOs lose their luster over the weeks after going public.

The pattern of many hot tech IPOs is pretty clear: the stock rises for a while, then comes tumbling down after initial investor demand has been met.

Wall Street saw this pattern earlier this year with the Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -4.14%) IPO, and I wouldn't be surprised if Anthropic's stock behaves similarly. However, Anthropic does have one item working in its favor: the end of the year.

Stocks tend to do quite well at the end of the year because portfolio managers are positioning their clients for 2027 and showing which stocks they're in. What better stock to show ownership of than the latest and greatest artificial intelligence stock that hit the market?

Anthropic checks a lot of boxes, and it could become a popular buy over the next few months. However, I don't know if that fervor will last, and it could dissipate throughout the start of 2027, aligning with the IPO sell-off schedule that companies like SpaceX have experienced.

As a result, I think investors are better off staying patient and waiting about six months before buying the stock. I've done that with SpaceX, and I think staying patient with these new IPOs is key to investing success.

#3119. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

#3116. Netflix Stock Is Down 36% and Wall Street Keeps Downgrading It. History Says That's a Mistake.

The streaming giant's rising profitability may help improve overall investor sentiment in the coming months.

By Manali Pradhan, CFA – Nov 20, 2026 at 9:07AM EST

Shares of Netflix (NFLX +0.80%) are down by more than 36% so far in 2026 (as of Nov. 18), and some on Wall Street have been growing gloomier about its outlook. Among the analysts who have downgraded its stock this year are those at prominent financial institutions such as Wells Fargo, HSBC, and Needham.

However, the company subsequently introduced paid account sharing and a lower-priced ad-supported subscription tier that allowed the company to sell commercials for the first time. Netflix's operating margin also increased from 18% in 2022 to 27% in 2024.

The question now is whether Netflix can continue growing earnings despite its currently weakening viewer engagement.

There have been other rough spots for Netflix before.

Those gains took time to materialize. Investors who bought Netflix immediately after the January 2022 downgrades were still in the red a year later. But over the longer term, improving business performance matters more than analyst downgrades.

On Sept. 22, HSBC downgraded Netflix from buy to hold and reduced its 12-month price target from $96 to $76, highlighting weaker viewer engagement and growing competition from Alphabet's YouTube. On Oct. 21, Needham also downgraded the stock from buy to hold due to the company's slowing revenue growth and concerns about its content offerings.

In the third quarter, Netflix's revenue increased 11.8% year over year to $12.9 billion, while operating income jumped 37% to $4.5 billion. However, its results from Q3 2025 included a $619 million charge stemming from a tax dispute with Brazilian authorities. Excluding the impact of that expense on the 2025 results, operating income would have increased 15% year over year.

Netflix's revenue growth is also slowing. The company's year-over-year revenue growth rate declined from 16.2% in the first quarter to 11.8% in the third quarter. Netflix expects further moderation in revenue growth to 11.5% in the fourth quarter. Management also reduced its 2026 advertising revenue forecast from $3 billion to $2.7 billion, mainly because some advertising features are taking longer than expected to launch.

Investors should also consider a $2.8 billion payment Netflix received after its proposed acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery fell through. That one-time termination fee increased Netflix's reported net income and operating cash flow in 2026, but did not affect operating income. Hence, Netflix's reported earnings may make the stock appear cheaper than it is relative to its recurring profits.

At its Nov. 18 closing price of $59.82, Netflix traded at approximately 15.9 times analysts' expected 2027 earnings of $3.77 per share. This is considerably below Netflix's forward earnings multiples of more than 30 in each of the previous three years. However, buying at that lower valuation will benefit investors only if Netflix can sustain its expected earnings growth.

Additionally, the company's existing subscription business continues to generate substantial profits. Netflix expects an operating margin of 31.5% in 2026, up from 29.5% in 2025.

Hence, I believe that interested long-term investors should buy Netflix gradually at current prices. The company's track record of successful turnarounds demonstrates its ability to adapt, while its current profitability supports further growth.

#3115. ​Why Small Businesses Are In Cybercriminals’ Firing Line

Smaller organizations face the same cyber threats as enterprises but have fewer resources to defend themselves. In a February 2026 Morning Consult survey of U.S. small business owners (on behalf of Public Private Strategies Institute), 72% reported being hit by attacks in the prior year, most often through payment fraud and email phishing. Affected companies averaged about $60,000 in losses from the former and more than $90,000 from email compromise.​​

Verizon’s 2026 Breach Impact Study (download), an analysis of U.S. cyber insurance claims, found that in the most severe cases, losses at small and midsize entities exceeded 7% of revenue, compared with no more than 2% at larger companies. Size is no protection, and the attacks behind these losses are getting cheaper to run.​

Attackers see small businesses as easy prey with predictable gaps, such as stretched IT teams and weak authentication policies. Owners recognize the risk, with 63% of those surveyed by Morning Consult calling cyber threats a serious problem, but most aren’t ready to defend against these attacks. Only 40% are prepared for payment fraud, 41% for email compromise, 31% for social engineering scams and 30% for a ransomware attack.

In the same Morning Consult survey, 76% of targeted businesses said AI was used in the attack. The FBI’s Internet Crime Complaint Center (ICCC) logged 22,364 complaints referencing AI in 2025, with losses of nearly $893 million. The figure is likely higher, as the ICCC only counted cases where victims mentioned AI.

Phishing is one example of how AI has made attacks easier to execute. With the technology, bad actors can write polished, personalized emails that match the tone of legitimate companies and colleagues, without the spelling and grammar mistakes employees were trained to spot. Details from social media and company websites make a message credible and urgent enough that people click before checking.

The various tactics are deployed with the goal of capturing a username and password. With reuse of the latter an entrenched problem, one stolen login can unlock several other professional and personal accounts. The 2026 Verizon Data Breach Investigations Report (DBIR) (download) found credential abuse appeared across the attack chain in 39% of breaches, and 73% of ransomware victims had an infostealer infection or credential leak in the year before the attack. The report also found a median of 4% of Active Directory passwords had already been compromised, compared with fewer than 1% that failed complexity checks.​

NIST SP 800-63B-4 (download), the current federal guidance, prohibits composition rules and calls for passwords to be screened against a blocklist of compromised values when they’re created or changed. Because the check runs only at those moments, a password that passes can be exposed in a later breach. Multifactor authentication is an important layer of protection, yet only 48% of small businesses in the Morning Consult survey have adopted it.

To mitigate the various risks, small businesses can shore up their defenses with the following six steps:

1. Follow NIST guidance on passwords. Drop complexity rules and screen new and changed passwords against known compromised credentials.

2. Don’t reuse passwords. If credentials are compromised, they can be used to gain access to other accounts, potentially with financial information.

3. Keep checking after a password is set. Monitor for employee credentials that appear in new breaches or infostealer logs and require a reset when one does. NIST already calls for a password change when there’s evidence of compromise.

4. Extend MFA to every account that supports it and remove any option to sign in with a password alone. Start with email and payment systems, as fraud and email compromise are the attacks owners report most often. MFA doesn’t solve exposed credentials if users can still sign in with a password when the second factor isn’t available.

5. Verify before acting. Treat pressure to act immediately as a warning sign, especially in a message asking users to click a link or share details. Check the sender’s address, domain or phone number for small mismatches, such as extra digits in a familiar domain, and confirm anything unusual by calling a number you already know.

6. Vet providers on identity coverage. For a company without security staff, a managed service provider (MSP) or value-added reseller (VAR) is a way to put controls in place. Before signing with a provider, confirm they integrate with Active Directory or cloud identity tools, monitor for credential exposure on an ongoing basis and can support frameworks such as NIST, PCI-DSS and HIPAA.

As generative AI makes it easier and cheaper to create effective cyberattacks like phishing, more stolen logins are likely to circulate. Cybercriminals are quick to take advantage, with the DBIR finding a median of 95 days between an infostealer infection or credential leak and a ransomware attack. To reduce risk, small businesses must modernize their approach and adopt the steps outlined.​

#3113. Bloom Energy Is Down 50% From Its High. Should You Buy the Dip?

Bloom has lost half its value. Is this a buying opportunity, or should you stay away?

By Steven Porrello – Nov 20, 2026 at 9:15AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Bloom Energy ›

My, how the tables have turned on Bloom Energy (BE -6.44%), a stock that traded above $340 as recently as this June. Today, Bloom trades at about $172.

Obviously, when a stock drops 50% while also experiencing surging revenue growth (138% year over year in the third quarter) and raising full-year revenue guidance for several quarters in a row, the question naturally arises: Is this a buying opportunity?

The thing is, Bloom's extraordinary growth has come at an extraordinary cost. Last quarter, it reported $225 million in operating income, yet it also burned through $27 million in operating cash and spent roughly $153 million in capital expenditures. All in all, free cash flow was negative by about $181 million.

Lucky for Bloom, it has about $2.5 billion in cash and equivalents, so it's not up a creek without a paddle. Still, I believe it's important to distinguish between a company that's growing quickly and one growing profitably. This is especially pertinent for Bloom, whose capital requirements have historically been substantial and whose ability to generate sustainable profits and free cash flow has long been a question.

It's also important to consider that Bloom stock, even after its 50% decline, still trades at roughly 11 times its projected 2026 revenue -- a sizable valuation for a company whose primary customers, data center developers, could face delays if the enormous investments in AI fail to deliver the returns expected of them.

Bloom might be trading lower than it was over the summer, but it's not necessarily a bargain. I would continue to treat it as a growth stock with the potential to supply power to data centers, but with plenty of challenges to overcome before that growth translates into profits.

#3107. Why AI Agent Governance Must Be Built Into Enterprise Architecture

As AI agents take action across enterprises, governance must be built into their architecture to ensure accountability, protect customers and preserve trust.

→ Baca artikel asli

#3106. Every AI Agent Needs An Owner

Jesse Stockall is Chief Architect, Flexera.

​AI agents have officially moved from controlled pilots into everyday enterprise workflows. While this shift unlocks increased productivity, it risks creating a new layer of technology sprawl. Agents can access data, trigger actions and consume resources with limited human involvement. That makes them fundamentally different from much of the software enterprises are accustomed to managing.​

Enterprises spent the first phase of generative AI asking, “What can we automate?” As agents move into production, architects now have to answer a harder set of questions: Who is accountable for them? What are they allowed to do? What should the business be willing to spend on them?​

Negative consequences are not inherent to AI agents themselves, but they can emerge when organizations deploy agents faster than they establish the visibility and accountability needed to manage them. With key provisions of the EU AI Act having taken effect in August 2026, governance expectations around AI are also becoming increasingly concrete.​

Beyond determining whether an agent can be connected, organizations must understand how they will identify, govern, observe and ultimately retire each agent. Every AI agent needs an owner because ownership provides the operating model for enforcing standards, governing costs, accounting for actions and scaling agents responsibly within enterprise architecture.​

Here are a few key steps for building sound agentic architecture:​

Autonomy makes accountability essential. An owner is more than the person who built the agent: they are accountable for its purpose, compliance with enterprise standards, access to systems and data, authority to act, outputs, costs and retirement.​

Ownership should follow the agent throughout its lifecycle and remain tied to an accountable person or team, just as the agent itself is tied to a distinct identity. Ninety-five percent of enterprises worldwide now have at least one company-funded agent-enabled workflow in production, with the average organization running roughly 11 across different functional areas. As that number grows, informal ownership will become increasingly difficult to sustain. If nobody can identify who is responsible for an agent after deployment, the organization does not truly control that agent.​

Every cost, resource, license and action should be visible and attributable to an accountable user, whether human or autonomous.​

Enterprises cannot assign accountability for agents they do not know exist, making a complete view of where agents are deployed and who is responsible for them essential. This challenge will only grow as agent adoption accelerates. Gartner forecasted that the average global Fortune 500 company could have more than 150,000 AI agents in use by 2028, up from fewer than 15 in 2025.

Foundational questions all architects should be able to answer are:

• What agents are running across the enterprise?

• What permissions have they been granted?

AI activity can span models, SaaS applications, infrastructure, agents and employees. Every autonomous action must be traceable to its originating agent and accountable owner, with an audit trail and a clear path to rollback.

Multi-agent systems, in which one agent delegates work to specialized subagents, have become a real architectural pattern. Companies such as Anthropic and OpenAI both have systems that support multi-agent architectures where a “manager” agent coordinates work across multiple specialized agents.

Traditional access controls answer an important question: “What can this system reach?” Agentic architecture also must answer another one: “What is this agent actually allowed to do once it gets there?”

Agents need enforceable boundaries beyond basic access control: limits on the actions they may execute autonomously, human approval thresholds for higher-risk actions, cost and budget thresholds, and shutdown mechanisms when behavior falls outside defined standards or expectations.

Agent architecture must therefore govern more than access. It must set standards for how and when agents may act, make their decisions about how and when to act, ensure actions are traceable to an accountable owner, and enforce limits on what those actions may cost.

Cost should not sit outside agentic architecture as a reporting exercise conducted after deployment. Instead, it should remain a constraint an agent is designed to operate within.

An agent that delivers $100,000 of business value may justify substantial consumption, while an agent performing a low-value administrative task may not. Organizations therefore need cost attribution by agent and use case, visibility into consumption and enforceable budget thresholds. Because one workflow can consume models, tokens, infrastructure, SaaS resources and licenses across the technology stack, those costs must be assigned to an accountable human or non-human user rather than left in disconnected reports.

For architects, the goal should be to make an agent’s economic activity as visible and attributable as its technical activity, with both tied to an accountable owner. AI optimization should maximize return rather than simply minimize spend. Mission-critical applications and agents may warrant different spending limits based on their business importance, but those limits should be explicit, measurable and governed.

The lesson from cloud and SaaS is that governance becomes much harder once adoption is already widespread. Enterprises have an opportunity now to avoid repeating that pattern with AI agents.

Agents should enter the technology estate with ownership, visibility, standards, controls and economic accountability already attached. Before an agent enters production, you should be able to answer four questions:

3. How will its actions be traced and reviewed?

4. What is it worth spending on, and where will that cost be assigned?

The enterprises that scale agents successfully will not necessarily be the ones that deploy the most. They will be the ones whose architectures make every agent, resource, cost and action visible, attributable and controllable from the start.​​
