#3104. Robotaxis: 4 Stocks Positioned to Win

Robotaxis should become a multitrillion-dollar market.

By Ryan Vanzo – Nov 20, 2026 at 9:30AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Lucid Group ›

Robotaxis have launched in a growing number of metro areas across the U.S. While total market penetration remains low, experts are bullish about how rapidly the build-out will proceed.

A survey of industry insiders conducted by McKinsey says, "While [Level 4, or highly automated] robo-taxis are now available in the first cities in the United States and China, the global rollout of robo-taxis is now expected to become reality at a large scale in 2030."

Private autonomous vehicles aren't predicted to arrive at scale until after robotaxi fleets are scaled. up. McKinsey also says: "Overall, experts expect that robo-taxis will be the first commercial application for L4 in mobility -- not privately owned cars. L4 urban pilots for private passenger cars are expected to be pushed out from 2030 to 2032, and fully autonomous trucking is expected to reach viability by 2032, not 2031."

Note that this survey of experts believes that robotaxis will become a reality at a large scale by 2030. That suggests a mature robotaxi market in the U.S. will be realized over the next three to four years.

How big could this market become? Tesla (TSLA -0.76%) bull Cathie Wood of ARK Invest expects robotaxis to become an $8 trillion to $10 trillion market globally over the long term. Estimates from Morgan Stanley are more conservative, calling for a $1 trillion global market by 2040. Still, the bank believes the industry will "reach break-even by 2028 and expand significantly over the following years, with approximately 2.5 million robotaxis in service worldwide by 2035."

How can investors profit from the rollout? There are two main strategies, each with two promising stocks worth considering.

Take a look at the market cap of Uber Technologies (UBER +3.37%). The company's $140 billion valuation is significantly larger than many automakers' stocks. That's true even though the company doesn't manufacture a single vehicle in its ride-hailing fleet.

Instead of producing cars, Uber operates a software layer that coordinates their use. The company's ride-sharing platform currently holds a dominant market share in the U.S., with impressive margins and rising free cash flow.

Uber has invested aggressively in the self-driving revolution, taking direct equity stakes in many automakers. But at a high level, the company remains fairly asset-light. The vast majority of ride-share seekers in the U.S. already look to Uber first for a ride. Autonomous vehicles looking to maximize utilization rates will almost certainly add their vehicles to Uber's huge platform.

The exception will be Tesla, which plans to operate its own ride-sharing platform. That strategy makes sense given that it has developed its own self-driving technology and can produce its own vehicles at scale.

If you want to own the software layer of the autonomous vehicle revolution, Uber and Tesla should top your watch list.

There's an old saying worth noting: During a gold rush, sell shovels. That way, you'll profit even if the rush only discovers fool's gold.

What are the shovels of the robotaxi revolution? The vehicles themselves. EV makers Rivian (RIVN -1.82%) and Lucid Group (LCID -0.54%) have positioned themselves accordingly.

Both companies have already signed deals with Uber to supply tens of thousands of robotaxis. Lucid and Rivian's long-standing tech-first approach finally appears to be paying off, even if total volumes remain low. Some industry insiders believe other robotaxi operators -- including Alphabet's Waymo, which cannot manufacture robotaxis in-house -- will add to the growing corporate-customer lists of Rivian and Lucid.

Of the four stocks on this list, Rivian and Uber intrigue me most. Uber's valuation is significantly more attractive than Tesla's. The company has a free cash flow yield of nearly 7.5%. Rivian, meanwhile, has more capital at its disposal than Lucid, with more-mature production capabilities -- a key benefit for robotaxi operators looking to build scale.

Investors should keep in mind that the robotaxi story won't play out over months. It will likely take years for Rivian, Uber, Lucid, and Tesla to accumulate meaningful revenue from their robotaxi exposure. But with a trillion-dollar opportunity, all four stocks have a clearly defined growth runway in robotaxis.

#3102. Prediction: These Will Be the Best-Performing Industrial Stocks of 2027

Infrastructure spending could fuel another year of strong growth.

By Jeff Siegel – Nov 20, 2026 at 9:45AM EST

The industrial sector could produce some of the market's biggest winners in 2027, particularly among companies supplying the equipment and infrastructure needed to meet America's growing electricity demand. Three companies I'd put near the top of that list are Quanta Services (PWR -2.69%), Eaton (ETN -1.75%), and Vertiv Holdings (VRT -4.57%).

Quanta Services is positioned to benefit from the construction of new transmission networks, while Eaton supplies the electrical equipment needed for those projects. Meanwhile, Vertiv stands to benefit as new AI data centers require increasingly sophisticated power management and cooling systems.

All three have substantial opportunities extending into the coming year, supported by existing orders and multiyear infrastructure investments. And while their valuations could limit returns, continued spending on these projects gives all three a credible chance of outperforming the broader market in 2027.

Quanta Services builds and maintains the infrastructure that delivers electricity and natural gas. Its work includes transmission lines, substations, pipelines, and other infrastructure needed to support the expansion of power demand.

In Q3 2026, Quanta reported revenue of $12.01 billion, up approximately 57% from $7.63 billion a year earlier. Adjusted earnings per share jumped 82%, from $3.33 to $6.06.

Meanwhile, its total backlog reached a record $63.7 billion, providing considerable visibility into future revenue. Management subsequently raised its full-year 2026 revenue guidance to between $41.3 billion and $41.6 billion, with adjusted earnings per share expected to reach $17.90 to $18.30.

Quanta's key advantage is its ability to handle large, complicated infrastructure projects. Utilities need additional transmission capacity, data centers require reliable electricity connections, and aging electrical systems need upgrades. These projects can take years to complete, potentially supporting revenue well beyond 2027.

The risks, however, include labor shortages, project delays, and a valuation that may already reflect substantial future growth. But Quanta's backlog and earnings momentum make it a very strong industrial stock play.

Eaton manufactures electrical equipment used to manage, distribute, and protect power. Its products are essential components in data centers, factories, commercial buildings, and utility infrastructure.

In Q3, revenue reached a record $8.9 billion, increasing 28% year over year. Organic revenue growth, which excludes acquisitions and currency fluctuations, reached 19%, and adjusted earnings per share climbed to $3.68, while segment operating margins reached a record 25.3%. Demand is also accelerating.

Eaton reported that its electrical businesses experienced a 46% year-over-year increase in total backlog. Meanwhile, rolling 12-month organic orders increased by 39% in Electrical Americas and 51% in Electrical Global. Those numbers suggest customers are continuing to invest heavily in electrical infrastructure.

Management expects full-year 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $13.70 to $13.80. The company is also expanding manufacturing capacity to meet growing demand.

Overall, Eaton's biggest advantage is its exposure to multiple industries. It benefits from data center construction, but also serves utilities, manufacturers, aerospace customers, and commercial facilities. That diversification could help sustain growth even if spending in one market slows.

As artificial intelligence drives demand for more powerful computing systems, data centers require increasingly sophisticated cooling and electrical equipment. Vertiv is benefiting directly from that spending.

Third-quarter revenue reached $4.31 billion, increasing 61% year over year. Organic revenue growth, which excludes acquisitions, reached 55%. Meanwhile, adjusted earnings per share jumped 78% to $2.21, while adjusted operating profit increased 83% to approximately $1.09 billion.

Organic orders more than doubled compared with the same quarter a year earlier, and the company also generated $652 million in adjusted free cash flow during the quarter, an increase of 50%.

Management subsequently raised its full-year 2026 revenue guidance to between $14.45 billion and $14.85 billion. Adjusted earnings per share are expected to reach $7.10 to $7.20, representing approximately 69% to 71% growth. Those numbers suggest Vertiv could enter 2027 with substantial momentum.

However, the stock carries considerable risk. Its rapid growth has attracted significant investor enthusiasm, and any slowdown in data center spending could pressure both earnings expectations and its valuation.

Of these three companies, Vertiv could deliver the strongest earnings growth in 2027. But I'd be careful about assuming that translates into the biggest stock gains.

Quanta's record backlog offers visibility into future infrastructure spending, while Eaton's diversified electrical business provides exposure to several growing markets. Vertiv has the fastest recent earnings growth but potentially the greatest sensitivity to shifting expectations around AI infrastructure spending.

My prediction is that all three will benefit from continued investment in electrical infrastructure during 2027. The biggest winner, however, may be the company that delivers earnings growth exceeding what Wall Street has already priced into its shares.

#3099. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#3098. Robinhood Markets vs. Nu: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Nu's forward P/E sits at 13.7 versus Robinhood's 38.3, yet the cheaper stock carries a different set of risks worth weighing.

By Erin Kennedy – Updated Nov 20, 2026 at 9:55AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Robinhood Markets ›

As digital transformation continues to sweep through the global financial landscape, investors are comparing Robinhood Markets (HOOD -1.09%) and Nu (NU -0.13%) to see which stock offers the best growth potential through 2027.

Robinhood disrupted the domestic brokerage industry by democratizing access to trading and cryptocurrency for millions of users. Nu is a dominant digital bank in Latin America, providing low-cost financial services to a massive, underserved population. Both companies represent a shift away from traditional banking toward mobile-first, technology-driven financial ecosystems.

Robinhood provides a multifaceted platform for retail investors, offering stocks, Bitcoin (BTC -3.67%), and digital banking services. It recently expanded into institutional-grade services and introduced a credit card to deepen its relationship with users. According to its latest annual report, the company relies on partners like Amazon (AMZN +0.37%) for cloud infrastructure and Morningstar (MORN +2.04%) for research data.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $5.1 billion, an increase of roughly 21.1%. The company reported net income of approximately $2 billion during this time frame. This performance reflects a significant stabilization in its business model compared to previous years when trading volumes were more volatile.

As of Robinhood's December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was about 1.3. This ratio measures how much debt a company uses relative to the value owned by shareholders. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was close to $1.6 billion. Free cash flow is the cash left over after a company pays for its operations and capital expenditures.

Nu operates as a digital-first bank, providing credit cards, personal loans, and savings accounts to millions of customers. It focuses on underbanked populations in Brazil, Mexico, and Colombia, using lower overhead costs to offer better rates than traditional incumbents. The company's rapid expansion has made it one of the largest financial institutions in its region.

In the 12 months ended June 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $19.4 billion, an increase of approximately 53.2%. Net income for the period was roughly $3.6 billion. This growth highlights the scale of the digital banking shift occurring across emerging markets as users move away from physical branch locations.

As of Nu's December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was about 0.5, and the current ratio was roughly 0.6. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was close to $3.5 billion, providing capital to fund further international expansion.

Robinhood Markets faces significant regulatory and legal uncertainty regarding its payment for order flow practices and the classification of its event contracts. These legal challenges, which include scrutiny from state gaming regulators, could impact its future revenue streams. Additionally, the company is highly dependent on third parties like Amazon for its underlying cloud technology infrastructure.

Nu faces risks associated with operating in emerging markets, where economic volatility and currency fluctuations can impact its reported results. The company must also manage credit risks as it expands its lending products to a broader customer base during different economic cycles. Since it operates as a bank, it is subject to rigorous capital requirements and oversight by various international financial regulators.

While Nu offers a significantly lower forward P/E multiple, Robinhood carries a higher premium that reflects its diverse expansion into different financial services and trading asset classes.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

The P/B ratio compares the stock price with the company's book value, which represents its assets minus its liabilities. Investors frequently use this metric to evaluate bank stocks. A lower P/B ratio indicates that a stock is less expensive relative to its paper value.

Neither of these stocks is something I'd normally invest in, because I'm a pretty conservative investor. Nu does not appeal to me given my past negative experiences with emerging market stocks (once bitten, twice shy). I don't find Robinhood attractive because at a previous publication I worked at, we rated brokerages. Robinhood was consistently low-scoring, and users were not shy about saying so. In particular, respondents reported that it has poor customer service. Contrast that with a competitor like Schwab (SCHW +0.53%), which has online chat (with real people), a phone line, and even physical branches. And while Robinhood may have pioneered commission-free trading, that's table stakes now.

Both of these companies went public in 2021. Let's see how their stocks have performed since:

Nu does not compare favorably here; its stock hasn't even kept pace with inflation. Robinhood has delivered a strong performance, but it's seen a fair amount of volatility along the way. Of course, past performance is no indication of future results. But if I had to choose between these two, my vote would be for Robinhood.

#3094. Is Kimberly-Clark Stock a Buy Now That the Kenvue Acquisition Is Finalized?

Cost savings may be a bigger benefit of the Kenvue acquisition than revenue growth.

By Jack Delaney – Nov 20, 2026 at 10:00AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Kimberly-Clark ›

Kimberly-Clark (KMB +2.71%) closed its acquisition of Kenvue, a consumer health company spun off from Johnson & Johnson (JNJ +1.41%), on Nov. 2.

The deal was valued at $48.7 billion, and Kimberly-Clark expects Kenvue to offer $2.1 billion in merger-related savings and revenue gains. That sounds good, but management still has to prove Kenvue is a value driver rather than just an expensive acquisition.

Kimberly-Clark has brands found in homes across the world, including Kleenex, Huggies, and Cottonelle. Adding Kenvue's product portfolio to its lineup seems like a strong fit, as those are also items people generally don't think twice about reaching for when they need them. Kenvue's offerings include the brands Tylenol, Neutrogena, Listerine, and Band-Aid.

"This transaction brings together two iconic American companies to create a combined portfolio of complementary products, including 10 billion-dollar brands, that touch nearly half the global population through every stage of life," Kimberly-Clark said in a press release.

Interestingly enough, the main appeal of the deal may not be sales contributions. While Kimberly-Clark expects $2.1 billion in synergies (reinvesting $300 million), only $500 million of that is incremental profit expected to be obtained within the first four years after the acquisition closed.

The bulk of the acquisition's value lies in cost savings, which could be worth $1.9 billion and could be achieved within the first three years after the acquisition. Compared to those revenue assumptions, lower costs and higher margins will be easier to spot in quarterly reports.

Everything looks good on paper, but there's a difference between projections and execution. Kenvue was spun off from Johnson & Johnson in 2023, allowing the legacy brand to focus more on high-growth areas.

Kenvue may ultimately be a better fit for Kimberly-Clark's portfolio than for Johnson & Johnson's. But there's no guarantee that the synergies Kimberly-Clark expects will take shape.

Also, as Kimberly-Clark shared, the $2.1 billion figure mentioned earlier will take a few years to reach. But over the next several quarters, we'll start to get early previews of how successful the acquisition may be through margins, cost savings, debt levels, and cash flow.

It's too early to say whether Kimberly-Clark is a buy based solely on the Kenvue acquisition. But as it stands, it's a solid business that has seemingly grown even stronger thanks to the new brands it just acquired. Plus, while shareholders wait, Kimberly-Clark pays out a dividend currently yielding 4.7%.

#3091. Here's the Average Social Security Benefit by Age From 62 to 80

The average benefit doesn't climb in a straight line, and the age you claim shapes far more than the headline number suggests.

By Kailey Hagen, CFP® – Nov 20, 2026 at 10:00AM EST

Social Security was designed to function as supplemental income. It replaces about 40% of your pre-retirement pay, a share that is well short of what most retirees need to cover their costs. But how much you get depends on individual factors.

The Social Security Administration (SSA) calculates your benefit based on your earnings across your 35 highest-earning years. Full retirement age (FRA) ranges from 66 to 67, depending on your birth year, and it's 67 for everyone born in 1960 or later. Your final check depends heavily on the age you choose to start collecting.

Here is the average monthly retired-worker benefit by age, as of December 2025, the most recent data available. These figures reflect the population of retirees at each age today, not the amount you're guaranteed if you claim at that age.

The age-70 average is roughly 59.7% higher than the age-62 average, a monthly gap of $850.28 or $10,203.36 per year. This climb occurs because claiming at 62, with a full retirement age of 67, results in a permanent 30% reduction. Waiting until 70 adds delayed retirement credits worth 8% for each year you wait past full retirement age.

After age 70, average benefits plateau and even start to decline. This is not a benefit cut for individuals. Older groups likely include more people who claimed early and locked in lower checks. Women also make up a larger share of the oldest groups because they live longer on average. They often have lower lifetime earnings due to the gender pay gap and time out of the workforce for caregiving.

Even at the age-70 peak, the average monthly benefit of $2,274.68 ($27,296.16 annually) leaves a large gap in a household budget. The average senior household spends $61,432 in 2024, according to the most recent Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data. This results in a $34,135.84 annual shortfall that retirees must cover through other means.

A 3.6% cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) will take effect in 2027. It will add about $82 a month on top of the age-70 average, though these snapshot figures don't yet include it. The most useful number on the table is the row that matches your planned claiming age, not the peak for 70-year-olds.

The ideal Social Security claiming age for you depends on your finances, life expectancy, and personal preferences. Those with short life expectancies often get a larger lifetime benefit by applying early, while those with average or above-average life expectancies may do better by delaying their application, possibly until age 70 when they qualify for their largest checks.

But if you cannot afford to delay Social Security and you're unable to work, claiming early could make more sense for you. If you don't want to sign up immediately at 62, you could wait a few months or a few years before applying. Every month you delay increases your checks slightly.

If you're married, talk your decision over with your spouse, as it may affect them, too. You cannot claim a spousal benefit on your partner's work record until they are already claiming retirement checks. If your goal is to maximize your household income, it can help to coordinate your claiming ages.

For more on how Social Security affects your retirement, see this guide.

#3087. Why Critical Metals Stock Plunged This Week

November is winding down, and temperatures are dropping -- as is investor interest in this rare-earth stock.

By Scott Levine – Nov 20, 2026 at 10:07AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Critical Metals ›

An updated development roadmap and heavy share dilution from a Greenland mining acquisition sent Critical Metals (CRML -3.88%) tumbling 28.7% this week, according to data provided by S&P Global Market Intelligence.

Critical Metals stock changed hands at $3.48 as of the close on Thursday, Nov. 19, down 28.7% from Friday, Nov. 13's close through Thursday, Nov. 19's close. Across those five sessions, the S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.00%) fell 5.1%, and the Nasdaq-100 was down 5.1%.

Critical Metals completed its acquisition of European Lithium on Nov. 5, taking full control of the Tanbreez rare-earth project in Greenland and raising its post-closing cash balance to $264.5 million. However, issuing roughly 90.5 million new shares to fund the transaction increased the total share count to 194.2 million, resulting in substantial dilution for existing equity holders. Pressure on the stock mounted after an analyst downgrade at peer USA Rare Earth (USAR +2.04%) triggered a broad retreat across rare-earth developers, leaving the stock down 84.1% (as of the close on Thursday, Nov. 19) from its 52-week high.

Critical Metals is a pre-revenue mineral development company advancing the Tanbreez rare-earth project in Greenland, with no commercial sales yet, meaning its value depends entirely on securing project financing and building mine infrastructure. The business aims to supply heavy rare-earth elements to Western industrial and defense customers looking to bypass concentrated global supply chains. But with initial ore extraction at Tanbreez targeted for late 2028 at the earliest, commercial revenues remain several years away.

Mining megaprojects demand huge upfront capital expenditures for environmental studies, site permitting, and refinery infrastructure. Without current revenue, failing to secure non-dilutive debt or government grants could force further equity raises, creating a high risk of permanent capital loss for existing shareholders. This week's updates flip the focus from acquiring the Tanbreez deposit to managing massive capital needs and share dilution before commercial production begins.

With no commercial revenue, it's difficult to apply a strict multiple comparison here, as early-stage mining developers trade on asset scale and development milestones rather than sales multiples. Critical Metals now has a market cap of $675.9 million, backed by $264.5 million in cash and short-term investments as of its post-merger update, providing initial liquidity to advance engineering work. The most critical event for investors to monitor is the definitive feasibility study expected in late 2027, which will establish the project's true economic parameters and long-term funding requirements.

Investors may find the analyst's downgrade of USA Rare Earth disheartening, but it's hardly a sufficient cause to trim -- or exit altogether -- their positions in Critical Metals. Analysts' opinions vary, and there are potential catalysts on the horizon -- such as the company's definitive feasibility study -- that are more important to monitor.

Similarly, the shareholder dilution resulting from the acquisition of European Lithium is surely unwelcome, but it may be worthwhile if the company succeeds in developing the Tanbreez project.

The swing in Critical Metals stock this week may be disappointing, but volatility like this is typical for growth stocks. For those who are losing sleep over the volatility, however, it may be more comfortable to hold a rare-earth ETF rather than Critical Metals directly.
