#3083. Ross (ROST) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Comparable sales rose 8% as new stores outpaced expectations.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 20, 2026 at 10:14AM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Ross Stores (ROST -3.38%) reported third quarter results that included double-digit sales growth and an expansion in operating margins. Management indicated that comparable store sales growth was primarily driven by increased customer transactions and foot traffic across both its Ross and dd's DISCOUNTS brands. The company reported that performance was broad-based across all geographic regions and major merchandise categories, with particular strength in cosmetics and home goods. The company completed its annual store expansion program, opening more locations than originally planned for the fiscal year. Management maintained its fourth quarter sales outlook while updating its full year earnings guidance to reflect year-to-date performance and anticipated holiday season trends.

Operator: Good afternoon, and welcome to the Ross Stores Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Release Conference Call. The call will begin with prepared comments by management, followed by a question-and-answer session. [Operator Instructions] Before we get started, on behalf of Ross Stores, I would like to note that the comments made on this call will contain forward-looking statements regarding expectations about future growth and financial results, including sales and earnings forecasts, new store openings, and other matters that are based on the company's current forecast of aspects of its future business. These forward-looking statements are subject to risks and uncertainties that could cause actual results to differ materially from historical performance or current expectations.

Risk factors are included in today's press release and in the company's fiscal 2025 Form 10-K and fiscal 2026 Form 10-Qs and 8-Ks on file with the SEC. And now I'd like to turn the call over to Jim Conroy, Chief Executive Officer.

James Conroy: Thank you, John, and good afternoon, everyone. Joining me on our call today are Michael Hartshorn, Group President and Chief Operating Officer; Bill Sheehan, Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer; and Connie Kao, Senior Vice President, Investor Relations. Before reviewing our results for the third quarter, I want to take a moment to recognize and thank our associates across the organization. Your passion, commitment, and hard work continue to help us drive very strong results. Our third quarter performance is a reflection of your efforts, and we are grateful for all that you do. I will now turn to our third quarter results. We delivered a very strong quarter from both a top and bottom line perspective.

Total sales were up 12%, driven by a robust 8% increase in comparable store sales. The strength of our comp store sales growth was particularly encouraging as we cycled a strong 7% growth last year. Sales were strongest early in the quarter, supported by a healthy back-to-school selling season, while unseasonably warm weather dampened fall selling late in the quarter. Despite the unfavorable weather, our 2-year stacked comparable store sales accelerated from 12% in the second quarter to 15% in the third quarter with solid double-digit trends in each month. We believe that the 15% 2-year stack in comp store growth underscores our ability to achieve strong results on a year-over-year basis and demonstrates the power of our growth flywheel.

Further, we continue to be pleased with the health of the comp sales increase. Consistent with the pattern we have seen over the past several quarters, customer traffic remained the primary driver of our strong comparable store sales growth, reinforcing the positive underlying trends in our business. The sustained growth in customer traffic reflects the progress we are making across multiple areas of the business. Let's begin with our marketing efforts. We have been focused on modernizing the Ross brand and ensuring it remains relevant to today's shopper. That includes reaching our core customer in ways that better align with how she spends her time, particularly across digital and social channels.

We're also working to make our messaging more meaningful by engaging in culturally relevant moments and exploring new opportunities and events that help introduce Ross to new audiences while strengthening our connection with existing shoppers. The early results have been encouraging, and we believe it is helping us tell the Ross story in a fresher and more compelling way. Our merchandise assortment continues to evolve in ways that we believe are resonating with customers. As our merchants identify more opportunities with both new and existing vendor partners, we are expanding our offerings across a broader range of brands, fashion, and price points while maintaining the great values our shoppers expect.

This is enabling us to attract new shoppers while staying true to the extremely strong value proposition that has been at the core of Ross throughout our history. We're also seeing the benefits of the work underway in our stores. Our teams have done an incredible job embracing and executing a number of operational and merchandising improvements designed to enhance the shopping experience. Stores are cleaner, easier to shop, and more productive, while front-end improvements have reduced checkout times. Collectively, these efforts are creating a more compelling and enjoyable shopping experience. Taken together, the progress we are making in marketing, merchandising, and the in-store experience are helping drive the sustained traffic growth we have seen in the business.

We are attracting new customers, bringing back lapsed customers, and seeing more frequent visits from our existing customers. Importantly, the gains are across a broad range of income levels and age groups, including younger shoppers, reinforcing our belief that the Ross brand and value proposition have broad appeal. Consistent with the trends we saw in recent quarters, the strong performance at Ross was broad-based across both merchandise categories and geographies. In the third quarter, cosmetics and home were our strongest businesses. By geography, we saw strength across all markets with the Midwest performing the best. dd's DISCOUNTS also delivered solid sales and experienced similar broad-based performance across merchandise areas and geographic regions. Turning to inventory.

Consolidated inventories at quarter end increased 13%, in line with the growth in total sales. Packaway represented 36% of total inventory, similar to last year. As we head into the holiday season, we have a broad assortment of great brands and values and the flexibility to capitalize on closeout opportunities that can further enhance our offerings and deliver even more value to our customers. During the third quarter, we opened 41 new Ross and 10 dd's DISCOUNTS stores as we continue to expand our presence in both new and existing markets.

We remain very optimistic about our store growth opportunity as performance in our newer markets, including the Northeast, continues to significantly exceed our expectations and further reinforces our confidence in the long-term potential of both brands. These openings completed our 2026 store growth program, reflecting 89 Ross and 26 dd's DISCOUNTS openings or 115 new locations in total, which is 5 more than our original plan at the outset of the year. We plan to close and/or relocate 2 locations in the fourth quarter and expect to end the year with 1,992 Ross stores and 383 dd's DISCOUNTS locations. I want to take a moment and recognize the outstanding efforts of our real estate and property development team.

Their hard work and strong execution were instrumental in delivering another successful year of store growth and the highest number of new stores in any year in the company's history. Overall, while we are encouraged by our progress, we believe we have many opportunities ahead of us. Many of our initiatives are still relatively new, and we see numerous avenues to further strengthen our assortment, deepen customer engagement, and enhance the shopping experience. The fourth quarter is off to a very solid start, and we remain optimistic about the path ahead. The consistency of our recent performance reinforces our confidence that these improvements are creating lasting benefits and that we will still have significant room to grow.

With the momentum we have built, the flexibility of our off-price business model, and a long runway for improvement across the organization, we believe Ross is well positioned to drive sustainable growth and continue gaining market share over time. Now Bill will provide further details on our third quarter results and additional color on our outlook for the fourth quarter.

William Sheehan: Thank you, Jim. Building on our strong first half, we reported very healthy sales and earnings results for the third quarter. Total sales for the period grew 12% to $6.3 billion with comparable store sales increasing 8%. As Jim mentioned earlier, consistent with the trend in the first half, the primary driver of the comp growth was an increase in the number of transactions. Gross margin for the third quarter improved by 70 basis points. Merchandise margin rose 20 basis points as the strong buying environment and the benefits of lapping tariff-related costs were partially offset by planned higher shrink as we cycled last year's favorable results.

Occupancy leveraged by 20 basis points on the strong comp performance, while distribution costs were 40 basis points lower given higher productivity and, to a lesser extent, as we anniversaried last year's tariff-related processing costs. In addition, buying costs leveraged by 15 basis points. Partially offsetting these benefits were higher freight costs, which deleveraged by 25 basis points from higher fuel prices. SG&A was flat versus last year despite the higher expenses as we continue to improve the in-store experience. Third quarter operating margin increased 70 basis points to 12.3% compared to 11.6% last year.

Third quarter net income was $606 million compared to $512 million last year, and earnings per share were $1.91 compared to $1.58 in the prior year, reflecting very solid double-digit earnings growth. Sales for the first 9 months of 2026 grew 15% to $18.5 billion, up from $16.1 billion in the prior year. Comparable store sales for the first 9-month period of 2026 were up 11% and earnings per share were $6.59 compared to $4.61 in the prior year. As a reminder, the 2026 year-to-date results include $253 million or approximately $0.60 in earnings per share of tariff refunds that were recognized in the second quarter. Now to our shareholder return activity.

As noted in today's release, we repurchased approximately 1.4 million shares during the quarter for an aggregate total cost of $319 million under the 2-year $2.55 billion authorization approved by our Board of Directors in March of this year. We remain on track to buy back a total of $1.275 billion in stock during 2026. In addition, the company's Board of Directors declared a regular quarterly cash dividend of $0.445 per share. Now let's discuss our outlook for the fourth quarter. As noted in today's press release, we are confident in our plans for the upcoming holiday season and are maintaining our sales outlook for the fourth quarter even as we face our most challenging sales comparison of the year.

Comparable store sales are forecasted to increase 4% to 5%, with earnings per share expected to be in the range of $2.16 to $2.25 versus $2 last year or growth of 8% to 13%. Our guidance assumptions for the fourth quarter of 2026 reflect total sales are forecast to increase 7% to 9% versus the prior year. If same-store sales perform in line with our forecast, operating margin for the fourth quarter is planned to be in the range of 12.1% to 12.4% compared to 12.3% last year. Our forecast reflects higher merchandise margins and favorability in buying costs, which are partially offset by higher freight costs and investments in stores and marketing to support the holiday selling season.

Net interest income is estimated to be approximately $37 million. The tax rate is projected to be about 24% and diluted shares outstanding are expected to be approximately 318 million. For the full year of 2026, our updated guidance for earnings per share is $8.75 to $8.84. As a reminder, this guidance includes an approximate $0.60 earnings per share benefit from tariff refunds recognized in the second quarter. Now I'll turn the call over to Jim for closing comments.

James Conroy: Thank you, Bill. We are very pleased with our year-to-date results and encouraged by the strength we see across the business. As we enter the holiday selling season, our focus remains on executing well and delivering great value for our customers. Based on our current outlook, we are on track to deliver one of the strongest years in the company's history with robust sales growth and record levels of earnings. Looking beyond this year, we continue to see significant opportunities to gain market share.

While we have made substantial progress, we are still in the early phases of many of our initiatives, and we believe there is still considerable room to build on our recent success and drive continued sales and earnings gains over the long term. At this point, we would like to open the call and respond to any questions that you may have. John?

Operator: [Operator Instructions] And the first question comes from the line of Matthew Boss with JPMorgan.

Matthew Boss: Congrats on a nice quarter. So Jim, with high single-digit comps against high singles a year ago, could you speak to drivers of the 2-year stacked acceleration relative to the second quarter and the front half? And then could you elaborate on initiatives that you're seeing in early innings today or if anything, that you're seeing building today rather than giving back? And just speak to the sales productivity opportunity that you see multiyear.

James Conroy: Sure. Great questions. Yes, we're really pleased, right? We just put up an 8% on top of a 7%. When we started -- when we saw the inflection point in the comps last year in this quarter, the question was, "Well, that's great. Are you ever going to be able to comp the comp?" And we had an entire conversation around the notion of comping the comp or starting a multiyear growth platform and continuing to roll forward with sort of the flywheel effect of gaining new customers, converting them in store, making the store experience better, enabling you to invest more in marketing and store labor, gaining more customers, et cetera. And that's what we've seen.

So we're really, really pleased that our first proof point of can you comp the comp should be a resounding yes. As we are now about to embark upon our fourth quarter or just maybe a couple of weeks ago, have embarked upon our fourth quarter, and we're cycling a plus 9%, and we're guiding a 4% to 5%, that should be a second yes to that same question of can you comp the comp? In terms of the initiatives, I could list probably 20 different things. I'll give you a flavor for a few. Let's start with marketing. We started changing our marketing last year around back-to-school. We were still very much learning and testing some different things.

Then we changed and brought our in-house -- brought our creative in-house in the spring. We started changing our media mix. We continue to make improvements in our media mix and how we are trying to prospect for new customers, regain lapsed customers, and increase frequency for current customers. But all those things that I've just spoken to from a marketing standpoint are very, very early. We've tested some things, learned some -- what has worked, and we'll continue to double down on those investments. And some things haven't worked as well, and we'll kind of redirect that money.

But we're still kind of climbing the learning curve there, and I think we will have an opportunity to continue to grow customer count for several quarters going forward. From a merchandising standpoint, we continue to open up new vendors, and new brands are becoming more appealing as a customer to lots of vendor partners. The merchants continue to knock on doors. Vendors that hadn't historically sold to us are now open to selling to us partly because of some of the great work the stores have done and their ability to showcase product better. We've done some really great things in terms of bringing together themes across the merchandise assortment across categories, connecting those themes to the marketing messages.

And then in-store, we started to experiment with redirecting or reallocating store labor dollars away from certain activities that aren't customer-facing and making the stores more productive by recovering inventory more quickly, doing some visual merchandising, shortening the queue line. But all of those things are still early innings in terms of our development. So when I think about the health of the comp, mostly transactions, much of those transactions are new customers or lapsed customers. Some of those are recaptured or customers shopping more frequently.

And to be able to have a really, really strong comp on top of last year's strong comp with an improvement in merchandise margin and solid growth in earnings, almost 20% growth in earnings per share, we're really quite pleased with how things have evolved.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Lorraine Hutchinson with Bank of America.

Lorraine Maikis: Jim, the investments you're making are clearly having an impact on the comp. Can you elaborate on the investment in stores and marketing that are ramping in the fourth quarter? And if we should expect outsized growth in any of these areas in '27?

James Conroy: Sure. I did use the word investments, that perhaps wasn't the best use of terminology for the third quarter. In the third quarter, we really found the money throughout the expense structure mostly to redirect into marketing and into store labor. In the fourth quarter, we're ticking up slightly in marketing and making some investments in store labor because when we saw what we did last year with both of those things, we just saw some really great kind of response in top line sales and in the customer experience. So we're going to do a little bit more of that this December as we go into the holiday season.

As we get into 2027, I think we're -- it's much too early to think about guidance or laying out the following year. We haven't gotten through Christmas and holidays this year. But I would expect we're going to revert back to at least our long-term algorithm, and we see no reason why we can't continue to grow and comp against this comp. I'm sure the market will continue to be concerned about that for quarters to come, including in the first quarter, and I see no reason why we can't put up a positive number in the first quarter.

So look, we're just really pleased with how the team has come together, how the different functions are working in sort of a very synchronized way, how the customers have responded, how we've captured tremendous amounts of market share over the last year. And we're looking forward to getting through holiday with another great quarter and starting out next year.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Michael Binetti with Evercore ISI.

Michael Binetti: Nice quarter. So unlike last quarter, just asking a near-term question quickly, guidance last quarter embedded an acceleration in the stacks, and you talked about the nice consistency in the double digits that you saw each month. You've gotten us used to thinking about upside opportunities here, Jim. So is there something in the 2-year stack deceleration in the fourth quarter in the guidance that we should think about? It seems fairly well discussed at this point that there's some weather and maybe election distraction early in the quarter. Or anything you're just seeing that we should -- that changes how you're thinking about the industry backdrop for the holiday, I guess?

And then I just wanted to orient around the comment you just made for Lorraine about reverting back to the long-term algorithm next year. Was that related to the long-term same-store sales algorithm or the 10 to 15 basis points per point of comp? Is that related to the flow-through that you guys usually point to on the point of sales upside?

James Conroy: Sure. On the first point, on the 2-year stack, it is funny. As we're thinking about the preparation for this call, we're like, all right, well, we guided this quarter to be a 2-year stack of 14% and we delivered a 2-year stack of 15%. We're guiding the fourth quarter to be a 2-year stack of 14%. I honestly didn't think that would raise any eyebrows in terms of a "deceleration" because the numbers are so absurdly high. I think people are sort of losing sight of the fact that deceleration to a measly 5% or a 2-year stack of 14%, I wouldn't read anything into that. We raised our guidance.

If you recall, we raised our fourth quarter comp guidance in the last call. Perhaps we should have held on to that. But I wouldn't read anything into a 2-year stack comp going from 15% to a guide of 14%. In terms of next year, Michael, do you want to take the next year?

Michael Hartshorn: Yes, I was just going to say, Michael, on the algorithm, obviously, after -- we have to get to the fourth quarter, but after a great year, we haven't changed our long-term algorithm, which is calling for 5% unit growth, 3% to 4% comp, and double-digit EPS growth. So we haven't changed it, and that's what he was referring to.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Brooke Roach with Goldman Sachs.

Brooke Roach: I wanted to follow up on your high-level thoughts as you think about continuing to drive growth alongside the algorithm. You're exiting the year close to a 13% operating margin, which is really nice to see the recovery in the year. How should we be thinking about the ceiling of the operating margin in this business? Is there a ceiling? Are prior peaks achievable for this business?

And then in the near term, how should we be thinking about the impact of some of the freight and fuel inflation that looks to be on the horizon, both in terms of the magnitude of the impact in the fourth quarter, but also any chance you can help us with the wraparound effects into 2027 and the various mitigation factors that you have at play to deliver that double-digit EPS growth despite some of those incremental cost headwinds?

Michael Hartshorn: Brooke, on the freight cost, a lot of what's driving the freight inflation right now is fuel. And for us, we were 25 basis points off last year, and much of that was fuel related. Clearly, we're seeing pressure in the market. I wouldn't guide next year at this point. But I think the long-term algorithm, we wouldn't change -- it wouldn't take down the overall EBIT growth. We would find other opportunities in the business. In terms of thinking about long-term EBIT, I think it's going to continue to be based on the model, based on store productivity. To the extent that we can comp between 3% and 4%, we'll grow EBIT from there.

And the more we can outcomp, the more we can grow EBIT.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Corey Tarlowe with Jefferies.

Corey Tarlowe: I just wanted to ask on the fourth quarter guide. It looks like the earnings were refined a little bit. Could you provide a little bit more color as to what drove that either from a gross margin or SG&A perspective?

William Sheehan: Sure. Happy to take that one. Obviously, we reiterated that 4% to 5% comp on top of that 9%. So we feel great about that. EPS, right, is in that range of $2.16 to $2.25, and that reflects higher fuel costs relative to our initial guidance, right? That EPS is benefiting slightly from some higher interest income. And then on the flow-through side, I think -- we think about within margin in Q4, we continue to plan for increase in merchant margin and lower buying costs, partially due to anniversarying higher incentives from last year. And then there is some, like we just mentioned, and I'll mention again, some higher domestic freight from an increase in fuel prices.

We expect kind of SG&A to delever a bit in the fourth quarter as we have some modestly higher store payroll and marketing to support our holiday priorities.

Corey Tarlowe: That's helpful. And then just on pricing, a number of competitors or retail peers have already reported, they've talked about lowering some prices in response to competition. Other companies have talked about higher rollbacks or higher discounts. Are you seeing any pressure as it relates to that? Or are you responding in kind to that?

James Conroy: We started out with maybe a different starting point, right? A lot of retailers over the last couple of years, particularly in light of tariffs, were increasing their retails and increasing AURs. And we sort of maintained some just really, really strong value pricing. I mean, so much so, right, that last year, we took a hit to earnings because we didn't want to raise prices and we want to keep our prices low for our customers, particularly in today's environment. So as we look across the landscape now, I think most of those retailers are probably honestly trying to revert back to where they were and maybe match our pricing.

That's how I would answer it at a macro level. On a category-by-category or product-by-product standpoint, all of the merchants know their assortments so extraordinarily well that they are constantly out competitive shopping. And we -- relative to the mainstream retail, we will always maintain that pricing umbrella so we can show a significant discount against mainstream retail. And against the other off-pricers, sometimes we're in line, sometimes we're slightly lower. But we still think we have the best values in sort of mainstream and off-price retailing. So it's -- that's how we view it. I don't think we've seen any competitive pressure from a pricing standpoint that's been hurting our business, right?

We just guided to plus 4% to 5% for the quarter. So we feel pretty good.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Simeon Siegel with Guggenheim.

Simeon Siegel: Another really nice job. Jim, just curious, how do you -- on the back of that, how do you view the competitive landscape between the full price and the off-price, anything you're seeing there? And just to your earlier point about the new vendors being more excited to work with you guys, any way to quantify the order of magnitude there? Is penetration of better and best changing at any noteworthy rate? And I guess you mentioned maybe like-for-like, but are you seeing any AUR from a mix dynamic there lifting?

James Conroy: Sure. On the first piece, at the highest level, off-price is still a share gainer. And the forfeitures are coming mostly from traditional department stores over multiple years now. We've seen some -- we talked about this on the last call, actually, some share forfeiture from some of the big e-commerce players, from some specialty stores. So off-price is still a growing retail segment. It still has tailwinds. We're pleased with our growth, right, we have got 12% growth for the quarter. We're certainly taking share in retail in general, and we believe within the off-price sector.

In terms of how to quantify sort of the vendor capture, et cetera, it's really difficult because sometimes we're opening up vendors that might stretch up, and that's where people generally believe it's happening. Other times, though, we just will be able to open up a vendor that had been reticent to sell into off-price or perhaps specifically into Ross. And they're just a very strong, solid national brand, but it might be a good price point or a better price point, but they hadn't sold to off-price in the past.

And as they enter the mix, we might sell a lot of their product, but it doesn't really have an impact on the AUR even as we kind of mix it in. So if you think about the 8% comp largely driven by transactions, again, we can't really underscore that enough. The health of the comp, a greater portion of it was due to transactions, and even in the prior quarters. So only a small portion of that was due to the size of the basket, and that's where the AUR piece would show up.

So we're not really looking to get sort of inflationary comp store sales growth, which gets us back to sort of the durability of the comp. It's not like we're buying it with higher price points or we're buying it by overinvesting in marketing or by lowering our merchandise margin. It's extremely healthy broad-based comp store sales growth.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Mark Altschwager with Baird.

Mark Altschwager: Just first, following up on merchandise margin. You're guiding to merch margin up in the fourth quarter. With some of the tariff lap shrinking and shrink running a bit higher, can you just speak a bit more to what's driving the improvement in merchandise margin here? And how much room do you have to invest in price if holiday were to get more promotional?

William Sheehan: I mean when we think about the merch margin, mainly what we're talking about from a guidance perspective, just the buying environment has been great. So that's driving merchant margin in general. There are some -- again, some lower buying costs that we're anniversarying. But on the merchant margin side, it's the continuation of what we've seen this year.

Michael Hartshorn: And then particularly on the quarter, we called out shrink. Shrink was because we were truing up -- last year, we had a favorable true-up, so we were up against it. The total cost of shrink in the company has been relatively flat.

Mark Altschwager: And then on the customer retention, for the new and the lapsed customers that you brought in, in the last year, 1.5 years, I guess, what is the second-year retention and trip frequency? How is that tracking relative to your core base? Just wondering if there are any metrics you can share there and just how that's supporting your confidence in the flywheel and the comping the comp.

James Conroy: I don't have a specific metric to share. When we look at that, what we're learning about the new customers coming into the business is they reflect much of the same demographics and household income and age as our current customers, and they shop with roughly the same frequency. So I think they're going to be ongoing customers added to the Ross brand. There's no reason to believe that they're going to be onetime shoppers and then sort of disappear.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Chuck Grom with Gordon Haskett.

Charles Grom: A lot of crosscurrents out there impacting consumers. I'm just curious, in recent weeks, have you observed any notable shifts in behavior across the country, either geographically, demographically? And is there any way to speak to how much you think weather and maybe what October did to the entire quarter comp?

James Conroy: Sure. There are a lot of crosscurrents, but I think we've sort of largely risen above it. The tail end of October, particularly the last week, was very rough weather. The heat, particularly in some of our biggest markets, had our sort of colder weather fall assortment off to a really slow start on the apparel side. But now we get into the fourth quarter, and we've seen a really, really nice step-up back to kind of in line with our guidance.

In terms of what has been impacting our business, I mean, from a day-to-day or week-to-week basis, extremely hot weather in a particular state that's different than last year or a tremendous amount of rain in a particular state that's different than last year. But our customer still feels loyal to us and pretty resilient. So I think that you can see that embedded in how we guided the quarter. We are cognizant of the fact that there's a lot of inflationary pressure, consumer confidence is extremely low.

So we just continue to try to underscore that we want to have the best values in the store, and the merchants have done just an incredible job of maintaining great values, anchoring into our core customer, and now as we get into December, really making the store a gift-giving destination. So I'm really, really excited to see how the next several weeks unfold because we have kind of another year under our belt with the 2 chief merchants in place, and each of them have done a really nice job of getting their teams and the assortments ready for prepackaged gifts and gifts across the store, common themes across categories.

I'd encourage everybody to go to a store, you'll see it. We are really poised to have a very strong holiday season.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Alex Straton with Morgan Stanley.

Alexandra Straton: Congrats on a great quarter. Maybe, Jim, just a follow-up on one of your prior answers. Since you started, comp has been driven mostly by transactions, which is great. But as you start to lap that, do you view that as still being the case? Or should there also be a basket opportunity ahead? And then separately, you mentioned a lot of similarities in the new customers you're acquiring or the reactivated. But are you seeing any differences that we should be aware of and how we should think about those?

James Conroy: Sure. On the second part, just to tick that off, the differences are minor and not worth even calling out. And we have a group that looks at all the customer information. They can tease out all sorts of insights. But the macro story is we're attracting more customers that look like our current customers. On the first part of your question around transactions and traffic, et cetera, I've always believed that the best way to drive your business forward is to rely on customer traffic. And our first part of our strategy was to prospect for new customers. We've done a lot of that.

You can add to that as you go forward, how do you get these customers to shop more frequently than they otherwise would? We've got a number of initiatives in place not only in marketing, but really in merchandising to encourage more frequent repeat purchases, and we've seen that tick up a little bit. That's another one of those things that while we feel really good about the progress so far, we have far from perfected what we would be doing going forward. If we turned our attention to the size of the basket, the place I would go to is how do I get a customer to shop and buy more units per transaction?

I just don't see raising AUR to get a short-term growth in same-store sales that is transitory and likely erodes your value proposition of the brand going forward. It's relatively easy to do. It can appease shareholders for a quarter or 2 by getting inflationary comp growth, but it's just not durable. So if you went back through our last several quarters and kind of summarize what we've been saying, it's been health of the comp, driven by customers, driven by transactions, getting them into the store.

And yes, as we go forward into next year, we are looking for opportunities to expand the number of units that they buy, but we really like to keep our prices as low as possible to deliver the absolute best values, particularly given what customers are facing today.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Adrienne Yih with Barclays.

Adrienne Yih-Tennant: Congrats on the momentum. My question is on kind of category commentary. Can you talk about the performance of ladies apparel versus home versus beauty? And then are the new merchants overindexing any of those categories from missed opportunities last year? And then more importantly, go forward, what are the opportunities that you see in each of those categories? And then my second one for Bill is we've been in a period of sort of SG&A growing in line with sales, heavier investment in payroll, et cetera, which has done great for the top line.

But just wondering, at what point do we see maybe next year, what's the philosophy on spending up to the growth in sales or allowing it to flow to the bottom line?

James Conroy: I can start. On the first part about category performance, there are some pieces of that question that we would view as pretty competitive information. But I'll give you a sense of what's happening from a category-by-category perspective. We called out home and cosmetics. That shouldn't be a tremendous surprise, right? Cosmetics has been strong for several quarters in a row now. The home business has come on strongly over the last few quarters as well. And given some of our commentary and all the commentary in the retail industry right now about the difficult weather in the tail end of Q3, a lot of the apparel businesses were under pressure.

We're happy to report that every single merchandise category, every single major merchandise category comped positive in the third quarter. So despite that pressure, we still had strength across every single business across the entire company, including the apparel business and including ladies. The ladies business is slightly less strong than it was in the prior quarter. It's also up against a very strong quarter last year in the third quarter. So we feel pretty strongly that, that business will have a decent fourth quarter. One of the things, and I know you recognize, so one of the things about off-price is we've got the ability to shift our businesses around.

And what the merchant team and the planning team has done really nicely is chase into businesses that were growing and downtrend some other businesses. We anticipated some of the weather problems. So they did a really nice job of positioning their assortment. So we were able to get growth in the third quarter across all of our apparel categories, which I think would be something that's pretty unusual in the business right now.

Michael Hartshorn: Adrienne, it's Michael. On SG&A, as we think about SG&A, first of all, any investment would be in the S and not the G. So what we've done over the past year, we really started in the fourth quarter last year. We have a pretty mature test and learn technology that allows us to test a variety of different either payroll investments, reallocation of payroll. And to the extent that we find in that testing environment that it drives top line growth, we'll roll it out to the whole chain. And we'll continue that going into next year and beyond.

If we find something that works in that testing environment, we're not going to be afraid to invest in the store environment to drive top line growth.

Operator: And the next question comes from Dana Telsey with the Telsey Advisory Group.

Dana Telsey: Congratulations and nice to see the progress. Given you just gave the breakdown on what you saw by category, anything else on region? Because Midwest has been the strongest for a while. And speaking of that, saw the article in Crain's the other day that you're entering New York City in the former Old Navy spot in Chelsea. How are your Northeast stores doing relative to your expectations for cost, volume, and profitability?

Michael Hartshorn: Dana, on the geographic performance, as you mentioned, the Midwest was the strongest performer, all regions. So it's pretty broad-based geographically. All regions had a solid mid-single-digit comp growth or better. In terms of larger markets, Texas came in line with the chain around an 8% comp. California and Florida were slightly below. California performance was certainly hindered late in the quarter with unseasonably warm weather. On new stores, we're happy with the whole group of stores this year. We came into the year, our planning assumption was that the average new store volume in its first year would be about 70% to 75% of an average store in the chain.

That was elevated over previous years where we're at 60% to 65%, and you could deduce from the numbers that we're running ahead of that. On the Northeast stores, we're very happy with the stores that we've opened. We had 12 coming into the year in the Northeast region. We added 23 locations and some of the higher volume stores, and as a group have significantly exceeded our pro forma estimates. So we feel really good and excited about the opportunity to expand growth there.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Krisztina Katai with Deutsche Bank.

Krisztina Katai: Congrats on another great quarter. I wanted to ask, you talked about the buying environment continuing to support merchandise margin. So if we're looking ahead into a more normalized sort of long-term algo, 3% to 4% next year, how should we think about the opportunity for merchandise margin? Can that still be a source of EBIT expansion? Or does some of the benefit that you're seeing today depend on the stronger sales environment?

William Sheehan: I think, obviously, we're not providing any guidance on the go forward. But in general, right, like we talk about, we think we can get improvement across the P&L with that 3% to 4% comp growth. So sometimes that could come from merchant margin, that could come from other places in the P&L, but we continue to think there's opportunity there.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of John Kernan with Cantor.

John Kernan: Congrats on another great quarter. Wanted to go back to some of the new store openings, particularly in the newer regions you spoke of earlier. One thing that's been evident really since the comps accelerated, it's also been the acceleration in new store productivity, at least by the way we calculate it. How durable is this level of new store productivity just nearing 100% into next year and as you go into some of these new markets?

Michael Hartshorn: Well, on the new store productivity, much of the improvement are the same improvements that you're seeing in the rest of the chain. So if we're making -- one example is in new stores, and we tested this and continue to roll it out in existing stores, an example is, for instance, self-checkout. So we're putting self-checkout in new stores. It's helping in the front end. It's helping the customer experience. But that's an example of things we're doing in existing stores that's also helping the new store growth. The other thing I would say, it somewhat depends on the regionality, the locations you're opening, whether the population density, the group of new stores.

We are seeing the Northeast where there is much more population density than maybe the Midwest regions, we're seeing higher volumes there. But we would continue to plan at this point in the long-term algorithm of new stores at 70% to 75%, and we hope to beat it.

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Ike Boruchow with Wells Fargo.

Irwin Boruchow: Michael, I think you touched on this earlier, but I wanted to go back. Could you unpack the planned shrink headwind that you guys talked about recognizing in the third quarter? I guess, is that primarily a comparison against unusually favorable shrink last year? Or does it reflect higher underlying shrink rate? And I guess, to what extent, if any, is that related to the inventory build you guys have been doing the past 12 months to drive those comps? Just kind of would be helpful to understand the basis point impact in the quarter and whether you expect the pressure to persist if it's more onetime.

Michael Hartshorn: Sure, Ike. Last year, in the third quarter, our physical inventory results were much lower than our shrink accrual. So we had a favorable true-up that helped the third quarter last year. So the unfavorability this year was only that comparison. In terms of total cost of shrink, that has been relatively constant. And so therefore, the higher inventory hasn't driven a total cost -- an increase in the total cost of shrink.

Irwin Boruchow: Got it. So it's not a go-forward issue or headwind that we need to think about?

Operator: And the next question comes from the line of Jay Sole with UBS.

Jay Sole: Terrific. Jim, just want to take a step back and make sure I understand the strategy. I think I heard you say you're going after better brands with your merchandising strategy. You want to grow your customer count. Does that mean you're looking to go upmarket, so to speak, or go after more middle-income consumers? Because at the same time, I thought I heard you say you're getting customers that look like your current customers. So I guess just how are you balancing just the wish to acquire new customers versus the wish to continue to serve the core customer?

And at the same time, as you get better brands, I heard you very clearly say that trying to go after AUR is not a durable strategy, but how do you get better brands and not raise AUR? Presumably those better brands are higher-quality brands, you're going to charge more for them. So if you can kind of just unpack that for us a little bit, that would be helpful.

James Conroy: Sure. In the world of retail, everyone talks about good, better, and best. And when we say better, we could just be getting a solid national brand. It can be a relatively low-priced brand or a low-price category, but that wouldn't necessarily sell to us 2 years ago or maybe not to off-price in general. So as we sit around every week and every month talking to the merchants, what we're celebrating is, "Hey, we've just opened up a new vendor." Sometimes there are higher price points, but not always. And you can see it reflected, right?

We don't comment specifically about the AUR, but you can deduce that if our 8% comp growth was mostly transactions, leaving only a small piece left over for the basket, the basket is only up modestly. So we've really, really paid attention to keeping our AUR low. In terms of bringing customers in, there's a lot of natural hypotheses that people have put forth, and they're all understandable. What the data tell us is that the customer growth across income levels is extremely consistent. So there's a philosophy that we're going up and we're getting higher-income customers, they're trading down into off-price. That's where the growth is coming from. There's a thesis out there that low-price retailers are winning.

So maybe the lower income customers are growing. What we're seeing across all customer cohorts is very solid growth, and it's extremely consistent across incomes, extremely consistent. We haven't -- I know it's been written about and people want to believe that we're trying to make Ross reach higher up. It's just not true. We absolutely don't want to abandon the core modest income customer that's made this business successful for 40 years. We'd like to get more of them. And as we bring more of them in, they're looking very much like our legacy customer from a composition standpoint.

Operator: And our final question comes from the line of Aneesha Sherman with Bernstein.

Aneesha Sherman: I have 2 follow-ups, please, on previous comments. So on inventories, Jim, you talked about growing the inventory and not seeing an offset in shrink. You've grown inventories double digits this year and filled up the stores with new product, which has clearly benefited comp. Do you feel like you're at a good balance now between filling up the stores versus maintaining that healthy margin? Or do you think inventory could continue to be a lever you can pull beyond this year as well? And then a second follow-up on a comment you made about weather.

I think you said you're now, as the weather is starting to get colder, you're seeing a step-up back to in line with the guide. Is it right to say then that your exit rate out of Q3 was kind of running below your Q4 guide and now that the weather has gotten colder, you're running in line with your Q4 guide?

James Conroy: Sure. On the inventory piece, our inventory and our sales growth are kind of in line, right? Our inventory is up 13%. Our sales for the quarter were up 12%. So we don't really view that as sort of out of line. In terms of is there more opportunity to do different things with inventory going forward? It's a constant conversation, right? What's the right amount of inventory to have? How does that vary by high-volume stores, low-volume stores? But we continue to turn extraordinarily fast, which is why our merchandise margin quarter after quarter has continued to grow.

In terms of parsing out specific week-to-week changes in our comp store sales growth, what I said was the fourth week of October was a difficult week. So yes, that particular week was lower than our current fourth quarter-to-date. I think that's true for every retailer under the sun. It was extremely warm in California. Halloween shifted from a Friday to a Saturday, which is actually a very meaningful shift for a 1-week period of time. But when we look back and say that every single month of the third quarter was a double-digit comp on a 2-year stack basis, but we try to remain humble. I'm not sure there's another retailer out there that can say that.

Operator: And at this time, that is the end of the question-and-answer session. And I would like to turn the call back over to Jim Conroy for any closing remarks.

James Conroy: Very good. Thank you, everyone, for joining us today. We look forward to speaking with you on our next earnings call. Wishing you and yours a happy holiday season. Take care.

Operator: Ladies and gentlemen, thank you for your participation. This does conclude today's teleconference. Please disconnect your lines, and have a wonderful day.

#3079. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#3078. Keysight (KEYS) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Record orders and AI demand carried fiscal 2026 results past expectations.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 20, 2026 at 10:14AM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Keysight (KEYS -1.01%) reported fiscal year 2026 revenue of $7.157 billion and record orders of $8.126 billion. Management stated that growth was broad-based across all end markets, noting significant demand in wireline applications for artificial intelligence infrastructure and defense programs. The company reported sequential revenue increases every quarter while expanding manufacturing capacity to meet order levels. Management indicated that the company enters fiscal year 2027 with a record backlog and a pipeline of opportunities driven by new technology standards in the wireless and semiconductor sectors.

Operator: Good day, ladies and gentlemen, and welcome to Keysight Technologies' Fiscal Fourth Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. My name is Hilary, and I will be your lead operator today. [Operator Instructions] This call is being recorded today, Thursday, November 19, 2026, at 1:30 Pacific Time. I would now like to hand the call over to Liz Morali, Vice President of Investor Relations. Please go ahead, Ms. Morali.

Liz Morali: Good afternoon, and thank you for joining us for Keysight's fourth quarter earnings conference call for fiscal year 2026. Joining me on today's call are Satish Dhanasekaran, President and CEO; Neil Dougherty, Executive Vice President and CFO; Kailash Narayanan, President of the Communications Solutions Group; Jason Kary, President of the Electronic Industrial Solutions Group; and Steve Yoon, Senior Vice President of Global Sales. Following the prepared remarks from Satish and Neil, we will conduct a question-and-answer session. The press release and information to supplement today's discussion can be found on our Investor Relations website, investor.keysight.com. During today's discussion, we will make forward-looking statements about the financial performance of the company.

Actual results may differ materially from those mentioned in these forward-looking statements as a result of risks and uncertainties. Information about these risks and uncertainties can be found in our most recent Forms 10-K and 10-Q filings with the SEC. We do not intend to update any forward-looking statements. In addition, we will refer to non-GAAP financial measures and reference core growth, which excludes the impact of acquisitions or divestitures completed within the last 12 months and currency movements. The most directly comparable GAAP financial metrics and reconciliations can be found on our Investor Relations website, and all comparisons are on a year-over-year basis unless otherwise noted. I will now turn the call over to Satish.

Satish Dhanasekaran: Thank you, Liz. Good afternoon, everyone, and thank you for joining us today. Fiscal 2026 was the strongest year in Keysight's history. We delivered a record fourth quarter, exceeding the high end of our guidance with orders up 53% and revenue up 41%. For the full year, orders surpassed $8 billion and revenue exceeded $7 billion with broad-based growth across all our end markets. Earnings per share grew 64% to $11.72, nearly twice our revenue growth. We enter fiscal 2027 with a record backlog and a robust pipeline of opportunities. These results validate our strategy, our operating model and reaffirm our long-term track record of executing and delivering on our commitments.

Businesses today are facing increasingly demanding engineering challenges as new technologies reshape industries and raise performance expectations. Our customers rely on Keysight as a trusted innovation partner. We uniquely provide design, emulation and test solutions that enable engineers to advance new technologies from design and development through launch. And with the pace of innovation accelerating, our opportunity is only expanding. Keysight's disciplined early investments are enabling us to capture that opportunity and deliver first-to-market solutions. Over the past several years, we have invested in portfolio differentiation and long-term growth, made acquisitions that allow us to offer customers more complete solutions and strengthened our operations end to end.

This year, we launched the largest refresh of our core high-performance measurement tools and deepened our collaborations with industry-leading companies on next-generation technologies. The strength of these customer relationships and the pipeline of new products will sustain our momentum for years to come. Customer demand continues to build with fourth quarter orders setting a new record. As we shared earlier this year, we took a long-term view of supply and initiated strategic actions to scale our operations to meet increasing demand. These include broadening our supply base, building longer-term partnerships and adding manufacturing capacity. As a result, we grew revenue sequentially every quarter, and we begin the new year well positioned for continued value creation. Now on to business segments.

Communications Solutions delivered a record year with full year orders up strong double digits. Fourth quarter orders grew for the 10th consecutive quarter to a new record, and revenue grew 51%. In commercial communications, the rapid development of AI infrastructure technologies continue to drive our wireline business. Wireline delivered record orders again this quarter with strong growth in both R&D and manufacturing for electrical and optical applications. Demand was broad-based across the ecosystem, including chip and silicon designers, interconnect and switch makers, transceiver and module manufacturers, and hyperscalers themselves. As I discussed last quarter, AI infrastructure is evolving into a broader, more heterogeneous environment.

AI compute spans the wide range of performance and power points from the most advanced GPUs to purpose-built inference processors. Racks and clusters are designed in modular ways with components and interconnects from a diverse supply chain. More protocols are being adopted at every layer of the stack, and agentic and inference workloads are adding new requirements. That widens our opportunity across the full AI life cycle from silicon design through system emulation into high-value manufacturing. Let me share a few examples from this quarter. First, chip and silicon designers used our high-speed digital and protocol solutions to validate next-generation switching and accelerator silicon, including the first PCIe 7.0 designs, so they can prove interoperability before tape out.

Second, interconnect and switch makers used our analyzers and newly introduced backplane test solutions to characterize 1.6T copper and optical links, so they can ship with confidence in signal integrity and yield. Third, transceiver and module manufacturers scaled production of 800 gig and 1.6T modules on our digital communication analyzers, so they can ramp volume without sacrificing quality. And fourth, hyperscalers used our workload emulators to validate scale-up, scale-out and scale-across fabrics before deployment. We also advanced our collaboration with industry leaders, including Cisco on scale-across networks and Broadcom on industry's first multivendor Ethernet scale-up interoperability demonstration at OCP.

Looking ahead, we continue to see 4 key pillars of opportunity over the long term: AI infrastructure scaling, speed transitions, optical and photonics technologies, and system-level emulation. Today, the industry is in early innings of 1.6T ramp with 3.2T already in active R&D. Co-packaged and near-packaged optics add an incremental opportunity on top of those transitions with meaningful revenue already realized and significant growth opportunities ahead. And as the new AI architectures emerge, demand for system-level emulation will continue to rise, our first-to-market solutions, deep customer collaborations and continued investment across all 4 pillars give Keysight a strong position to capture this growth. Turning to wireless.

Orders grew double digits this quarter, driven by early 6G development, nonterrestrial networks and demand from wireless supply chain as it supports AI infrastructure scaling. First in 6G, the standardization time line remains on track with 3GPP Release 21 expected in 2029. Leading chipset and network equipment manufacturers are expanding from research into funded development. Investments in AI-RAN and integrated sensing and communication continues to grow. Keysight joined the Verizon 6G Forum as its independent design, emulation and test partner as we enable 6G over-the-air research at UC Berkeley. Second, nonterrestrial networks are becoming a new architectural layer ahead of 6G as LEO constellation scale and direct-to-cell services near launch.

We have built our leadership in this ecosystem from early 5G end-to-end NTN connection with Qualcomm and MediaTek to industry's first NR-NTN multiorbit handover with KT SAT. With Samsung, we validated satellite-to-satellite mobility on modem silicon and tested NR-NTN devices aligned with planned direct-to-cell deployments, including Starlink. Building on that work this quarter, Korea Testing Laboratory selected our solutions to certify satellite-enabled devices. Third, chipset makers, interconnect suppliers and contract manufacturers across the wireless ecosystem are adding capabilities to serve the AI infrastructure build-out. We're enabling them through our high-speed interconnect, AI server board and optical interconnect silicon test solutions. As the engineering complexity of wireless networks grow, so does the opportunity for our solutions.

In aerospace and defense and government, orders grew strong double digits with growth across all regions as record defense budgets translated into new programs and production. In the U.S., long-term contracts are enabling the primes to move new programs into production and add test capacity. In Europe, governments are investing in sovereign capabilities, including space and satellite as well as electromagnetic spectrum operations and radar, and a new generation of venture-backed defense companies is bringing commercial speed to autonomous systems, satellites and counter-UAV defense technologies. Our electromagnetic spectrum operations business was the highlight of the year. This quarter, a leading U.S. prime contractor selected our threat emulation platform for a major program and U.S.

Navy broadened its use of the platform to validate next-generation systems. In radar, counter-UAV detection and antenna characterization drove demand for our radar scene emulation and over-the-air test systems. We also joined Lockheed Martin, Verizon and NVIDIA to demonstrate AI-enabled airspace protection against UAVs over commercial 5G spectrum. Beyond defense, the FAA will deploy our handheld analyzers at more than 100 sites nationwide to modernize its radar infrastructure. And our positioning, navigation and timing portfolio from Spirent grew strongly this year as customers design systems that stay resilient to GPS jamming and spoofing. As programs move into production and modernization continues, we see a long runway of growth ahead.

Moving to Electronic Industrial Solutions Group, we also delivered a record year with full year orders up strong double digits. Fourth quarter orders grew for the sixth consecutive quarter and were up significantly in each of our 3 markets, while revenue grew 16%. In general electronics, orders grew double digits, led once again by AI-related investments across the electronic supply chain. The impact of AI on this business is broadening. PCB manufacturers adding high-frequency test capacity for higher speeds drove growth this year, and that demand continued. Alongside it, the component test demand we noted last quarter gained traction led by multilayer ceramic capacitors and photodiodes for near-packaged optics.

Looking further out, customers are engaging us early on their road maps for interposers that will enable co-packaged optics. In education, demand for our semiconductor workforce development solutions grew as funded programs expanded and our university engagements extended into next-generation wireless research as the industry moves from 5G to 6G. Heading to fiscal 2027, the diversity of this business is growing as AI drives architectural upgrades across the stack. In semiconductor, orders grew double digits, driven by capacity expansion for advanced nodes, high-bandwidth memory and silicon photonics as the industry races to scale through 2030. Leading foundry and memory makers in Asia, the United States and Europe expanded wafer test capacity with Keysight throughout the year.

The most important development is in silicon photonics. As optical interconnects move into data center at scale, leading foundries are taking silicon photonics into commercial production and engaging us earlier on their long-term road maps for near-packaged and co-packaged optics. These programs require optical validation alongside electrical parametric test we already provide. Early engagement keeps us in lockstep with our customers' capacity plans. Finally, in automotive and energy, orders grew double digits with growth across software-defined vehicle, electric vehicles and manufacturing applications. In software-defined vehicles, semiconductor and OEM customers expanded their use of design and development solutions, and makers of automotive chipsets are adopting high-speed digital test as vehicle architectures move to higher data rates.

Electric vehicle and energy grew as OEMs and utilities invested in charging, grid and power test, including the new requirements of megawatt charging. An independent UL Solutions report confirmed that our automated grid-interconnection test matches the traditional method while cutting test time from weeks to under a day. Looking ahead, the trends associated with software-defined vehicle and energy infrastructure investments remain strong. As we look into fiscal 2027, the growth initiatives we have invested in over the past few years are meeting the inflecting market opportunities. Every step up in frequency, bandwidth, data rates and protocols creates more complexity for customers and more demand for Keysight.

We have a clear set of priorities for the year ahead: first, deliver growth and outperform our markets; second, invest to realize long-term opportunities for differentiation from AI infrastructure today to 6G and defense in the years ahead; and third, remain disciplined in how we allocate capital, delivering strong incremental margins and compounding long-term value for shareholders. Based on our visibility into demand and supply through the first half of the year, our base case for fiscal 2027 is 20% revenue growth. The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced an enforcement action against American Express Company to address, among other things, the firm's failure to sufficiently detect and report certain suspicious activity related to money laundering.

The Board also identified significant deficiencies in how American Express Company's enterprise-wide anti-money laundering program was implemented, in particular at the firm's subsidiary national bank. The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency, the primary federal regulator of the firm's national bank, has issued a separate enforcement action against the bank.

The Board's action will help ensure that the firm operates in compliance with all U.S. laws and regulations.
