#3027. Timberwolves star Anthony Edwards speaks on decision to undergo knee surgery, has no clear return timeline: 'Whenever my knee tells me it's ready, then I'll be ready'

Three weeks after undergoing arthroscopic surgery on his ailing knee, Minnesota Timberwolves star Anthony Edwards is speaking out about his recovery.

On Thursday, the guard talked to reporters for the first time since the procedure, saying that the rehabilitation process was "going great" and that he was focused on improving the strength in his leg post-surgery. Edwards also spoke about the timeline of his injury before deciding to go under the knife, saying that his knee had been bothering him on and off for years but that it had "flared up" significantly last month.

"It was frustrating, but I realized I had to do it, so I just made my mind up that same day, like we're gonna do it tomorrow," Edwards said.

The 25-year-old guard said that he had managed his knee well in the offseason and that it was still "good" in camp. But by the Timberwolves' season opener on Oct. 21, it was getting worse, and was "really bothering" him in the team's win over the Orlando Magic on Oct. 23.

Minnesota's back-to-back on Oct. 25 and Oct. 26, against the Toronto Raptors at home and the Cleveland Cavaliers away, is when it really started to be a problem: "After the back-to-back it was really bad, like painful to fly," Edwards said.

Edwards said that he hopes that the surgery will help mitigate the pain going forward.

"It was something that I needed, it needed to be done in order for me to be able to play without any pain for the next couple years," Edwards said. "But who knows, maybe in a couple years I might have to get something else, but hopefully not. I think I'll be pretty good."

Edwards, who said that this is the longest he's ever gone without playing basketball, added that he plans on coming back this season but doesn't have a clear timeline. At the time of his surgery, reports indicated that he would need more than two months to recover.

"I have no clue. Whenever my knee tells me it's ready, then I'll be ready," he said of a return to the court.

Losing Edwards initially seemed to be a brutal blow to the Timberwolves' chances: Before the surgery, Edwards was averaging 33.3 points per game and seemed on track for another dominant season. But Minnesota has fared well in his absence, going 8-3 in the weeks since Edwards' surgery.

"I was thirsty to get back, especially after the first game," Edwards said, referring to the Timberwolves' loss to the Golden State Warriors right after his procedure. "I was ready to get back and just get in their ear, tell them what I'm seeing, feel the energy. But after that game, we kinda took off, so I was like, I'll take my time. But I'm happy to be around now."

While speaking to reporters, Edwards had high praise for all of his teammates, specifically pointing out the "phenomenal" play of Ayo Dosunmu — who currently leads the team in points — and noting that forward Jaden McDaniels is "stepping up big time." He also confirmed that he's been sharing his scouting reports with his teammates, staying involved in the team despite his absence from the court.

Led by Dosunmu and LaMelo Ball, Minnesota is currently fourth in the Western Conference with a 11-4 record and a pretty favorable schedule over the next few weeks.

#3023. Spurs guard Dylan Harper reportedly expected to miss up to 3 weeks with left rotator hip strain

San Antonio Spurs guard Dylan Harper has reportedly been diagnosed with a left hip rotator strain, ESPN's Shams Charania reports.

The recovery timetable for that injury is one to three weeks, depending on how Harper's rehabilitation progresses, according to Charania.

Harper, 20, sustained the injury during the Spurs' 105-93 loss to the Detroit Pistons on Tuesday. In the closing minute of the fourth quarter, Harper was about to receive a pass from Stephon Castle while matched up against Detroit's Ausar Thompson.

However, Harper's right foot slipped on the court and caused him to stretch awkwardly. He stayed down on the floor while play continued on the other end of the court, appearing to grab at his left hip or groin area.

The second-year guard was eventually able to stand up and walk off the court with some assistance from teammate Keldon Johnson. But he was noticeably limping and in pain as he went to the locker room. Harper did not return to the game during the final 53 seconds.

"It just looked like he slipped, obviously," Spurs coach Mitch Johnson told reporters afterward, via the San Antonio Express-News' Tom Orsborn. "So hoping for the best, but don't know anything yet."

San Antonio Spurs guard Dylan Harper slipped and suffered a hip injury late in Tuesday's loss to the Detroit Pistons.

Harper appeared at Thursday's shootaround with no noticeable limp, according to Orsborn. But that was apparently before imaging results came back from the team's medical staff.

"I'm happy he's not as hurt as he could be," said Spurs forward Julian Champagnie, who will replace Harper in the starting lineup. "Obviously, we need him, so finding out it wasn't that serious, or as serious as it could have been, was good news."

In 15 games this season, Harper is scoring 18.4 points per game, second on San Antonio behind Victor Wembanyama. He's also averaging 5.4 rebounds and 4.5 assists. He's shooting 50% from the floor and 31% on 54 3-point attempts.

At 12-3, the Spurs are a half-game behind the Los Angeles Lakers and Philadelphia 76ers for the NBA's top record. San Antonio hosts the Los Angeles Clippers (4-9) on Thursday night, then has two days off before hosting the New Orleans Pelicans (5-11) on Saturday.

#3018. Dylan Harper could miss 2-3 weeks with left hip external rotator strain

After the Spurs announced yesterday that Dylan Harper would miss today's game against the Clippers with "hip soreness" but were still waiting for more information, they have now revealed that he suffered a left hip external rotator strain and could miss up to 2-3 weeks (although it could be less if all goes well). While any injury is not ideal news, it is certainly much less serious than a groin injury, which was the initial fear, and that timeline for return is friendlier than the 4-6 weeks social media doctors were suggesting when the injury occurred.

Harper suffered the injury in the final minute of Tuesday's loss to the Pistons when he was running up the court and slipped, falling awkwardly into a splits and appearing to pull his groin. He stayed down for a bit before limping back to the locker room. This morning's shootaround brought some relief ahead of the Spurs releasing the medical results, as Julian Champagnie hinted that it was not as serious as initially feared, and he was seen walking around without a limp.

Harper has been the Spurs' second leading scorer this season after Victor Wembanyama, averaging 18.4 points on 50% shooting. He has also been the most efficient finisher at the rim among guards in the entire league, where he shoots 76.9% while averaging 5.4 rebounds and 4.5 assists per game in his first season as a starter.

Replacing his production will be a team effort. The Spurs still have De'Aaron Fox and Stephon Castle, and sliding Champagnie back into the starting line-up gives them the same unit that made the Finals last season, but bench production has been an issue so far this season. Expect Jordan McLaughlin to see some regular minutes at back-up point guard while Harper is out, and Ja'Kobi Gillespie, who is currently tearing up the G League, could be called up (down, since San Antonio is south?) from Austin.

#3012. This move will prove to pay off for the Suns

As we waited for the Phoenix Suns season to start, many questions arose ahead of the final decisions about the team. With them having 15 players under contract, one of them, Mark Williams, out for an extended period before the season starts, and only two centers on the roster, changes were inevitable. That is where we saw Haywood Highsmith get waived, giving the Suns flexibility with their 14-man roster, and we also saw moves on two two-way contracts. These were C.J. Huntley and Koby Brea, who were waived to add more size with Duop Reath and Drew Timme. Both Huntley and Brea remained with the Valley Suns after being waived, but they brought in two new faces who have both seen some time in the NBA.

Reath spent the last three years with the Portland Trail Blazers and brings versatility to the frontcourt with his shooting. In the past, I highlighted him as a low-end free-agent target for the Suns, and I am happy to see him in the Valley this year. Even if he is just there as insurance if another big goes down, the Suns need that, and Reath can provide valuable shooting to a bench unit that already excels at that if he does get an opportunity.

The name that went unnoticed, though, was Drew Timme, who joined the Suns after being waived by the Los Angeles Lakers. As with Jordan Goodwin last season, the Suns brought in someone from the Lakers, and this could prove a strategic move. After Goodwin paid off last offseason, the investment in Timme may also pay off, given his run in the G League so far.

Now, Timme is no stranger to the spotlight; he was a dominant collegiate player at Gonzaga before going undrafted in the 2023 draft. After bouncing around the Nets and Lakers, he now finds himself in the Valley with an opportunity to impress. Even though it is just the G League, these numbers prove that Timme is better than his current standing. We saw this trend with Collin Gillespie his first year in the Valley, and he turned out to be a key piece for the Suns. Timme could be that next buy-low product that the Suns invest in that pans out, and over the last few seasons, that is how they have found key depth pieces.

Both Gillespie and Bouyea were on two-way contracts before landing NBA deals, and with the Suns still having that open roster spot, could that happen by the end of the season? Only time will tell if Timme gets an opportunity, but with his recent play, I wouldn't rule it out. Especially with Mark Williams still out, the Suns lack some size in the frontcourt and have elected to give Koa Peat some small-ball minutes to replicate their third center for now. Could Timme end up fighting for that, depending on whether the Valley Suns are home? Personally, I'd like to see it.

Timme, even though he hasn't had overwhelming success in his 36 NBA games, has shown flashes of impact. Sadly, he played for a pretty poor Nets team, but with the Lakers, he made plays to help Luka Doncic and LeBron James. If you can get those quotes from the GOAT, that must mean something, right?

Timme can come in and be another solid passer and playmaker in this frontcourt. Like Ighodaro, he has the basketball IQ to make not only smart plays but the right ones, and he would help open up more opportunities for those bench scorers. The team is already deep, and as a two-way player, it is not shocking that he hasn't seen much time with the Suns yet, but if injuries open a spot, the Suns should give him that shot.

This shows the Suns' ability to build on the margins. They identify talent that can stand out, and even if it ruffles some feathers, they don't care. They will give up on draft picks or guys who haven't panned out if there is a better name out there, and you have to appreciate that from a business standpoint: they have gotten guys like Gillespie and Bouyea, and they only saw time with injuries. Timme's time may relate to that, but once again he is proving, like those guys, he is better than the G League, which is what the Suns want. They need these small-impact guys to pan out and be better than anticipated to build out the bench and depth, and they've done that.

All in all, as a fan of the team, it is nice to see them make these smart moves compared to the ones that have not worked. It shows they can succeed with these marginal moves and offers some hope that, if there is someone you don't know who is signed to this team, they may actually pan out.

#3011. Federal Reserve Board releases results from survey of senior financial officers at banks about their strategies and practices for managing reserve balances

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released results from a survey of senior financial officers at banks about their strategies and practices for managing reserve balances. The Senior Financial Officer Survey is used by the Board to obtain information about banks' reserve balance management strategies and practices, their expectations around the size of their balance sheets and deposit balances their activity in money markets, and their views on other matters, such as operations on weekends and holidays.

The survey was conducted in collaboration with the Federal Reserve Bank of New York between September 18, 2026, and September 28, 2026, and includes responses from banks that held around three-fourths of total banking system reserve balances at the time of the survey.

#3007. How to watch Warriors vs. Grizzlies

The Golden State Warriors' six-game road trip comes to an end Thursday night as they take on the Memphis Grizzlies on the second night of a back-to-back. The game will be played at 5:00 PM PT in Memphis and can be watched on NBC Sports Bay Area.

It was more of the same for Golden State during Wednesday night's 116-94 loss to the Dallas Mavericks. The Warriors struggled to generate much offense outside of the energy provided by Will Richard, who earned a spot in the starting lineup and finished with a team-high 20 points on 9-of-13 shooting from the field. The defeat marked Golden State's fourth consecutive loss, a drastic turnaround just over a week after completing a perfect six-game homestand.

The Warriors will look to salvage at least one win from this road trip with tonight's matchup against the Grizzlies. That being said, the impact of Steph Curry's absence continues to be felt throughout the lineup, particularly for rookie Yaxel Lendeborg, who has struggled to find his offensive rhythm in recent games.

Lendeborg thrived earlier this season as a secondary scorer and playmaker alongside Curry. However, with the Warriors superstar sidelined, the rookie is getting his first real taste of what it means to be a primary offensive option in the NBA. The Warriors narrowly lost their first meeting against Memphis earlier this season, with Lendeborg finishing that game with 18 points to Boozer's 25 points. If Golden State hopes to end its losing streak, getting Lendeborg going offensively while setting the tone defensively from the opening tip will be key to closing out this road trip on a positive note.

#3005. Amid recent banking outages, should you start carrying cash again? Top advisors weigh in

For years, Americans have moved away from using physical U.S. currency in favor of credit, debit and digital payments. But amid the recent outages affecting some consumer banking services, experts say it's wise to revisit cash.

JPMorgan Chase on Monday reported a technical issue that disrupted some consumer banking services, with ripple effects extending to several major U.S. banks and the peer-to-peer payments network Zelle. A day later, a separate outage affected Apple, temporarily disrupting Apple Pay and iCloud, the service that keeps data available across various Apple devices.

Those reports came on the heels of separate, widespread issues with Microsoft Outlook and communication systems used by air traffic control centers.

As technology outages expose vulnerabilities across parts of the U.S. economy, some top advisors say consumers should reconsider keeping physical money on hand.

"This is about preparedness, not panic," said David La Placa, CEO of Intellectus Partners in San Francisco, which ranked No. 92 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 list for 2026. "I think keeping some cash at home is a reasonable starting point for many households."

La Placa advises consumers to "build redundancy" into their financial lives.

"Maintain access to at least a second financial institution, keep a backup credit card from a different issuer and make sure you have more than one way to access liquidity," he said.

A client's cash management should be diversified, said Seth Fierston, a certified financial planner and co-president of Fierston Financial Group in West Hartford, Connecticut. The firm ranked No. 22 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 list.

Having multiple accounts at brokerages or banks and various ways to move money, including Automated Clearing House, or ACH, wire, check, Venmo or Zelle, "can help mitigate technology risk," Fierston said. "One of our guiding principles is flexibility."

When it comes to everyday transactions, Americans are increasingly relying on digital forms of payment.

These days, only about 46% of Americans carry cash with them always or most of the time, according to a survey of nearly 10,000 adults conducted between May and June by the Pew Research Center, a nonpartisan research group.

Roughly 42% of U.S. adults said they don't use cash for any purchases in a typical week, up from 24% in 2015, Pew found.

Young adults are the least likely to carry physical currency. Among adults under 30, 51% said they go completely cashless, according to the survey.

"I recommend to my clients that they always keep three days of average cash spend on hand given the uncertainty of life in general," said Tim Lootens, CFP, managing director at Chilton Capital Management in Houston, which ranked No. 7 on this year's Financial Advisor 100.

Considering the recent technical troubles, Lootens said consumers should keep even more cash at the ready.

"We've had the Microsoft Outlook outage, the FAA ground shutdown and now banking disruption," he said. "This makes me think five to seven days may be a better number."

The right amount is as psychological as it is financial, he added. "The ability to access cash and having comfort knowing it is there when needed is so important," he said.

Brian Vendig, president and chief investment officer at MJP Wealth Advisors, which ranked No. 42 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100, also said he recommends holding a dollar amount that corresponds with a household's discretionary living expenses. He advised having enough for at least one to two weeks, depending on their needs.

While there is little precedent for a banking or payment system outage lasting more than a few business days, Vendig said, a one- to two-week cash reserve provides a "reasonable buffer" for essentials such as groceries, gas and other necessities, if electronic payments become unavailable.

"We apply this range because it is also an emotional concern for most people, and they might want to have a little extra saved at home due to the size of their household and having a little extra 'peace of mind' guarantee," he said.

Vendig said he recommends stashing extra cash in a secure location at home for emergency needs.

Vendig also said consumers should be vigilant in ensuring their electronic devices have the most recent software security patches installed.

Criminals will try to exploit moments of uncertainty, experts say.

"Consumers should assume they will be targeted," said Craig Timm, senior director of anti-money laundering at ACAMS, an anti-financial crime group.

"[This week's] reported bank outages are likely to cause widespread confusion and fear among consumers about losing access to their money," he said. "Criminals will try to exploit that anxiety by pushing victims to take urgent action to protect their funds or restore account access."

As always, "stay alert, never click on links in unexpected bank messages and never share personal information such as passwords or PINs," he advised. "If you need to reach your bank, use a trusted, official channel," Timm said.

#3001. Raptors' RJ Barrett out with strained right hamstring, Collin Murray-Boyles placed in concussion protocol

Toronto Raptors forward RJ Barrett has been diagnosed with a mild right hamstring strain and will be re-evaluated in two weeks, the team announced Thursday.

Additionally, forward Collin Murray-Boyles was placed in concussion protocol as a precautionary measure.

Barrett missed Wednesday's 103-99 loss to the Washington Wizards with hamstring tightness. That was the second of back-to-back games for the Raptors, who also lost to the New York Knicks on Tuesday. Barrett played 39 minutes in that game, scoring 22 points with 5 rebounds and 5 assists.

However, hamstring discomfort has been an issue for Barrett in recent weeks. He has been using a stationary bike on the sideline when not on the court, in addition to during timeouts and stoppages in play, according to The Athletic's Esfandiar Baraheni.

Murray-Boyles left Wednesday's contest during the first half after playing four minutes. The Raptors announced that he would not return due to illness, which has apparently been going around the team locker room. When he sustained a possible concussion is not certain.

Barrett, 26, is Toronto's second-leading scorer this season, averaging 20.9 points with 6.4 rebounds and 4.3 assists per game. He's shooting a career-low 28% on 74 3-point attempts. Prior to sitting out Wednesday's contest, Barrett had appeared in all 14 of the Raptors' games.

In his second season, Murray-Boyles is averaging 9.3 points, 4.6 rebounds and 1.8 assists, in addition to 1 steal and 1.1 blocks in 14 games (eight starts). He was the Raptors' 2025 first-round pick (No. 9 overall) out of South Carolina.

Toronto (5-10) has lost its past six games and eight of its past 10. The Raptors currently rank ninth in the Eastern Conference and 16th in the NBA. They visit the Detroit Pistons (10-4) on Friday.

#2996. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2995. Dylan Harper avoids serious hip injury in relief to Spurs

Dylan Harper's feet went out from under him with a little more than a minute left, and for a few long minutes it looked like the San Antonio Spurs' red-hot start might go down with him.

Harper has a left hip rotator strain and avoided a major injury, both ESPN and the San Antonio Express-News reported. He is expected to miss from one to three weeks.

The second-year guard got hurt late in the Spurs' 105-93 loss to the Detroit Pistons on Nov. 17. With 1:07 left, Harper stopped to plant on a pass from Stephen Castle, and his feet slid out from under him on what appeared to be a wet spot on the court. Detroit's Ausar Thompson picked off the pass while Harper stayed on the floor, grabbing at his groin.

He was down for about a minute before a Spurs foul stopped play. Teammates and staff had to help him off the court. He had 20 points, four rebounds and two assists in 35 minutes before he left.

The loss ended San Antonio's 12-game winning streak. The way the Spurs talked after the game, they seemed braced for a long absence. Two days later, the mood has changed. The San Antonio Express News reported that Harper walked into the shootaround before Thursday's game against the Los Angeles Clippers without a limp.

The Spurs had already ruled out Harper for the Nov. 19 game against the Clippers because of left hip soreness. It's the first game he has missed this season. He had started and played in all 15 games alongside De'Aaron Fox and Castle in the backcourt.

The Spurs are 12-3 and second in the Western Conference, and Harper has been a big reason why. The No. 2 pick in the 2025 NBA Draft is averaging 18.4 points, 5.4 rebounds and 4.5 assists while shooting 50.5% from the field.

This comes as a dose of good news. The Spurs are talented and deep enough to remain competitive in Harper's absence, especially because of San Antonio's depth at guard.

Harper, 20, had been in the middle of a breakout season, his first as a starter, and has become one of the more promising young players in the NBA.

He excels in transition and does not shy away from getting to the rim, which helps open up space for San Antonio's perimeter shooters, so it's not like his absence won't be felt.

But the Spurs can actually return to the starting lineup that led them to an NBA Finals appearance last season, with Julian Champagnie sliding into small forward to replace Harper.

If Harper remains sidelined past the return of Tobias Harris (calf), that would give San Antonio further flexibility, as Harris is a floor-spacing forward known for his perimeter shooting. Harris is yet to make his Spurs debut, but he would mitigate the loss of Harper, albeit in a very different way.

And while the Spurs love the contributions of Keldon Johnson, they prefer him to come off the bench as the sixth man; don't expect that to change.

All to say: San Antonio should be breathing a sigh of relief because the continued development of Harper is necessary for any championship run.

#2992. As he did with the Giants, Colts QB Daniel Jones is struggling, right after getting paid

Daniel Jones' career has had some ups, but also plenty of downs, both with the Giants and with the Colts. He has a knack for having his "ups" in contract years, and his "downs" after getting paid.

Jones was awful in Thursday night's loss to the Texans, continuing the worst season of his career — which comes after the Colts signed him to a two-year, $88 million contract in the offseason.

It's not the first time Jones has performed poorly after getting a big deal. In fact, that's all Jones has done throughout his NFL career.

Jones' career started when the Giants made the surprising decision to select him with the sixth overall pick in the 2019 NFL draft. That earned him a four-year, $25.7 million contract, with the Giants having a fifth-year option. Jones didn't live up to expectations on that rookie contract, and was playing some of the worst football of his career when his third season ended early with a neck injury, so the Giants declined his fifth-year option.

But Jones then played very well in his fourth year, leading the Giants to a surprise playoff berth and a win in the wild card round. That led the Giants to sign him to a four-year, $160 million contract in 2023, committing more money to him than his fifth-year option would have cost. Jones responded to that with a fifth season in which he played poorly, suffered another neck injury and then suffered a season-ending torn ACL.

Jones returned in 2024 but was so bad the Giants first benched him, then cut him. Jones spent the rest of that season on the Vikings' practice squad.

In 2025 the Colts signed him to a one-year contract, and he responded by winning a quarterback competition with Anthony Richardson and then playing the best football of his career early in 2025 as the Colts jumped out to an 8-2 start. He started to struggle down the stretch in that season, and then his season was cut short by a torn Achilles tendon, but the Colts were so impressed by his start that they signed him to a two-year, $88 million contract in March.

And now, in the first year of that contract, Jones is worse than ever. His 13 interceptions are the most of any quarterback in the NFL this season and already a career-high, with six more games to play. He's paired that with just seven touchdown passes. He still hasn't had a single game this season with more touchdowns than interceptions. His 65.4 passer rating is a career low. His 30.3 QBR is a career low. His 58.4 percent completion rate is a career low. His 5.6 yards per pass average is a career low. Jones was once an effective runner, but his 2.9 yards per carry average is also a career low. Any way you slice it, Jones is worse than ever.

He's also more expensive than ever. Whether the Colts keep him or cut him, he's going to have a massive salary cap hit in 2027, the kind of cap hit that will make it that much harder for the Colts to rebuild their roster.

If the Colts cut Jones, he may sign another one-year contract with another team. No matter how well he plays, that team would be wise not to give him a lucrative extension.

#2985. Kraken parent's acquisition of Tribe Capital flags new chapter in evolution of crypto exchanges

Payward, the owner of crypto exchange Kraken, is pushing into private markets with its latest acquisition, underscoring a broader shift in cryptocurrencies, from trading-led growth to the buildout of financial infrastructure.

Payward said late Wednesday that it's buying venture capital and asset management provider Tribe Capital Management, led by Payward's co-CEO Arjun Sethi. No purchase price was disclosed.

Tribe, where Sethi is co-founder and chairman, invests across early- and late-stage private companies and has $2.7 billion in assets under management.

"Joining Payward gives us the opportunity to bring Tribe's investment approach to a global financial platform," Jonathan Hsu, another co-founder of Tribe Capital, said in a statement. "We see a strong fit between our investment approach and Payward's infrastructure, distribution, and product-building capabilities."

Payward didn't respond to a request for comment.

The acquisition is novel among crypto exchanges. Unlike exchange-owned venture arms such as Coinbase Ventures or Binance Labs, Payward is looking to integrate Tribe's investment and fund-management capabilities into an existing custody, trading, collateral and tokenization infrastructure to develop private-market investment products.

Payward is trying to position itself to capture value from a broader crypto-driven transformation of financial services, not use the venture vehicle to draw more capital into the crypto ecosystem.

The acquisition is another step in the evolution in the crypto exchange business model. With cryptocurrency prices continuing to decline this year, crypto exchanges have worked to move past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, focusing instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance.

#2983. Grading the Browns defense: New faces, similar results at the bye week

Going into the 2026 season, the Cleveland Browns defense was undergoing some changes that were seen as major from the outside. Reigning Defensive Player of the Year Myles Garrett was traded to the Los Angeles Rams, and defensive coordinator Jim Schwartz chose to leave the team after being passed up for the head coaching job.

In the Myles Garrett trade, the team received defensive end Jared Verse from the Rams (who won defensive rookie of the year in 2024 and is a two-time Pro Bowler), who isn't necessarily the same caliber player as Garrett but has shown signs of being a really good edge rusher while in LA. The Browns hired Atlanta Falcons passing game coordinator Mike Rutenberg to be their defensive coordinator.

Analysts and fans were concerned about the defense taking a step back after losing Garrett and Schwartz, but it appears that the defense is doing just fine. Just like we did with the offense earlier this week, it is time to size up the Browns defense at the bye week and give it a grade.

Outside of losing Garrett and Schwartz, not much. After losing linebacker Devin Bush in free agency, the team replaced him with linebacker Quincy Williams. Cornerback Martin Emerson also left, but outside of those minor subtractions, the team has essentially returned most of their group from last season.

Defensive tackle Mason Graham has emerged as the best player on the defensive line. Linebacker Carson Schwesinger might be a top 10 linebacker in this league, maybe even Top 5. Extending cornerback Denzel Ward and safety Grant Delpit were no-brainers. Ward, who is playing at a top-5 level, will likely be an All-Pro this season, and Delpit has been one of the more underrated safeties in the league. After being acquired in a trade with the Jacksonville Jaguars last season, cornerback Tyson Campbell has fit in well in his second full year with the team.

When Schwartz wasn't retained as the defensive coordinator, many wondered how the scheme would look.

Rutenberg, also known as Rudy, decided to not change the scheme but instead tweak it. Schwartz's defenses weren't complex in terms of how the scheme looked. Not much creativity in terms of mixing up the coverages and disguising looks. Schwartz also failed to make the necessary adjustments, and his defenses were easy to decipher against better offensive minds. Rutenberg has made tweaks to the scheme but is still keeping the core principles that made the Browns defense elite last season.

Overall, going from a top 5 defense to a top 10 is not that bad. One of the more surprising stats is how the team is still top 10 in pressures despite losing Garrett. It speaks volumes as to how talented their defensive line is. Players such as defensive end Isaiah McGuire and tackles Adin Huntington and Maliek Collins have contributed to the pressure.

In terms of the play on the back end, the Browns play man coverage more than anyone in the league, and they are really good at it. Rookie safety Emmanuel McNeil-Warren has started to stand out, and it's not long before he becomes a full-time starter because of how well he's played.

Slot corner was considered a weakness going into the season, but cornerback Myles Harden might have fixed it. Harden has shown his effectiveness on blitz packages, but his coverage is what stands out. Harden had a rough training camp, but he was able to bounce back and show his contributions at a high level. Teams will regularly throw in his direction, but that is a testament to Ward and Campbell more than anything else.

It's not a major concern, but the run defense has taken a step back from last season. As good as the Browns defensive line is, they lack someone big along the interior. Cleveland's defensive line is small but quick in terms of their get-off, but against teams that run trap/wham run concepts, they are susceptible to giving up chunk yards.

The tackling also needs to improve, but some of the tackling woes can be due to the team's attacking style they play. In terms of who needs to improve from a player standpoint, it's Jared Verse. Verse hasn't had the season he's wanted, but he has to find a way to convert pressures into sacks. Last Sunday against the Texans, Verse played one of his best games as a Brown, but it can only get better if he builds off that performance.

Despite losing Garrett, the Browns defense is still a top 10 unit in the league. Rutenberg has been an upgrade over Schwartz. The team is still missing that alpha in the defensive end room, but overall, for a unit that many people thought would fall off, the fact that this unit has been top-10 has been a surprise, but not to those who pay close attention.

How would you grade the Browns defense as the bye week continues?

#2981. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2980. Anthropic IPO 'exactly what the markets need,' but analysts warn of volatility

Anthropic's blockbuster IPO on Thursday gave markets a much-needed boost after weeks of weakness, though analysts warned that the stock could remain volatile in the coming weeks.

Share of the Claude maker opened at $111.50 and closed at $97.17 per share, 17% above the IPO price. The offering raised $81.3 billion through the sale of 980 million shares — surpassing SpaceX's June listing to become the largest IPO on record.

Nomura strategist Charlie McElligott described investors as "foaming at the mouth" for the offering, with allocations "impossibly low," underscoring the strength of demand.

"Anthropic's IPO is exactly what the markets need to revive overall sentiment," said Rebecca Walser, chief investment officer at Walser Wealth Management, adding that markets have been "depressed with all of the negativity in the AI complex over the past several weeks."

Still, U.S. equities fell sharply on Thursday, with the tech heavy Nasdaq Composite dropped 2.25% to end at 23,879.20, S&P 500 down 2.02% and the Dow Jones Industrial Average down 2.19%.

Artificial intelligence and technology stocks initially rose thanks to the IPO, but the gains faded as "profit-taking spread across AI-related stocks," said James Ooi, chief market strategist at Tiger Brokers.

While the initial pop was not surprising for such a highly anticipated listing, investors should avoid "chasing the IPO," Walser said.

She expects the stock to come under pressure in the coming weeks as former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang testifies before the U.S. Senate, potentially reviving concerns around the AI sector.

"The shares remain highly volatile," said Morningstar senior equity analyst Malik Ahmed Khan.

Walser said that any weakness around the time of Huang's testimony could offer an entry point for investors interested in Anthropic, adding that she remains bullish on the OpenAI rival over the long term.

"For investors, it's going to become a blue-chip stock like Google," Ross Gerber, president and CEO of Gerber Kawasaki Wealth & Investment Management told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

He added that U.S. technology valuations are at ridiculously low levels relative to the "crazy amazing innovation" coming out of California.

Morningstar's Khan struck a more cautious tone, warning investors against getting swept up in the enthusiasm around Anthropic, advising them instead to step back and "carefully" assess the company as a long-term business.

That will depend on Anthropic's ability to scale enterprise adoption, continue improving its models and turn those capabilities into economically useful products and services in the AI ecosystem, he said.

"We remain confident that the firm will be able to execute on all three fronts," Khan added, setting a $92 fair value estimate for the stock.

Walser stated that Anthropic is "extremely important for the overall AI story" as the first non-Chinese large language model developer to go public.

Its listing gives public-market investors their first direct test of how a major standalone AI model developer will be valued and perform outside the chip sector. Meanwhile, rival OpenAI could follow with an IPO sometime in 2027.

"This IPO is the show me moment for the AI story," Walser said, as much of the stock market's AI narrative has so far centered on chipmakers.

#2977. Lumentum CEO defends Marvell deal after stock plunge: 'We've got work to do' with shareholders

Lumentum CEO Michael Hurlston defended the company's planned merger with chipmaker Marvell Technology on Thursday, acknowledging that the deal has received a disappointing reception.

"Their numbers are phenomenal. Our numbers are phenomenal, and we feel the combination is even more powerful," Hurlston told CNBC's Jim Cramer on "Mad Money." Nevertheless, Hurlston said, "Shareholders are not happy ... It's been a tough couple of weeks."

Shares of Marvell and Lumentum have tumbled 45% and 42%, respectively, since the close on Oct. 28. That night, Bloomberg News reported Marvell was nearing a megadeal to buy Lumentum, sending shockwaves through the market and beginning the wild ride that both stocks have been on. The tie-up was officially announced on the morning of Nov. 2 with the deal expected to close in the first half of 2028. At the time, the all-stock deal implied an enterprise value of $115 billion for Lumentum.

Beginning on Oct. 29, Marvell shares experienced three straight days of double-digit percentage declines. In total, they've advanced in just three sessions since the Oct. 28 close. For its part, Lumentum rose modestly on Oct. 29 following the initial M&A rumor. Then the selling pressure arrived, as investors questioned the merits of the deal. The stock has been green in five sessions since the Bloomberg report.

The merger would combine Marvell's semiconductor and networking technology with Lumentum's optical components as AI data centers increasingly shift from copper wiring to fiber-optic connections to handle faster data transmission. Hurlston said that transition, rather than simply the construction of more data centers, will be a major growth driver for the planned combined entity.

"We've got work to do with our shareholders," Hurlston said, though he sees catalysts on the horizon that could help change their minds. He pointed to Marvell's earnings report scheduled for Dec. 1 and the upcoming release of a securities filing, known as an S-4, required as part of the merger process.

"As people see [Marvell CEO Matt Murphy's] earnings he's going to announce in early December, we've got the S-4 coming out that's going to describe how both businesses are performing, we've got our earnings … I think those things together will give the shareholders confidence that, look, there's nothing wrong here. I mean, these businesses are great, and they're even better together," said Hurlston, who has led Lumentum since February 2025.

To be sure, the dramatic decline in both Marvell and Lumentum shares isn't entirely due to the investor displeasure with the planned transaction. In recent weeks, the market has soured on the AI trade more broadly, amid questions about the sustainability of the AI buildout due to higher borrowing costs and political backlash on data center construction, which was exemplified by the midterm election results. Though Hurlston acknowledged these other forces affecting the stocks and leading to compressed valuation multiples, he emphasized the work that he needs to do to win shareholder support.

Despite concerns about the broader AI buildout, Hurlston said Lumentum has seen no slowdown in demand. The company reported better-than-expected quarterly results on the day the merger was announced, with revenue more than doubling from a year earlier.

"I think we're going to get that multiple back once we can show our numbers and show how strong the two businesses are doing," he said.

#2976. President Donald Trump Pardons Pete Rose, Claims Baseball Legend Was ‘Treated Terribly’ by MLB

Pete Rose has received a posthumous presidential pardon more than two years after his death.

President Donald Trump announced the decision in a Truth Social post on Thursday, November 19, praising Rose's accomplishments and criticizing Major League Baseball for keeping him out of the Hall of Fame.

"I have just given a Complete and Total Pardon to the Late, Great Pete Rose, a true Baseball SUPERSTAR & LEGEND, whose record of 4,256 Career Hits will never be broken," Trump, 80, wrote.

The president highlighted Rose's 3,562 career games, .303 batting average, 17 All-Star selections and three World Series championships.

Trump also compared Rose to former pitcher Roger Clemens, writing that both were "treated terribly by Major League Baseball in not allowing him to go into the Baseball Hall of Fame. Politics, I guess!"

Rose, who died on September 30, 2024, at age 83, spent 24 seasons playing for the Cincinnati Reds, Philadelphia Phillies and Montreal Expos. He also managed the Reds from 1984 to 1989.

His career was overshadowed by a gambling scandal that resulted in his permanent ban from baseball in 1989.

According to ESPN, the FBI released 130 pages of documents detailing Rose's connections to bookmaker Ronald Peters.

Per the report, a 1987 FBI memo cited a witness who claimed "at one time Rose owed Peters $90,000 in sports wagering losses."

Rose later admitted to betting on baseball, telling then-MLB commissioner Bud Selig during a November 2002 meeting about his lifetime ban, "Yes, sir, I did bet on baseball," according to CBS News.

Separately, according to the Los Angeles Times, Rose pleaded guilty to two federal counts of filing false tax returns in 1990 and served five months in prison. Trump's pardon addresses those federal convictions, not baseball's disciplinary action.

ESPN reported that MLB commissioner Rob Manfred removed Rose from the permanently ineligible list in May 2025, making him eligible for Hall of Fame consideration.

#2975. Record Book: Josh Allen has Jim Kelly in his sights

We're going to need a fresh pencil, a good eraser, and maybe a little more room in the record book. Josh Allen keeps giving us names to cross out and new numbers to write in their place.

Allen moved past Peyton Manning on the NFL's all-time touchdown list and tied Eric Dickerson on the career rushing touchdown list. And while we've been keeping a close eye on both of those records for weeks, seeing Allen check them off in the same game gave us a little extra work to do.

Of course, we're not complaining. Crossing names off our lists and moving Allen up a few spots is pretty much the whole point of this exercise. Besides, he's not the only one keeping us busy. There are still plenty of records within reach for the Bills, and with another week of football ahead, it's time to see which pages we might need to rewrite next.

With another week in the books and a new set of milestones within reach, here's where things stand heading into Week 11.

_____________________________________________________________________________

Josh Allen moves into the NFL's top 50 in career passing yards

This one shouldn't take long. Josh Allen needs just 20 passing yards against Miami to move past Hall of Fame quarterback Kurt Warner (32,344) and into the NFL's all-time top 50. Allen enters Week 11 with 32,325 career passing yards, so we're probably looking at a first-quarter update to the record book.

And there's a good chance he won't stop there. Cam Newton sits just ahead of Warner with 32,382 career passing yards. Allen needs 58 yards to pass both quarterbacks and move up another spot on the leaderboard.

Unless something goes very wrong against Miami, Allen should be leapfrogging a couple of NFL legends before halftime.

_____________________________________________________________________________

Josh Allen moves up the NFL's all-time rushing touchdown list

Allen didn't waste any time making changes to this list last week. With two rushing touchdowns against the Jets, he reached 90 for his career, tying Eric Dickerson and Curtis Martin on the NFL's all-time rushing touchdown list.

Now he's just one away from moving up again. Another rushing touchdown against Miami would put Allen at 91, moving him past Dickerson and Martin and into a tie with Jerome Bettis and Franco Harris for 12th all-time. Not bad company for a quarterback.

Josh Allen climbs the all-time passing touchdown list

Allen has another opportunity to move up the NFL's passing touchdown leaderboard this week, and he won't need a career day to do it. He enters Week 11 with 233 career passing touchdowns, just one behind Donovan McNabb (234).

One touchdown pass would tie McNabb for 38th all-time, while two would move Allen ahead of him. Three would bring Allen to 236, tying George Blanda for 37th. We're not saying Allen is going to throw three touchdown passes against Miami. But we're not not saying it, either.

James Cook takes sole possession of second place

For eight straight games, James Cook has rushed for at least 75 yards and scored a touchdown. That's a pretty impressive streak on its own, but it gets even better when we look at where it puts him in NFL history.

Cook is currently tied with John Riggins, George Rogers, and LaDainian Tomlinson for the second-longest streak in league history. If he reaches both marks again against Miami, he could put those names behind his.

And then there's Terry Allen, who holds the NFL record at 10 straight games. We're not quite there yet, but Cook could be knocking on that door before November is over.

At this point, Greg Rousseau's name might as well have a permanent spot in this chapter. We've been waiting a few weeks to move his single-season sack record from the watch list to the history books, but that ninth sack is proving a little stubborn.

Rousseau enters Week 11 with eight sacks, matching his previous career high. One more against Miami would give him a new personal best, and we'd finally get to retire this particular entry.

But don't worry, Groot. We've got another spot for you later in the record book. You're not getting out of here that easily.

_____________________________________________________________________________

Bills defense closes in on a franchise record

The Bills' defense has gone 33 consecutive opponent drives without allowing a touchdown. That's a lot of possessions ending without six points on the scoreboard, and it's the longest such streak for Buffalo since 1999.

That 1999 defense put together 38 straight drives without surrendering a touchdown, leaving this year's group just five away from matching the franchise mark. Six more would give the current defense the record outright.

Of course, if the Bills are going to break a franchise record, why stop at 39? They've already kept three straight opponents out of the end zone for a full four quarters. What's one more?

Dawson Knox moves up the Bills' receiving touchdown list

Dawson Knox is becoming another familiar face in our weekly record watch. We've been waiting for him to move up the Bills' receiving touchdown leaderboard for a few weeks now, and we're still holding his place.

Knox enters Week 11 with 28 career receiving touchdowns, tied with Stevie Johnson and Gabe Davis. One more would move him past both former Bills receivers and into a tie with Jerry Butler at 29. Two touchdowns against Miami would put him at 30 and move him ahead of Butler, too.

We've already made room for Knox a little higher on the list. Now we just need him to give us a reason to write his name there.

_____________________________________________________________________________

Josh Allen chases Jim Kelly's franchise passing touchdown record

We've already got Allen moving up the NFL's all-time passing touchdown list in Chapter 2, but this is where things get a little crazy.

Allen needs four touchdown passes against Miami to tie Jim Kelly's franchise record of 237, or five to break it. Six would also tie Len Dawson (239) on the NFL's all-time list.

Five touchdown passes in one afternoon? That's asking a lot, even of Josh Allen. But we've seen him do some pretty ridiculous things on a football field, so we're not ruling anything out – and that's the point of this Superhuman chapter anyway, isn't it?

Greg Rousseau chases Mario Williams on the Bills' sack list

Remember when we told Groot he wasn't getting out of our record book that easily? Well, here we are.

Rousseau enters Week 11 with 40 career sacks, sitting three behind Mario Williams (43) on the Bills' all-time list.

A three-sack game is a pretty tall order, even for Rousseau. But if he's going to pick a week to finally break that single-season personal best we've been tracking, he might as well make it one that sends us scrambling to update two chapters at once.

_____________________________________________________________________________

Some of these milestones could be checked off before halftime Sunday. Others might keep us reaching for that pencil for another week or two. Either way, there's no shortage of history within reach for Buffalo.

And while we've been keeping track of what individual players are accomplishing, there's one more number worth watching. A win over Miami would give the Bills their first 9–1 start since 1991.

That's a pretty good reminder that, for all the individual records being chased, the most important number on Sunday will still be the one in the win column. Let's hope we're adding another one.

→ Baca artikel asli

O'Neill's reigning champions are four points clear at the top of the league, having played a game more than nearest challengers Rangers.

The manager says his side have "got to play better" and "be more aggressive going forward".

On the prospect of January additions, O'Neill added: "We're definitely looking at players, wanting to strengthen the squad, which I think we would have to do.

"When people are fit, we will have numbers. We need a bit of quality.

"It would be lovely if, by January, we still had a chance of qualifying for the knockouts of the Europa League. The next couple of games will probably make that decision for us. Hopefully, it's not the breaking point of getting players in.

"I think that we would be doing something. We're absolutely looking at players now.

"I suppose every single club is doing that. The quicker you could get somebody in the better."

#2973. U.K.-India trade deal faces a stiff test over Scotch whisky import taxes

Within months of taking effect, the India-U.K. trade deal is facing an early test, as New Delhi resists extending preferential tariffs to bulk Scotch whisky imports.

Duty on bulk Scotch whisky imports was expected to be slashed to 75% then gradually to 40% over 10 years as per the trade agreement. However, a Reuters report Thursday said that India now plans to levy a 162.5% duty on these imports, higher than the 150% in place before the trade deal.

While India's commerce ministry did not respond to CNBC's request for comments, experts said that authorities in India classify bulk Scotch as a raw material and claim the preferential tariffs accorded in the deal apply only to imported bottled Scotch.

Amid the confusion over tax rates, thousands of liters of bulk Scotch in more than 80 shipping containers belonging to Pernod, Diageo and Bacardi are stuck at Indian ports, according to Reuters.

"Ministers are urgently working with the Government of India to resolve this issue to ensure UK businesses can export under the tariffs agreed in the trade deal," a U.K. government spokesperson told CNBC.

Distillers in India use bulk Scotch in their own locally blended whisky brands. As a result, Indian tax authorities are classifying bulk Scotch as "compound alcoholic preparations" which do not qualify for the preferential rate, said Sohrab Bararia, partner and leader of global trade at Grant Thornton Bharat.

Meanwhile, distillers argue that "genuine Scotch whisky" does not cease to be whisky merely because it is imported in bulk containers or used later for blending and bottling in India, Bararia said.

After three years, including 14 rounds of rigorous negotiations, India and the U.K. finalized the deal last year, which is expected to boost bilateral trade by 25.5 billion pounds ($34 billion) per year by 2040.

Officials from Diageo and Chivas Brothers had cheered the trade deal and underscored its importance for Scotch and Scotland.

"India is the world's largest whisky market by volume, so Scotch was always going to be one of the biggest prizes for the UK in this deal," said Reema Bhattacharya, head of Asia research at risk intelligence company Verisk Maplecroft.

Instead of simply exporting bottled whisky, bulk scotch exports offer British producers long-term ties with companies in India, deepening their stake in the South Asian country's domestic alcoholic beverage industry, Bhattacharya said.

In the global spirits markets, while the U.S., Europe, and China are facing volume contraction, ageing populations, and shifting social habits, nearly 100 million new consumers will be of legal drinking age in India over the next five years, Indian brokerage HDFC Institutional Research said in a report in September.

The alcoholic beverage market in India is dominated by Indian-made foreign liquors (IMFL), as per the report. IMFL refers to alcoholic beverages produced in India by combining local spirits often with imported malts and flavorings.

Greater access to bulk Scotch stocks at competitive prices helps Indian distilleries to improve blending quality for premium whisky products while maintaining cost efficiency, experts said. However, the latest dispute poses a major threat to these operations.

The lack of preferential tariffs on bulk Scotch could have "substantial" financial implications for importers, Parth Shah, partner at Deloitte India, told CNBC. "This could particularly affect Indian manufacturers who have invested in domestic blending and bottling operations using imported bulk Scotch whisky," he added.

Diageo-owned United Spirits, Pernod Ricard and Indian firms Allied Blenders and Radico Khaitan own and market some of the popular whisky brands in India.

Companies and industry bodies, the International Spirits and Wine Association and Confederation of Indian Alcoholic Beverage Companies, did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

A spokesperson for one of the whisky makers told CNBC that industry bodies were in talks with the commerce ministry to "represent adherence to the India-UK FTA." The person did not wish to be identified as they fear it could lead to a potential backlash from regulators.

"The lesson for the EU and other partners negotiating with India is that securing a headline tariff reduction is only half the battle," Bhattacharya of Maplecroft Verisk said, adding that partners need to pay close attention to whether "concessions translate into actual market access."

#2972. NFL considering rules to prevent short international stays like Rams' in Australia

It's far in the rearview mirror now, but the Los Angeles Rams drew a lot of attention for their travel plans leading up to Week 1 against the San Francisco 49ers in Melbourne, Australia. The 49ers arrived more than a week before the game, but the Rams took a very different approach by getting to Melbourne 28 hours before kickoff.

The Rams, obviously, were flat in that game and got steamrolled by San Francisco, 27-7. That only led to more criticism for the way they handled the trip.

In the future, the NFL could prevent such brief international trips for teams. According to The Athletic, the league will explore putting a mandate on how many nights a team must stay in the international market.

"Currently, we leave those football decisions on when the team arrives in the market to the club," NFL executive VP of club business Peter O'Reilly told The Athletic. "It is something that we'll consider moving forward on: Do we put some minimum nights in a market in there? It's something we'll look at."

Considering how soon before kickoff the Rams arrived, they didn't have much time to visit local spots and connect with the community. They essentially landed in Australia, played the game and left in an attempt to keep their body clocks on West Coast time.

The Rams have marketing rights in Australia, and though they weren't in Melbourne very long before the game, they spent the last two offseasons making trips there with players and members of the team.

That's when their community efforts took place, with team president Kevin Demoff calling it a year-round effort.

"For us, this is a year-round effort, and I would much rather win the game and grow the market the other 51 weeks a year than focus on having our players here for that week," Demoff told CNBC in September.

Still, the NFL would like to see teams spend more time in international markets before games so they can connect with the community.

"Having access to players and that type of experience when they're in the market does have a lasting impact," O'Reilly said in his interview with The Athletic. "That's a factor when you start to think about encouraging or potentially looking at a policy where you'd want a club in the market for more than the day before."

Under Sean McVay, the Rams don't usually spend much time on international soil before a game. They took the same approach for their London game in 2025 as they did with the Australia trip this season, preferring that to an early arrival long before kickoff.

This article originally appeared on Rams Wire: NFL considering rules to prevent short international stays like Rams' in Australia

#2971. Spurs Dylan Harper avoids major injury, likely out 2-3 weeks with hip strain

This could have been much worse. After further testing, the San Antonio Spurs have diagnosed guard Dylan Harper with a left hip external rotator strain, a story first reported by Jeff McDonald of the San Antonio Express-News.

There is no official timeline for a return, but it is likely a 1-3 week injury, according to reports. That feels like dodging a bullet for San Antonio.

Harper, a second-year guard, slipped on a wet spot on the floor and went down with just more than a minute left in the Spurs' loss to the Pistons on Tuesday. He clearly was in pain and stayed down while play continued on the other end, and he was eventually helped off the court and back to the locker room.

Harper, 20, has taken a step forward this season, averaging 18.4 points on 50.5% shooting, plus 5.4 rebounds and 4.5 assists per game, while playing high-level defense — he might already be the second-best Spurs player. The Spurs have a +22.3 net rating when he is on the court.

With Harper out, expect to see more Julian Champagnie, Jordan McLaughlin and Keldon Johnson — San Antonio has the depth to handle this loss. That said, this would be easier for the Spurs if Tobias Harris were healthy, but he remains unavailable.

#2970. Giants midseason 2027 NFL mock draft

We're officially in the second half of the New York Giants 2026 season with eight games remaining this year.

The Giants were very active at the trade deadline, though while they went "big game hunting" they weren't able to land any impact players. They were hoping to add another difference maker before they got to the meat of their schedule, but it wasn't to be. The upside of that is that the Giants still have all draft capital that they came into the season with.

All this year we've been using the NFL draft as a lens through which to evaluate the state of the Giants as well as context for a chaotic season of college football. Now the College Football Playoffs are taking shape while the NFL playoff race is heating up.

The Giants are in the unfamiliar position of being in control of the NFC East and making a run to the playoffs, but this is still a good time to take stock of the Giants' wants and needs as we head into the home stretch.

I used the simulator from Pro Football and Sports Network to do this draft.

The Giants were hot after a defensive tackle at the trade deadline, chasing after every impact lineman they thought they could pry loose. It didn't work out, so now they're diving into a deep draft class to add talent and depth to their defensive line.

My choices here were between David Stone of Oklahoma and A'Mauri Washington from Oregon. Both were a bit disappointing to start the year, but have come on in recent weeks. I opted for Washington because I believe he fits what the Giants want in a tackle more closely than does stone. He's an enormous young man at 6-foot-3, 330 pounds, but also has legitimately freakish power and athleticism. Washington ranked fourth on Bruce Feldman's annual Freaks List in 2025 and moved up to third on the 2026 list. He not only has elite first step explosiveness, but also has rare long speed for his size and clocked over 20 mph in a sprint.

Washington is an inconsistent defender at this stage of his development, but he flashes the ability to be an absolute rock in the run game as well as a real factor as a pass rusher thanks to his mass, strength, and explosiveness.

He's similar to a young Dexter Lawrence in that his athletic tools are overwhelming in one-on-one situations, but his technique is the limiting factor at this stage of his development. His freakish strength could make him a force on the defensive line if he can become a technician.

Also considered: David Stone (iDL, Oklahoma), KJ Bolden (S, Georgia), Trey'Dez Green (TE, LSU), Jackson Harris (WR, LSU)

Note: I'd like to shout out the Dallas Cowboys for taking Florida RB Jadan Baugh one pick before me, saving the Giants from themselves. I fully believe that Baugh will be high on the Giants' draft board, and he'll be in heavy consideration for their first-round pick if he's on the board when they pick. I'm not sure John Harbaugh will be able to help himself, even if drafting a running back in the first never works out for the drafting team.

I've been consistent over the last year (plus) in viewing wide receiver as a significant need. The Giants only have two receivers under contract beyond this year — Malik Nabers and Malachi Fields. The Giants might prefer to run heavy personnel packages, but a good 11-personnel package is still important to any and every scheme in the NFL.

I chose Duff over Alabama's Ryan Coleman-Williams for three reasons. First, while Coleman-Williams is as much of an explosive threat as Nabers, he has struggled with consistency. Second, I want a second big-bodied outside receiver to play the X opposite Fields.

Secondly, Malik Nabers has been the best slot receiver in the NFL, and much better from the slot than out wide. The Giants need to build the kind of receiving corps that will allow him to play where he's so devastatingly effective.

And finally, Duff himself has the potential to be special in his own right. He has great size at 6-foot-6, 225 pounds (listed), and appears to have good explosiveness, long speed, and agility for his size. He excels at the catch point, with a catch rate over expected of 17.7% and a miniscule 1.3% drop rate.

Duff has an impressive 1,858 yards (105 catches, 17.6 per catch) and 16 touchdowns over the last two years, despite playing on a Rutgers team that's probably most charitably described as "unfortunate". It's exciting to think of what he could do in the same offense as Nabers, Fields, Isaiah Likely, and Jaxson Dart.

Also Considered: Reed Harris (WR, Arizona State), Ryan Coleman-Williams (WR, Alabama), Ty Benefield (S, LSU)

Back to the defensive side of the ball we go.

I was hunting for an offensive lineman or running back at this spot, but the board didn't fall my way. Instead, I picked up Alabama's versatile STAR to help take the Giants' defense to the next level.

Dennard Wilson has taken the Ravens 2.0 defensive model and remixed it with a healthy dose of "Ryan style" aggression. The "Ravens style" defense loves to use a versatile safety hybrid as a force player to dictate terms to the offense and adapt to different personnel packages and looks. The Giants don't really have that, but Hubbard brings an excellent blend of size (6-foot-2, 217 pounds) and athleticism (estimated 4.4 speed). He has plenty of experience in Alabama's sophisticated defense and playing against elite competition in the SEC, which should allow him to step in and contribute right away.

He brings great run defense, solid pass coverage, physicality, and ball-hawking ability. Hubbard is a tackling machine with 193 tackles to go with 9 interceptions and 10 passes defensed over the last 3 years.

Also considered: Vicari Swain (CB, South Carolina), LJ Martin (RB, BYU)
